UX, aesthetics, and distinctive product design

depth: smallScoping

Both halves: practical usability (heuristics, IA, accessibility, design systems) and the creative side of making products visually beautiful, unique, and distinct from the polished-but-generic norm.

Evidence: 39 subtopics · 110 starter resources. Passes: 1. Verified by Mike: not yet. Flags: 7 open questions, not reviewed.
Next level: A deep pass with 25+ cited sources moves this to medium.

DateResearch performedAgentsSearchesOutput
2026-09-03Scoping pass only: map subtopics and a learning path, no deep research13939 subtopics in 6 groups, 109 starter resources, 10-step learning path, 16 names, 6 open questions

A scoped map of what a technical PM who builds with Claude Code needs to learn to ship products that are both genuinely usable and visually distinctive. It covers the usability canon (heuristics, IA, research, accessibility, type, color, layout, motion, copy), the craft of standing out from 'SaaS sameness' and AI-slop defaults, deliberate taste-building habits, AI-assisted design tooling and its pitfalls, front-end polish craft, and PM-level design leadership.

Subtopics (39)
Usability 14
  • Usability heuristics and heuristic evaluation. Nielsen's 10 rules of thumb (visibility of system status, match to the real world, user control, consistency, error prevention, recognition over recall, etc.) and the practice of walking a product against them to find issues cheaply without users. The fastest shared vocabulary for spotting why something feels clunky, and a checklist you can run on your own Claude-built UI in 30 minutes.
    Search: Nielsen 10 usability heuristics, heuristic evaluation, severity rating usability
    Start with: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design · How to Conduct a Heuristic Evaluation · Laws of UX (book and site)
  • UX foundations and design psychology. The classic mental models: affordances, signifiers, feedback, mental models, Fitts's/Hick's laws, peak-end rule. Why doors and microwaves confuse people, and how that transfers to software. This is the 'why' layer under every heuristic; beginner-friendly books give you the vocabulary to talk to designers credibly.
    Search: Design of Everyday Things, Don't Make Me Think, cognitive load UX, affordance signifier
    Start with: The 14 Best Books to Learn UX Design in 2026 · Laws of UX: Using Psychology to Design Better Products & Services · Emotional Design (topic overview)
  • Information architecture. Structuring, organizing, and labeling content and navigation so people can find things: taxonomies, navigation schemes, card sorting, tree testing. Most 'this app feels confusing' complaints are IA problems, not visual ones; as a PM you own the structure more than the pixels.
    Search: information architecture, polar bear book, card sorting, tree testing, navigation design
    Start with: Information Architecture: For the Web and Beyond (4th ed., the 'polar bear book') · What is Information Architecture? · Card sorting
  • Interaction design and the 'invisible details'. How things behave: states, transitions, gestures, feedback, timing, and the subliminal details (hover intent, optimistic UI, drag physics) that make an interface feel like an extension of the hand. This is the layer where polished products earn their 'feel'; it is also where Claude-generated UI is most often flat and generic.
    Search: interaction design principles, invisible details of interaction design, design engineering, micro-interactions
    Start with: Invisible Details of Interaction Design · Devouring Details (interactive course, 23 chapters) · Learn UI: interfaces, explained interactively
  • Accessibility (a11y) and WCAG. Making products perceivable, operable, understandable, and robust for people with disabilities: contrast, keyboard navigation, screen readers, focus, reduced motion. WCAG 2.2 AA is the usual target. Accessibility constraints are also polish constraints (contrast, focus states, motion fallbacks), and they are increasingly a legal requirement.
    Search: WCAG 2.2 AA, A11Y Project checklist, accessible color contrast, prefers-reduced-motion, ARIA basics
    Start with: The A11Y Project Checklist · Practica11y: learn web accessibility by doing · WCAG 2.2: Where to Begin
  • UX research basics. Lightweight, decision-driving research: interviews, surveys, competitive analysis, and knowing how much research is 'just enough' before building. PMs already do discovery; this frames it in design terms and stops you from designing for yourself.
    Search: Just Enough Research, user interviews for product managers, generative vs evaluative research
    Start with: Just Enough Research (2024 edition) · Google UX Design Professional Certificate · Google UX Design vs IxDF Membership
  • Usability testing (do-it-yourself). Watching 3-5 people try to use your thing, one morning a month, with a script and a severity list. Krug's approach is deliberately cheap and unscientific on purpose. The single highest-ROI practice for a solo builder: it finds the problems heuristics miss and costs a few hours.
    Search: Rocket Surgery Made Easy, think-aloud usability test, 5 users usability testing, unmoderated testing tools
    Start with: Rocket Surgery Made Easy · Rocket Surgery Made Easy (Goodreads)
  • Design systems, atomic design, and design tokens. Reusable components (atoms > molecules > organisms) governed by tokens (color, spacing, type, motion values) so a product stays consistent as it grows. Radix, shadcn/ui, Material, and Tailwind are the common reference systems. Consistency is the cheapest form of polish, and a tokenized system is exactly what you need to feed Claude so it stops inventing new styles per screen.
    Search: atomic design Brad Frost, design tokens, shadcn ui theming, Radix primitives, design system governance
    Start with: Atomic Design (free online book) · Design Tokens + Atomic Design · Design Tokens Course
  • Typography fundamentals. Font choice, size, line length, leading, hierarchy, pairing, and the rules that make text readable and professional. Typography is roughly 90 percent of what a screen is. Changing the font is the single highest-leverage move against generic AI output, and good type is what makes 'editorial' looks work.
    Search: Butterick's Practical Typography, font pairing, type scale, typographic hierarchy, Typewolf
    Start with: Butterick's Practical Typography (free web book) · Practical Typography: Websites chapter · Refactoring UI (heavy on practical type and hierarchy)
  • Color for UI (palettes, contrast, OKLCH, APCA). Building palettes with perceptually uniform color spaces (OKLCH), checking contrast with WCAG and the newer APCA algorithm, and using a dominant color plus sharp accents rather than timid even distribution. Color is where AI slop is most visible (indigo-to-purple gradients); a deliberate, constrained palette is instantly distinctive.
    Search: OKLCH color palette, APCA contrast, UI color system, Harmonizer color palette, 60-30-10 color rule
    Start with: Exploring the OKLCH ecosystem and its tools · Generating accessible color palettes for design systems, inspired by APCA · Atmos contrast checker (WCAG 2 and APCA)
  • Layout, grids, and spacing systems. Grid systems from Swiss graphic design applied to screens; consistent spacing scales; whitespace as a design tool; visual hierarchy through alignment and proximity. Grid discipline plus generous whitespace is what curated galleries like Godly reward, and it is learnable from rules rather than talent.
    Search: grid systems in graphic design, 8pt spacing system, visual hierarchy, Swiss style web design, CSS grid layout patterns
    Start with: Grid Systems in Graphic Design · Grid Systems Explained for Design Beginners · 7 Rules for Creating Gorgeous UI (updated 2024)
  • Motion design principles. Purposeful UI animation: Disney's 12 principles mapped to interfaces (anticipation, staging, slow-in/slow-out), durations of roughly 200-600ms, and motion that explains state change rather than decorating it. Motion is the difference between 'a template' and 'a product with a personality', and it is also the easiest thing to overdo.
    Search: 12 principles of animation UI, Material Design motion, UI animation duration, meaningful motion
    Start with: UI Animation: How to Apply Disney's 12 Principles of Animation to UI Design · Disney's Motion Principles in Designing Interface Animations
  • Microcopy and UX writing. The words in the interface: labels, empty states, error messages, onboarding, buttons. Strategic UX writing treats copy as a design material with measurable outcomes. Copy is where voice and personality live, and it is the cheapest lever a PM can pull without touching layout.
    Search: microcopy, UX writing, empty state copy, error message design, content design
    Start with: Strategic Writing for UX · Interview with Kinneret Yifrah (author of Microcopy: The Complete Guide)
  • Practical UI design for developers (Refactoring UI school). Tactical, rule-based visual design for non-designers: start with features not layouts, establish hierarchy with weight and color not size, shadows from a light source, limit choices with systems. This is the fastest on-ramp for someone who ships code; it turns 'design' into a few dozen tactics you can apply immediately in Tailwind.
    Search: Refactoring UI, design for developers, Learn UI Design Erik Kennedy, UI design tactics
    Start with: Refactoring UI · 7 Rules for Creating Gorgeous UI (Part 1) · 7 Rules for Creating Gorgeous UI (video)
Distinctive visual design 8
  • The 'SaaS sameness' debate (Linear / Vercel / Stripe aesthetic). The dark-mode, subtle-gradient, Inter-and-bento-grid look popularized by Linear, Vercel, and Raycast, now copied by thousands of startups. Understanding it precisely is the prerequisite to deviating from it on purpose. This is the exact 'smooth polished sameness' Mike wants to escape; you have to be able to name its ingredients to subvert them.
    Search: Linear design trend, Vercel aesthetic blueprint grid, why SaaS websites look the same, techno-futurist vs editorial SaaS design
    Start with: Linear design: The SaaS design trend that's boring and bettering UI · The Linear, Vercel, and Raycast Aesthetic: What It Actually Is (and How to Get It) · Why SaaS Websites Look The Same & How To Stand Out
  • Art direction and visual identity for the web. Starting from the story you want to tell and choosing type, color, imagery, layout, and motion to serve it, the way magazines and ad campaigns have for a century. The 'when and why' of distinctive design, not just the 'how'. Art direction is the missing discipline between 'usable' and 'memorable'; it is the frame that makes every other visual decision coherent.
    Search: art direction for the web, visual identity system, brand expression in product UI, concept-driven web design
    Start with: Art Direction for the Web · Meet 'Art Direction For The Web' (release article with excerpts)
  • Editorial and print design influence. Borrowing from magazines and books: strong typographic hierarchy, serif faces, asymmetric layouts, cream backgrounds, pull quotes, considered whitespace. The 'editorial' camp is currently the main counterweight to the techno-futurist SaaS look. Editorial is the most accessible 'stand out' direction for a text-heavy product and it plays well with the typography skills above.
    Search: editorial web design, magazine-style layout web, typographic website, Frank Chimero Shape of Design
    Start with: The Shape of Design (free online) · Frank Chimero on the Shape of Design and the Harmonics of Influence · Godly: curated, typographically considered web design
  • Brutalism, neo-brutalism, maximalism, and anti-design. Counter-trends that reject polish: raw HTML aesthetics, hard shadows and thick borders (neo-brutalism), clashing colors and dense layouts (maximalism), deliberate rule-breaking (anti-design). Neo-brutalism in particular is 'highly styled to look simple'. These are the loudest ways to not look like everyone else; worth knowing even if you only borrow one element (hard shadows, a single loud accent).
    Search: neubrutalism web design, brutalist websites, anti-design trend, maximalist web design, brutalist.website
    Start with: Neubrutalism: The Definitive Guide · Brutalist website design: a guide with 11 inspiring examples · Anti-Design Trends 2025: How to Make It Work?
  • Generative and procedural visuals. Using code (p5.js, canvas, WebGL shaders, noise, flow fields) to produce visuals that are unique per user or per load: backgrounds, avatars, OG images, hero art. The Tyler Hobbs / Fidenza lineage applied to product surfaces. You already write code; generative art is the one distinctive-visual skill where that is an unfair advantage over designers, and nobody's Tailwind template has it.
    Search: p5.js generative art beginner, creative coding, Tyler Hobbs essays, GLSL shaders web background, procedural avatars
    Start with: Tyler Hobbs (essays on generative art, color arrangement, flow fields) · Beginner's guide to learning p5.js for generative art · Generative art workshop (p5.js intro)
  • Illustration strategy (and escaping Corporate Memphis). Deciding whether and how to use illustration: the flat, gangly-limbed 'Corporate Memphis' style became the default and now reads as generic; alternatives include custom line work, collage, 3D, photography, and generative marks. Illustration is a big visual-identity lever that is easy to get generically wrong; knowing the critique saves you from the stock-illustration look.
    Search: Corporate Memphis, custom illustration product design, illustration styles that aren't Corporate Memphis, illustration system
    Start with: Corporate Memphis · What the Think Pieces About Corporate Memphis Tell Us About the State of Illustration · 4 Website Illustration Styles That Aren't Corporate Memphis
  • Delight, whimsy, and sound. Unexpected small pleasures: playful animations, easter eggs, tactile feedback, and short UI sounds in direct response to user action. Norman's visceral/behavioral/reflective levels explain why these create attachment. Delight is what people screenshot and share; used sparingly it is the cheapest memorability you can add to a side project.
    Search: whimsical animations, delight in UX, UI sound design, use-sound React, Emotional Design Don Norman
    Start with: Whimsical Animations (course) · A Million Little Secrets (whimsical animations essay) · UI Sounds
  • Personality, voice, and tone. Giving a product a recognizable character through copy, mascots, humor, and consistent tone (Mailchimp's Freddie and its 'folksy but competent' voice is the canonical example). Personality is a differentiator that does not require illustration skill, and it aligns with Mike's own preference for a casual, humorous register.
    Search: brand voice and tone guide, Mailchimp content style guide, personality in design, product voice
    Start with: Shaping Brand Personality · Personality in Design · Emotional Design
Building taste 4
  • Taste as a learnable skill. The argument (Paul Graham, Chimero, Kleon) that taste is cultivated pattern recognition, not preference: you get better, your old taste was worse, and great work is 'exacting taste plus the ability to gratify it'. Reframes 'I'm not a designer' as 'I haven't put in the reps yet', which is the mindset this whole domain runs on.
    Search: Taste for Makers, how to develop design taste, taste vs skill design, Steal Like an Artist
    Start with: Taste for Makers · Steal Like an Artist · Austin Kleon on building a sustainable creative practice (podcast)
  • Curation habits and inspiration galleries. Deliberate daily exposure to good work, with different galleries for different questions: Godly and Awwwards for craft and ambition, Land-book for landing pages, Mobbin for real product flows, Are.na for personal collecting. Ten minutes on Godly resets your sense of what good looks like; one Mobbin flow a week teaches hierarchy. Cheap, compounding habits.
    Search: godly.website, Mobbin, Land-book, Awwwards, Are.na channels, SiteInspire
    Start with: Mobbin: UI and UX design inspiration for mobile and web apps · 15 Design Inspiration Sites 2026: Godly, Mobbin, Typewolf · Are.na
  • Critique practice and the Dribbble trap. Learning to critique (analyze design choices against objectives, not assert opinions) and to recognize 'Dribbblisation': shots optimized for likes that fail basic usability or ignore the other 197 screens. Critique is how taste gets articulated; the Dribbble critique is a useful immune system against pretty-but-hollow references.
    Search: design critique framework, Discussing Design, dribbblisation of design, how to critique UI
    Start with: Discussing Design: Improving Communication and Collaboration through Critique · The Dribbblisation of Design · Dribbble Is Not For Product Designers (but that's okay)
  • Learning by redesign (video-led). Watching designers narrate full redesigns (Juxtopposed's YouTube redesign is the famous one) and doing your own unsolicited redesigns to practice decisions end to end. Narrated redesigns show the reasoning behind choices, which books rarely do; redesigning a product you use is the best homework.
    Search: Juxtopposed, UI redesign walkthrough, unsolicited redesign, DesignCourse, Flux Academy, Mizko
    Start with: Juxtopposed (YouTube channel) · I Redesigned the ENTIRE YouTube UI from Scratch · Top YouTube Channels to Learn UX/UI Design (Beginner to Advanced)
Design with AI 4
  • AI slop aesthetics and how to avoid them. The recognizable fingerprint of default LLM output: Inter font, indigo-to-purple gradients, three rounded cards in a row, glassmorphism glow, bounce-on-hover. It happens because models return the statistical median of the training set. Mike builds with Claude Code; knowing the tells lets him spot and prompt away sameness before it ships.
    Search: AI slop design, AI design tells, purple gradient AI website, generic Tailwind look, spot the slop
    Start with: Slop (a field guide to AI design tells) · Spot the Slop: A UI Designer's Guide to Fixing AI Defaults · Why Your AI Keeps Building the Same Purple Gradient Website
  • Anthropic frontend-design skill and DESIGN.md-style constraints. Anthropic's frontend-design plugin makes Claude declare purpose, tone, constraints, and differentiation before coding and bans overused fonts. DESIGN.md is the community pattern of a repo-root file encoding tokens, type, spacing, and component rules so agents stay on-brand. This is the direct bridge between the design theory above and Mike's daily Claude Code workflow: encode taste as constraints the agent must obey.
    Search: Claude Code frontend-design plugin, frontend-design SKILL.md, DESIGN.md AI coding agents, design system for AI coding, awesome-claude-design
    Start with: Frontend Design Plugin · frontend-design SKILL.md (source) · awesome-claude-design: 68 DESIGN.md design system inspirations
  • AI design and prompt-to-UI tools (Figma Make, v0, Lovable, Claude Design). The current landscape: Figma Make for prototypes inside Figma, v0 for Next.js front ends, Lovable for full-stack MVPs, and Claude Design as Anthropic's canvas entry. Each has a house style you must actively override. Choosing the right tool for exploration vs. shipping, and knowing each tool's default aesthetic, saves a lot of homogeneous output.
    Search: Figma Make vs Lovable vs v0, Claude Design, vibe coding tools comparison, prompt to UI
    Start with: v0 vs Lovable vs Bolt vs Replit vs Figma Make: A Real-Design Comparison · Claude Design vs Figma vs Lovable vs v0 (2026) · Figma Make vs Lovable vs v0: Hands-On Comparison
  • AI image tools for moodboards and art direction. Using Midjourney (and its moodboard / style-reference feature) to explore visual directions, textures, palettes, and illustration styles quickly before committing to production assets. A fast way to develop and communicate an art direction without illustration skill; the output is a reference, not the final asset.
    Search: Midjourney moodboard, Midjourney style reference sref, AI moodboard art direction, Midjourney for product designers
    Start with: Art Direction: Moodboards (using Midjourney v6) · Your Midjourney Moodboards Aren't Working Hard Enough · Storytelling through Midjourney for Product Design
Implementation craft 5
  • Web animation craft: easing, springs, and timing. The implementation side of motion: custom easing curves, spring physics, transform/opacity for performance, enter/exit choreography, reduced-motion fallbacks, and CSS vs. Motion for React. Emil Kowalski (Linear, ex-Vercel) literally teaches the feel of the products Mike admires; this is how you get it without copying their look.
    Search: animations.dev, easing curves UI, spring animation web, Motion for React, CSS transitions best practices
    Start with: Animations on the Web · Building an animation course · web-animation-design agent skill (open-agents)
  • Modern CSS fundamentals for polish. Layout modes (flow, flexbox, grid, positioning), stacking contexts, custom properties, container queries, and the mental models that make CSS predictable instead of magical. Polish dies in the details Claude gets 90 percent right; understanding CSS lets you fix the last 10 percent instead of re-prompting.
    Search: CSS for JavaScript Developers, Kevin Powell CSS, modern CSS layout, container queries, CSS custom properties theming
    Start with: CSS for JavaScript Developers · Josh W. Comeau's blog and courses · Kevin Powell on teaching front-end and making sense of CSS
  • Fluid typography and spacing (Utopia). Type and space scales that interpolate smoothly between viewport sizes using CSS clamp(), replacing breakpoint-by-breakpoint font sizes with a single system. Fluid scales make a product look intentional on every screen size, and Utopia's calculators give you the CSS in one click.
    Search: Utopia fluid type scale, CSS clamp typography, fluid space scale, responsive typography
    Start with: Fluid type scale calculator · Meet Utopia: Designing and Building with Fluid Type and Space Scales · Fluid vs. responsive typography with CSS clamp
  • Dark mode and theming. Token-based theming with parity between light and dark, avoiding pure-white-on-pure-black halation, elevation via lightness not shadow, setting the theme early to avoid flashes, and auditing performance after enabling. Dark mode is table stakes for developer-facing products and a common place where AI-generated UI breaks contrast or looks muddy.
    Search: dark mode design best practices, dark mode color tokens, prefers-color-scheme, halation dark mode
    Start with: Best Practices for Dark Mode in Web Design 2026 · Dark Mode Web Design: Tokens, Contrast, Accessibility
  • Performance as UX (Core Web Vitals, perceived speed). LCP, CLS, and interaction latency as user-experience metrics; skeleton states, optimistic UI, and visual hierarchy that makes pages feel faster than they are. Speed is the most under-rated component of 'polish'; a beautiful page that shifts layout or lags on click feels cheap.
    Search: Core Web Vitals UX, perceived performance, cumulative layout shift design, optimistic UI, skeleton screens
    Start with: How Google Core Web Vitals Can Improve UX Performance · The Best UX/UI Design Tips for Good Core Web Vitals
Design leadership 4
  • Giving design feedback as a PM. Critiquing against the problem and user outcome rather than personal taste ('make it feel premium' is not feedback), asking questions instead of prescribing UI, and increasing feedback frequency to lower stakes. Directly transfers to Mike's day job and to prompting Claude: specific, problem-anchored feedback gets better results from designers and models alike.
    Search: how product managers give design feedback, design critique for PMs, PM designer collaboration, design review meeting
    Start with: How Product Managers Should Give Product Designers Feedback · How to Give Design Feedback as a PM: A Guide for 2026 · Design critique as a PM
  • Design QA and shipping the intended design. The review pass between 'designed' and 'shipped': checking spacing, states, copy, responsive behavior, and motion against the intent so hidden disagreements do not reach QA or users. For a solo builder, design QA is the last-mile discipline that separates 'Claude made a thing' from 'this is a finished product'.
    Search: design QA checklist, design handoff review, UI polish checklist, visual regression
    Start with: Design Review Feedback Guide (PM prompts) · Comprehensive Accessibility Checklist for Web Projects
  • Measuring UX (HEART, goals-signals-metrics). Google's HEART framework (Happiness, Engagement, Adoption, Retention, Task success) and the Goals > Signals > Metrics process for turning design quality into numbers a team can track. Lets a PM argue for polish and distinctiveness with evidence, and pick which two HEART categories matter for a given launch.
    Search: HEART framework, UX metrics, goals signals metrics, task success rate, SUS score
    Start with: Measuring the User Experience on a Large Scale (original paper) · Google's HEART Framework for Measuring UX · How to Set UX Metrics with the Google HEART Framework
  • Design engineering as a discipline (and AI's craft crisis). The hybrid role (Vercel, Linear, Raycast) where design and code are one act, and the current debate that AI exposes a craft gap: it is easy to generate, hard to judge. Mike is effectively a one-person design-engineering team with Claude as the engineer; this framing tells him which skills to hire himself for: judgment and taste.
    Search: design engineer role, design engineering, AI craft crisis design, taste vs execution AI
    Start with: Why AI is exposing design's craft crisis · Rauno Freiberg interview · Raycast Community Stories: Rauno Freiberg, Design Engineer
Suggested learning path (10 steps)
  1. Read Refactoring UI cover to cover and Erik Kennedy's '7 Rules for Creating Gorgeous UI', then apply five tactics to one screen of a current side project. Fastest possible jump from 'developer output' to 'looks designed'; gives vocabulary (hierarchy, contrast, spacing) everything else builds on.
  2. Learn Nielsen's 10 heuristics and run a one-hour heuristic evaluation on your own product; log issues with severity. Usability is the foundation; distinctive-but-unusable is just art. This also arms you for design reviews at work.
  3. Start a daily 10-minute curation habit: Godly or Awwwards for craft, one Mobbin flow per week for hierarchy, and save what you like to an Are.na channel with a one-line note on why. Taste is pattern recognition built by exposure plus articulation. The 'why' note is the part most people skip and the part that actually trains you.
  4. Read the 'AI slop' field guides (Impeccable 'Slop', Mania 'Spot the Slop'), then install Anthropic's frontend-design plugin and write a DESIGN.md for your project with one non-default font pairing, a capped palette in OKLCH, and a spacing scale. This converts steps 1-3 into constraints Claude Code must obey, so every new screen inherits your taste instead of the training-set median.
  5. Work through Butterick's Practical Typography (free) and set up a Utopia fluid type and space scale in your CSS. Typography is the highest-leverage distinctiveness move and the backbone of the editorial look; fluid scales make it hold up on every screen.
  6. Pick one 'stand out' direction to study deliberately for a month: editorial (Chimero, Godly), neo-brutalist (neubrutalism.com), or generative (p5.js + Tyler Hobbs essays). Build one small piece in that direction. Distinctiveness comes from committing to a point of view, not sampling every trend. Generative is the one where coding skill is an unfair advantage.
  7. Take Emil Kowalski's Animations on the Web (or Josh Comeau's Whimsical Animations) and add three purposeful micro-interactions plus reduced-motion fallbacks to your product. Motion is where 'template' becomes 'product'. Learning easing and timing from a Linear design engineer gets the feel without copying the look.
  8. Run a Krug-style usability test: 3 people, one morning, a script, fix the top 3 issues that week. Then do an accessibility pass with the A11Y Project checklist. Validates that your new visual direction did not break usability, and makes 'polished' mean something for people who are not you.
  9. Read Discussing Design and the Reforge PM feedback piece; start giving problem-anchored critique (to designers at work and to Claude in prompts). Articulating why something works or does not is the last step of taste, and it makes you a better design partner and a better prompter.
  10. Instrument one product with two HEART metrics (usually Adoption + Task Success) and revisit them after your next visual iteration. Closes the loop: distinctive design should earn its keep, and numbers let you defend polish to stakeholders.

Names to know: Steve Schoger and Adam Wathan, Erik Kennedy (Learn UI Design), Nielsen Norman Group (Jakob Nielsen, Don Norman), Steve Krug, Rauno Freiberg, Emil Kowalski, Josh W. Comeau, Brad Frost, Matthew Butterick, Frank Chimero, Andy Clarke, Tyler Hobbs, Juxtopposed, Austin Kleon, Erika Hall, Kevin Powell

Open questions (7)
  • Which single 'stand out' direction (editorial, neo-brutalist, generative) fits Mike's actual products and audience? Worth a short moodboard exercise before committing a month to one.
  • How far can DESIGN.md plus the frontend-design skill actually push Claude Code away from the median look, and where does it still need human art direction? Deserves a hands-on experiment with before/after screenshots.
  • Claude Design (Anthropic's canvas tool) is new; its house aesthetic and how it interacts with a custom DESIGN.md are not yet well documented.
  • Is a paid structured course (Google UX Certificate, IxDF, Learn UI Design) worth it for a PM who already does discovery, or is the book-plus-habit path sufficient? Depends on how much accountability Mike wants.
  • Sound design in product UI has thin literature; use-sound and ale.today are starting points but a deeper resource search is needed if Mike wants to pursue it.
  • Every Layout (Heydon Pickering and Andy Bell) is a well-regarded CSS layout resource that did not surface in this pass; verify and add under Implementation craft.
  • Design QA as a solo builder: no canonical checklist surfaced; assembling one from the a11y checklist, heuristics, and a motion/responsive pass may be a worthwhile small deliverable.

AI product management

depth: smallScoping

The discipline of being an AI PM, as distinct from building with AI tools: evals, model quality and safety, AI product metrics and economics, platform and model strategy, data, designing for uncertainty, shipping and operating, governance, interview vocabulary.

Evidence: 45 subtopics · 109 starter resources. Passes: 1. Verified by Mike: not yet. Flags: 6 open questions, not reviewed.
Next level: A deep pass with 25+ cited sources moves this to medium.

DateResearch performedAgentsSearchesOutput
2026-09-03Scoping pass only: map subtopics, learning path, interview questions14345 subtopics in 9 groups, 111 starter resources, 12-step learning path, 18 names, 14 interview questions, 4 open questions

The discipline of defining, shipping, and operating products whose core behavior comes from probabilistic models (LLMs, agents) rather than deterministic code. It differs from 'building with AI tools' in that the PM owns quality definitions (evals), behavioral specs (prompts, guardrails), unit economics (tokens, inference COGS), and trust/safety/governance outcomes, and is expected to speak that vocabulary fluently in interviews. This map is scoped, not deep: it names the subtopics, gives search keywords, and points at starter resources found in search results.

Subtopics (45)
Evals 8
  • Evals: fundamentals and vocabulary. Systematic measurement of whether an LLM feature does what you intended. Core vocabulary: offline evals (run against a fixed dataset before ship) vs online evals (scored on live traffic), task vs trial vs transcript/trace, graders, pass rates, 'vibe checks' as the anti-pattern. This is the exact word he got caught on; it is now the single most-asked AI PM interview concept.
    Search: LLM evals for product managers, offline vs online evaluation LLM, eval-driven development, beyond vibe checks evals
    Start with: Beyond vibe checks: A PM's complete guide to evals · Demystifying evals for AI agents · AI Evals For Engineers & PMs
  • Error analysis and open coding ('look at your data'). The Husain/Shankar workflow: read ~100 real traces, write free-form notes on failures (open coding), cluster them into a failure taxonomy (axial coding), then build targeted evals for the top failure modes. Evals come after error analysis, not before. It is the single most-cited 'what PMs actually do' answer and the process the Maven course is built around.
    Search: error analysis LLM open coding axial coding, failure taxonomy LLM traces, Hamel Husain error analysis
    Start with: Building eval systems that improve your AI product (Hamel & Shreya) · An LLM-as-Judge Won't Save The Product - Fixing Your Process Will · How to Do AI Evals Step-by-Step with Real Production Data
  • Golden datasets and eval set construction. Curated sets of inputs (and often expected outputs or rubrics) representing real usage, edge cases, and past production failures. Start small (20-50), grow from production, keep a held-out set, version it. Interviewers ask 'where would your test cases come from?' -- the answer is production traces plus synthetic edge cases, not made-up examples.
    Search: golden dataset LLM evaluation, eval dataset curation synthetic data, test set from production traces
    Start with: Getting started with datasets · Evaluation best practices
  • LLM-as-judge and judge calibration. Using a model to grade another model's outputs at scale. Key ideas: binary pass/fail beats 1-5 scales, judges must be validated against human labels (agreement rate, TPR/TNR), position bias, and 'who validates the validators'. Second most-asked eval term; knowing its failure modes signals real depth versus buzzword familiarity.
    Search: LLM-as-a-judge, judge alignment human labels, EvalGen who validates the validators, pairwise vs absolute grading
    Start with: Using LLM-as-a-Judge For Evaluation: A Complete Guide · Evaluating the Effectiveness of LLM-Evaluators (aka LLM-as-Judge) · Who Validates the Validators? (EvalGen)
  • Rubric design and grader types. Choosing how each criterion is scored: code-based assertions (format, length, banned strings), model-based graders with written rubrics, and human review. Rubrics should be specific, binary where possible, and tied to a named failure mode. PMs are expected to write the rubric; 'define good before you build' is a stock interview phrase.
    Search: eval rubric design LLM, code-based vs model-based graders, assertion-based evals, DeepEval metrics
    Start with: LLM-as-a-Judge in 2026: Top evaluation techniques and best practices · LLM-as-a-judge: a complete guide
  • Regression suites, eval gates, and eval-driven development. Running the eval suite on every prompt/model/retrieval change (CI for AI) and blocking ships that regress. Chip Huyen calls the broader loop 'evaluation-driven development'. Maps directly to his engineering instincts (tests, CI) so it is the fastest bridge from 'builder' to 'AI PM' language.
    Search: evaluation-driven development, evals as CI regression suite LLM, continuous evaluation prompt changes
    Start with: AI Engineering (book) - chapter summaries incl. Evaluate AI Systems · How evals drive the next chapter in AI for businesses · Working with evals
  • Agent evals: trajectory vs outcome. Evaluating multi-step, tool-using systems: outcome grading (did the end state match?) vs trajectory grading (were the right tools called in a sane order?), multiple trials per task because of stochasticity, and avoiding agents that 'win' via unintended shortcuts. A 'core AI services' role will almost certainly involve agents; this is where his Claude Code hands-on experience becomes a story he can tell in eval vocabulary.
    Search: agent evaluation trajectory outcome, tool-call correctness metric, pass@k agent evals, multi-turn conversation evals
    Start with: Demystifying evals for AI agents · Agent Evaluation: A Detailed Guide · Evaluate agent workflows
  • Eval and observability tooling landscape. The platforms teams use to store datasets, run graders, trace production calls, and compare experiments: Braintrust (eval-first), LangSmith (LangChain ecosystem), Langfuse (open-source/self-host, acquired by ClickHouse 2026), Arize Phoenix (OTel-native, strong metrics library), plus open frameworks like openai/evals, RAGAS, DeepEval, promptfoo. Name-dropping the right tool for the right team shape is a cheap credibility signal; he can also just try one this week.
    Search: Braintrust vs LangSmith vs Langfuse vs Arize, LLM observability platform comparison 2026, promptfoo, openai evals github
    Start with: Top LLM Observability and Evaluation Platforms in 2026 compared · Comparing LLM Evaluation Platforms · openai/evals framework and benchmark registry
Model quality & safety 6
  • Hallucination and grounding. Confident-but-wrong outputs and the mitigations: grounding in retrieved sources, citation requirements, 'say I don't know' instructions, quote-extraction-first for long docs, verification passes, and measuring faithfulness. 'Users say the model is confident but wrong -- what do you do?' is a stock interview prompt.
    Search: reduce hallucinations LLM product, grounding citations faithfulness, hallucination detection mitigation
    Start with: Reduce hallucinations · Avoiding Hallucinations (interactive prompt engineering tutorial)
  • RAG quality and retrieval evaluation. Separating retrieval failures from generation failures. Standard metrics: context precision/recall (did we retrieve the right chunks?), faithfulness (is the answer grounded in them?), answer relevance. Chunking, embeddings, reranking, and index freshness are the levers. Most enterprise AI features are RAG; being able to say 'is this a retrieval or a generation problem?' is the diagnostic move interviewers look for.
    Search: RAG evaluation faithfulness context precision, RAGAS metrics, retrieval vs generation failure diagnosis, chunking reranking
    Start with: RAGAS: Automated Evaluation of Retrieval Augmented Generation · Evaluating RAG with RAGAs · RAG evaluation: Metrics, methodologies, best practices
  • Prompt and system-prompt ownership. Treating the system prompt as the behavioral spec the PM owns (persona, scope, refusal rules, output format, tool instructions), with the engineering discipline of versioning and eval-gating every change. He already writes excellent prompts for Claude Code; reframing that as 'I own the behavioral contract' is an interview asset.
    Search: system prompt design product manager, prompt as specification, Anthropic prompt engineering guide
    Start with: Prompt engineering overview · Prompt library and best practices
  • Guardrails, prompt injection, and LLM security. Input/output filtering, classifier layers, least-privilege tool scopes, and human approval for destructive actions, framed by the OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications (prompt injection, system prompt leakage, insecure output handling, vector/embedding weaknesses). Red teaming is the adversarial testing practice that finds these. Agentic products with tool access are the current security hot zone; a platform PM is expected to have a defense-in-depth story.
    Search: OWASP Top 10 LLM applications, prompt injection prevention, LLM guardrails defense in depth, AI red teaming
    Start with: LLM Prompt Injection Prevention Cheat Sheet · LLMSecurityGuide (OWASP GenAI Top-10, red-teaming tools, guardrails) · Constitutional Classifiers: Defending against universal jailbreaks
  • Refusals, safety policy, and responsible AI. How labs define acceptable behavior (usage policies, Anthropic's constitution and Responsible Scaling Policy, OpenAI's model spec), the over-refusal vs under-refusal tradeoff, and how product teams tune it with prompts, classifiers, and escalation paths. Responsible AI now shows up in every interview round as a product-failure question rather than a compliance footnote.
    Search: over-refusal vs harmful compliance, Anthropic Responsible Scaling Policy, model spec usage policy, responsible AI product manager
    Start with: Anthropic's Responsible Scaling Policy · Anthropic's Transparency Hub
  • Model cards and system cards. Standardized disclosure documents: model cards (Google, 2019) describe a model's intended use and limits; system cards (OpenAI from GPT-4, then Anthropic) document safety testing, red-team results, and mitigations for a deployed system. Reading two system cards is the fastest way to absorb how frontier labs talk about risk, evals, and capabilities.
    Search: model card vs system card, Claude system card, GPT system card safety evaluations
    Start with: System Cards Go Hard · 5 things to know about AI model cards
Metrics & economics 5
  • AI product quality metrics (task success, acceptance, containment). Outcome metrics for AI features: task success / resolution rate, acceptance rate (suggestions kept, e.g. merged non-reverted PRs), containment vs deflection (containment requires a positive resolution signal; deflection counts anyone who didn't escalate, including those who gave up), thumbs up/down rates, regeneration and edit rates, CSAT, first-contact resolution. 'Define a north star for this AI feature' appears in almost every loop, and the containment-vs-deflection distinction is a great trap to avoid.
    Search: AI product metrics task success rate, containment rate vs deflection rate, acceptance rate copilot metrics, thumbs up down feedback rate
    Start with: Chatbot Analytics: 9 Metrics That Predict Failure · Evaluation Metrics for AI Products That Drive Trust · AI Evaluation Metrics 2026
  • Operational metrics: latency, cost per task, tokens. The non-quality dials: time-to-first-token and total latency (and why streaming matters), tokens per request, cost per successful task, cache hit rates, error/timeout rates, and how model choice moves each. Interviewers probe whether he can connect model behavior to a P&L line and a latency SLA.
    Search: cost per task LLM metric, time to first token latency streaming, LLM cost tracking per feature
    Start with: How to measure AI productivity: From LLM token costs to business value · LLM Monitoring Best Practices: Complete Guide for 2026
  • Token economics, inference COGS, and gross margin. Why AI products run 50-60% gross margins vs 80-90% for SaaS: variable inference cost per use, heavy-user tail risk (the Copilot losing-money-per-seat story), and the levers (caching, smaller/routed models, context trimming, batching). Unit economics is where a senior PM differentiates from a junior one; the vocabulary here is new even to experienced PMs.
    Search: AI gross margin vs SaaS inference COGS, LLMflation, unit economics AI product tokens, inference cost optimization caching
    Start with: The AI COGS Problem: SaaS Gross Margin Compression 2026 · Why AI Gross Margins Are So Much Lower Than SaaS · AI Unit Economics: Pricing & Margins for AI Services
  • AI pricing models: seat vs usage vs outcome. Per-seat (familiar, fragile under variable cost), usage/credits/tokens (aligned to cost, volatile revenue), outcome-based (Intercom Fin's per-resolution pricing), and the dominant hybrid: seats with usage bundled. a16z frames it as 'software becoming labor'. Pricing is a classic PM case question and AI has genuinely changed the answer.
    Search: outcome-based pricing AI agents, per seat vs usage based pricing AI, Intercom Fin per resolution pricing, hybrid pricing credits
    Start with: AI Is Driving A Shift Towards Outcome-Based Pricing · AI Pricing Models Compared: Seats, Tokens, Outcomes · AI Software Pricing: Models, Metrics, and a Practical Framework
  • Feedback signals and instrumentation. Designing explicit signals (thumbs, ratings, 'report') and implicit ones (copy, edit, regenerate, abandon, escalate) so production usage feeds evals and datasets. Explicit thumbs are sparse and biased; implicit signals scale. Connects metrics to the data flywheel; interviewers like PMs who know thumbs-up rates are a weak signal.
    Search: implicit feedback signals LLM product, feedback instrumentation AI features, thumbs up down bias sparse feedback
    Start with: People + AI Guidebook - chapters incl. Feedback + Control · Data Flywheels for LLM Applications: Closing the Loop Between Production and Improvement
Platform & model strategy 7
  • Build vs buy and model selection. Choosing among frontier closed APIs, open-weight models (Llama, Mistral, etc.), and fine-tuned small models, weighing capability gap, per-token cost (open models roughly 5-10x cheaper), data residency, latency, and vendor lock-in. Most 2026 deployments are hybrid. A 'core AI services' PM literally owns this decision for the company.
    Search: open source vs closed LLM enterprise decision framework, model selection criteria product, vendor lock-in LLM switching costs, open-weight models cost
    Start with: Open Source vs Closed Source LLMs - Decision Framework (2026) · Open Source vs Closed LLMs: Technical Comparison 2026
  • Prompting vs RAG vs fine-tuning. The three customization levers and the standard hierarchy: start with prompting (hours), add RAG when the model needs knowledge it lacks or that changes (days-weeks), fine-tune only for a proven gap in style/format/latency/cost (months, plus ongoing upkeep). Named explicitly in 2026 interview guides as required 'working fluency'.
    Search: RAG vs fine-tuning vs prompt engineering, when to fine-tune LLM, customization hierarchy LLM
    Start with: RAG vs. Fine-tuning vs. Prompt Engineering: The Complete Guide · Fine-Tuning, RAG, or Prompt Engineering? LLM Decision Guide
  • Technical fluency: tokens, context windows, caching. How tokenization, context length, prompt caching, and output length drive both cost and latency; context rot in long contexts; why 'just put everything in the window' is not free. He knows this intuitively from daily Claude Code use; the gap is stating it crisply in cost/latency terms.
    Search: context window cost latency tokens, prompt caching, long context degradation, tokens explained product managers
    Start with: AI Engineering: Building Applications with Foundation Models · Prompt engineering overview
  • Agents vs workflows. Anthropic's distinction: workflows orchestrate LLM calls through predefined code paths (predictable, cheaper); agents let the model direct its own tool use (flexible, costlier, harder to eval). Patterns: prompt chaining, routing, parallelization, orchestrator-workers, evaluator-optimizer. Start simple. This is the vocabulary hiring managers use to talk about system design for AI features; he should be able to sketch the patterns on a whiteboard.
    Search: Anthropic building effective agents, workflows vs agents, orchestrator workers evaluator optimizer pattern, agentic design patterns
    Start with: Building Effective AI Agents · When Not to Build AI Agents: Anthropic's Workflow-vs-Agent Playbook
  • Tool use and MCP (Model Context Protocol). Function calling lets models invoke your APIs; MCP is the open standard ('USB-C for AI') so a tool is implemented once and any MCP client can use it. Product implications: your product becomes a set of permissioned tools an agent can call; auth, scopes, and audit become product surface. He uses MCP servers daily; he needs the product-strategy framing (distribution, integration cost, security) rather than the plumbing.
    Search: Model Context Protocol product managers, MCP server product strategy, function calling tool use LLM, MCP security permissions
    Start with: What is MCP (Model Context Protocol)? The 2026 Guide for SaaS PMs · What is Model Context Protocol (MCP)? A guide · What is Model Context Protocol (MCP)? A Guide for Product Managers
  • Model gateways, routing, and internal AI platforms ('core AI services'). The internal platform layer between product teams and model providers: a gateway (LiteLLM, Portkey, Kong AI Gateway) that unifies APIs, routes by cost/latency/capability, handles fallbacks, rate limits, budgets, key management, logging, caching, and guardrails; plus shared eval/observability, prompt registry, and a model catalog with approved vendors. This is the literal job description of a 'core AI services' PM; he should be able to name the components and the customers (internal product teams).
    Search: LLM gateway internal platform team, LiteLLM vs Portkey, model routing fallbacks cost tracking, AI platform PM internal developer platform, model catalog governance
    Start with: LLM Gateway Comparison 2026: Enterprise Buyer's Guide · LiteLLM - Open-Source AI Gateway & LLM Proxy · Portkey vs LiteLLM: Routing, Fallbacks, Cost Tracking, and Control
  • Model lifecycle: deprecations and migrations. Providers deprecate and retire models on ~60-day-plus notice; every migration is a behavior change that must be re-evaled. Platform PMs own the deprecation calendar, migration tooling, and communication to internal teams. A recurring operational headache for platform teams that most candidates never mention; it also shows why regression evals exist.
    Search: model deprecation policy migration, Claude model deprecations, AI model lifecycle calendar, migrating prompts across model generations
    Start with: Model deprecations · AI Model Deprecation and Lifecycle Calendar - Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, Amazon Bedrock
Data 4
  • Data strategy and data flywheels. Designing the product so usage generates proprietary data (traces, corrections, preferences) that improves the model or retrieval, which attracts more usage. Distinguish real flywheels from 'we log everything' and know when the moat is data vs distribution vs workflow. Standard strategy-round question: 'where is the moat if everyone has the same model?'
    Search: data flywheel AI product, data moat LLM applications, proprietary data advantage foundation models
    Start with: The data flywheel effect in AI model improvement · Data Flywheel (glossary)
  • Labeling and human feedback at the product level (RLHF/RLAIF lite). Turning human judgments into training or eval signal: annotation guidelines, inter-annotator agreement, pairwise preferences, expert-in-the-loop review queues, and using AI feedback (RLAIF / constitutional methods) to scale. At product level this usually means feeding curated corrections into evals, few-shot examples, retrieval, or fine-tunes. Lets him speak to how labs align models (RLHF, constitutional AI) without pretending to be a research scientist.
    Search: RLHF explained product, RLAIF constitutional AI, annotation guidelines inter-annotator agreement, human-in-the-loop labeling pipeline
    Start with: Agent-in-the-Loop: A Data Flywheel for Continuous Improvement in LLM-based Customer Support · The Data Flywheel: Why AI Products Live or Die by User Feedback
  • Privacy, PII, data residency, and zero data retention. What happens to prompts and outputs: provider training opt-outs, zero-data-retention (ZDR) agreements, PII redaction before model calls, regional hosting (EU/US), and subprocessor lists. Enterprise AI deals die on these questions; a platform PM is the one answering them.
    Search: zero data retention LLM providers, PII redaction before LLM, data residency AI enterprise, training opt-out enterprise agreement
    Start with: Zero Data Retention MCP Servers: Building SOC 2 & GDPR Compliant AI Agents · LLM vendor security/compliance checklist for procurement
  • Enterprise procurement and security review (SOC 2, DPAs). The buyer-side checklist an AI vendor or internal platform must satisfy: SOC 2 Type II, ISO 27001/42001, DPAs naming retention windows and subprocessors, HIPAA/GDPR where relevant, pen tests, and AI-specific questionnaire items (model providers used, ZDR, prompt logging). Even an internal 'core AI services' team gets security-reviewed by its own InfoSec and by customers' procurement.
    Search: AI vendor security review SOC 2 checklist, DPA AI subprocessors, ISO 42001 AI management system, AI procurement questionnaire
    Start with: AI Support Vendor Security Review: SOC 2 & Data Protection · AI Agent Security & Compliance: SOC 2, GDPR, and Key Risks
Discovery & design 5
  • Probabilistic UX, designing for uncertainty, and trust calibration. Designing for outputs that are sometimes wrong: showing confidence or provenance, drafts vs answers, easy undo/regenerate, graceful degradation, and calibrating user trust so people neither over-rely nor ignore the AI ('appropriate reliance'). Design questions in AI PM loops are about failure handling, not happy paths.
    Search: designing for AI uncertainty, trust calibration appropriate reliance, probabilistic design UX, confidence display AI UI
    Start with: Designing With Uncertainty: How AI Supercharges Probabilistic Thinking · Building and calibrating trust in AI · Designing for Appropriate Reliance
  • AI UX pattern libraries (Google PAIR, Microsoft HAX). The two canonical frameworks: Google's People + AI Guidebook (six chapters: user needs & success, data, onboarding, explainability, feedback & control, errors) and Microsoft's 18 Guidelines for Human-AI Interaction with the HAX Toolkit (workbook, design patterns, playbook). Citing PAIR or HAX by name in a design answer is a strong 'I know the discipline' signal.
    Search: People + AI Guidebook, Microsoft HAX toolkit 18 guidelines, human-AI interaction guidelines Amershi, AI design patterns
    Start with: People + AI Guidebook · Microsoft HAX Toolkit · Guidelines for Human-AI Interaction (paper)
  • Human-in-the-loop design and autonomy levels. Matching the interaction pattern to risk: accept/reject for low stakes, preview-and-approve for actions touching data or money, mandatory human sign-off for irreversible steps; agent pause/resume and escalation paths. 2026 interview guides explicitly expect 'set human-in-the-loop thresholds for irreversible actions' as an answer component.
    Search: human-in-the-loop AI product design, autonomy levels agents approval, irreversible actions confirmation agents
    Start with: Designing Interfaces Around Uncertain AI Outputs · Guidelines for Human-AI Interaction
  • When NOT to use AI. Recognizing deterministic, high-stakes, or audit-sensitive tasks where rules, classical ML, or plain code beat an LLM on cost, latency, and explainability; the 'plausibility trap' of using probabilistic engines for deterministic work. Saying 'this shouldn't be an LLM feature' at the right moment is the most senior-sounding thing a candidate can do.
    Search: when not to use LLM deterministic tasks, plausibility trap probabilistic engines, classical ML vs LLM decision
    Start with: The Plausibility Trap: Using Probabilistic Engines for Deterministic Tasks · Building Effective AI Agents (find the simplest solution)
  • Prototyping with LLMs and writing AI PRDs. Vibe-coding a prototype to test feasibility before writing the spec, then a PRD that adds: behavioral contract / persona, statistical acceptance criteria (target eval pass rate, not works/doesn't), failure-mode handling, latency and cost budgets, data and privacy requirements, and a launch eval plan. He already prototypes better than most; the delta is the PRD sections that make an AI spec credible.
    Search: AI PRD template, product requirements for AI features acceptance criteria, prototyping with LLMs product discovery, vibe coding prototype PM
    Start with: A Proven AI PRD Template by Miqdad Jaffer (Product Lead @ OpenAI) · How to Write Product Requirement Docs (PRDs) in the AI Era · Writing PRDs for AI Products: A Practical Guide for Senior Product and Cross-Functional Teams
Shipping & operating 4
  • Launch readiness and staged rollouts with eval gates. Ship AI changes like risky infra changes: feature flags, 1% -> 10% -> 50% -> 100% with eval and metric gates at each step, shadow mode, A/B against the old prompt/model, and instant rollback without redeploy. Execution-round question: 'how would you launch this safely?' The answer is flags plus eval gates.
    Search: staged rollout LLM feature flags, shadow mode AI launch, A/B test prompts models, launch readiness checklist AI
    Start with: LLM Observability: Tutorial & Best Practices · What We've Learned From A Year of Building with LLMs (Operational section)
  • Production observability and monitoring. Tracing every call (inputs, outputs, tool calls, tokens, latency, model/prompt version), sampling outputs for ongoing human review, online evals on live traffic, drift detection, and dashboards that tie quality to deployments. Named as a core AI PM skill ('prototyping, observability, evals') in current roadmaps; also the natural home for Langfuse/LangSmith/Arize.
    Search: LLM observability tracing production, online evals live traffic sampling, drift detection LLM quality
    Start with: LLM Monitoring Best Practices: Complete Guide for 2026 · Best LLM Observability Tools for AI Agents (2026)
  • Prompt versioning and change management. Treating prompts as versioned, reviewed artifacts with a registry, approval workflow, automated evals on every change, and production metadata that records which prompt/model version served each request. Root-cause analysis is impossible without it; also a concrete responsibility a platform PM can claim.
    Search: prompt versioning best practices, prompt registry management tools, prompt change control approval
    Start with: Mastering Prompt Versioning: Best Practices for Scalable LLM Development · 6 Best AI Prompt Management Tools with Built-In LLM Observability in 2026
  • Incident response for AI failures. AI incidents are behavioral, not just functional: gradual quality drift while SLOs stay green, undisclosed provider updates, retrieval index staleness, prompt injection in the wild. Runbooks: detect, triage, contain (route to fallback model / disable feature flag), evaluate, fix, blameless postmortem. Behavioral rounds ask for a time something broke; having the AI-specific runbook vocabulary makes his Claude Code war stories land.
    Search: LLM incident response runbook, AI postmortem behavioral failure, model rollback procedure, silent degradation LLM
    Start with: The LLM Incident Response Runbook for 2026 · The AI Incident Response Playbook: Diagnosing LLM Degradation in Production
Governance 3
  • EU AI Act. Risk-tiered regulation: banned practices (in force Feb 2025), GPAI model obligations (Aug 2025), Article 50 transparency/disclosure obligations (Aug 2026), with high-risk system obligations deferred by the 2026 Digital Omnibus to Dec 2027 / Aug 2028. Know your role (provider vs deployer) and risk tier. Any product with EU users or an EU-hosted platform must classify itself; interviewers may ask about disclosure of AI-generated content.
    Search: EU AI Act summary risk tiers, EU AI Act Article 50 transparency, Digital Omnibus AI Act deferral, GPAI obligations
    Start with: High-level summary of the AI Act · A comprehensive EU AI Act Summary [August 2026 update]
  • NIST AI RMF and ISO/IEC 42001. The US voluntary framework (Govern, Map, Measure, Manage) that regulators reference as a baseline, and the auditable AI management-system standard (ISO 42001) enterprises increasingly certify against. Together with the EU AI Act they form the governance triad. One sentence on each is enough to pass; not knowing they exist is a red flag for a platform PM.
    Search: NIST AI RMF govern map measure manage, ISO 42001 certification, EU AI Act vs NIST AI RMF vs ISO 42001
    Start with: EU AI Act vs NIST AI RMF vs ISO/IEC 42001: A Plain English Comparison · Global AI Governance Comparison 2026
  • Copyright, training data, model licenses, and disclosure. Fair-use rulings (Bartz v. Anthropic, Kadrey v. Meta, 2025), the shift toward provenance and licensing, the US Copyright Office's AI reports, open-weight license terms (e.g. Llama Community License is source-available, not OSI open source), output ownership/indemnity clauses, and disclosure of AI-generated content. Knowing that 'open source' Llama has a license with an acceptable-use policy, and that vendors offer IP indemnity, is the kind of detail that separates platform PMs.
    Search: Bartz v Anthropic fair use, Llama Community License commercial terms, AI output copyright indemnity, AI-generated content disclosure labeling
    Start with: A New Look at Fair Use: Anthropic, Meta, and Copyright in AI Training · Copyright and Artificial Intelligence, Part 3: Generative AI Training
Career & interviews 3
  • What AI PM interviews ask. Current loop structure: a dedicated AI product-sense round (sometimes with live prototyping), technical drill-downs on evals/RAG/tokens/latency even for non-AI roles, metrics-definition questions, responsible-AI woven into every round, and deep behavioral probing. Immediate need: know the question shapes before the next loop.
    Search: AI product manager interview questions 2026, how would you evaluate this AI feature interview, AI PM case interview metrics, Exponent AI PM questions
    Start with: How to Answer AI Product Evaluation and Metrics Questions | 2026 AI PM Interview Guide · AI Product Manager Interview Questions (2026 Guide) · Product Manager Artificial Intelligence Interview Questions
  • Courses and certifications. The credible options: Husain & Shankar 'AI Evals for Engineers & PMs' (Maven, highest-grossing), Marily Nika's AI PM bootcamp/certification (Maven), Miqdad Jaffer's AI PM Certification (Product Faculty/Maven), Aman Khan's evals course with Andrew Ng (DeepLearning.AI), Reforge AI courses, Product School AIPC, DeepLearning.AI 'Generative AI for Everyone'. Aakash Gupta maintains a ranked list. One cohort course is a resume line and a structured 4-6 week path; the evals course is the obvious pick given the gap.
    Search: best AI PM courses 2026, AI evals course Maven, AI product management certification
    Start with: The 12 Best AI PM Courses to Supercharge Your Career · AI Product Management Bootcamp & Certification · AI Product Management Certification
  • Canonical reading list and people to follow. The shared canon hiring managers assume: applied-llms.org, Chip Huyen's AI Engineering, Anthropic's engineering posts, Hamel's and Eugene Yan's blogs, Lenny's AI PM episodes (Aman Khan, Kevin Weil, Mike Krieger, Hamel & Shreya), Aakash Gupta's AI PM roadmap, Peter Yang's tutorials. Being able to reference these by name is table stakes in conversation with AI PM hiring managers.
    Search: AI PM reading list, Lenny's podcast AI PM episodes, AI PM learning roadmap Aakash
    Start with: What We've Learned From A Year of Building with LLMs · AI PM Learning Roadmap · Becoming an AI PM | Aman Khan
Suggested learning path (12 steps)
  1. Read Aman Khan's 'Beyond vibe checks' and Anthropic's 'Demystifying evals for AI agents' in one sitting (2 hours). Write down the vocabulary: offline/online eval, golden dataset, grader, LLM-as-judge, error analysis, regression suite, task/trial/trace. Closes the exact gap that surfaced in the interview and gives the vocabulary for everything after.
  2. Watch the Hamel & Shreya Lenny's Podcast episode, then do a mini error analysis on your own Claude Code sessions: pull 30 traces, open-code failures, cluster into a taxonomy, write 3 binary rubrics. Turns 'I build with AI' into 'I have run the eval workflow' -- a story you can tell in every interview.
  3. Read Hamel's LLM-as-judge guide and skim 'Who Validates the Validators'. Know: binary over Likert, validate the judge against human labels, position bias. LLM-as-judge is the most likely follow-up once you say 'evals'; you need its failure modes, not just its name.
  4. Learn the metrics stack: task success, acceptance rate, containment vs deflection, thumbs rates, latency (TTFT), cost per task. Draft a north-star plus guardrail metrics for one hypothetical AI feature. 'Define the north star for this AI feature' appears in nearly every loop.
  5. Read Anthropic 'Building Effective Agents' and the Aakash RAG vs fine-tuning vs prompting guide. Be able to whiteboard the workflow patterns and the customization hierarchy. System-design-for-AI vocabulary; especially relevant to a core-AI-services role.
  6. Study the platform layer: read the FloTorch LLM gateway comparison and LiteLLM/Portkey docs. Sketch a 'core AI services' architecture: gateway, routing, budgets, prompt registry, shared evals, model catalog, deprecation calendar. This is the literal job; interviewers will ask what you would build first and who your customers are.
  7. Read the a16z outcome-based pricing note and one AI gross-margin piece. Prepare a two-minute answer on seat vs usage vs outcome pricing and inference COGS. Unit economics is where senior PMs separate from junior ones.
  8. Skim Google PAIR Guidebook chapter list and Microsoft HAX 18 guidelines; read one trust-calibration article. Prepare an answer on human-in-the-loop thresholds for irreversible actions. Design rounds for AI are about failure handling and trust, and citing PAIR/HAX by name signals discipline knowledge.
  9. One-hour governance pass: EU AI Act high-level summary (tiers, Article 50 disclosure, 2027/2028 deferrals), NIST AI RMF's four functions, what a system card is, ZDR/SOC 2/DPA basics. Responsible AI and enterprise readiness show up in every round; a sentence on each is enough to pass.
  10. Do mock interviews using the Crack PM Interview and Exponent AI PM question banks; rehearse 'how would you eval this', 'the model is confident but wrong', and 'should this even be an LLM feature'. Converts reading into fluent answers before the next loop (target: end of week 2-3).
  11. Go deep: enroll in Hamel & Shreya's Maven evals course (or Aman Khan's DeepLearning.AI course), and read Chip Huyen's AI Engineering chapters 3-4 plus applied-llms.org end to end. After credibility comes competence; the evals course is the highest-signal credential for the gap you found.
  12. Build a small public artifact: an eval suite plus observability (Langfuse or Braintrust) over one of your Claude Code projects, with a write-up of failure modes found and fixed. A portfolio piece that proves the whole loop -- error analysis, evals, monitoring -- in your own product.
Interview questions to expect (14)
  • How would you evaluate whether this AI feature is good? Walk me through your eval strategy from day one to production.
  • Users say the model is confident but wrong. How do you diagnose and fix it? (retrieval vs generation, grounding, judge, rubric)
  • Define a north star and guardrail metrics for [AI assistant / copilot / support agent]. Why not deflection rate?
  • Prompting, RAG, or fine-tuning for this use case -- and in what order would you try them?
  • Should this be an LLM feature at all? When would you use deterministic logic instead?
  • Design an internal AI platform / model gateway. What do you build first, who are your customers, how do you measure success?
  • How do you price this AI product: per seat, usage, or outcome? What does inference cost do to gross margin?
  • A provider is deprecating the model you depend on in 60 days. What is your plan?
  • Design the human-in-the-loop for an agent that can take irreversible actions.
  • How would you handle prompt injection / a jailbreak found in production?
  • Our EU customers ask about the AI Act and data retention. What do you tell them?
  • Tell me about a time an AI system you built behaved unexpectedly. How did you detect it and what did you change?
  • Write the acceptance criteria for an AI feature. How is it different from a normal PRD?
  • How would you build a golden dataset with no production data yet?

Names to know: Hamel Husain, Shreya Shankar, Eugene Yan, Aman Khan, Lenny Rachitsky, Aakash Gupta, Marily Nika, Miqdad Jaffer, Peter Yang, Kevin Weil, Mike Krieger, Sherwin Wu, Chip Huyen, Andrew Ng, Bryan Bischof, Charles Frye, Jason Liu, Cameron Wolfe, Saleema Amershi, Sarah Guo

Open questions (6)
  • Does the target 'core AI services' role skew toward internal platform (gateway, evals-as-a-service, model catalog) or toward customer-facing AI features? The learning emphasis (step 6 vs steps 4/8) shifts accordingly.
  • Which eval/observability tool does the target company use (Braintrust, LangSmith, Langfuse, Arize, in-house)? Worth learning that one hands-on before the loop.
  • Is a paid cohort course (Maven evals ~4 weeks) worth the time before the next interview, or should it follow the offer?
  • How current is Mike on the 2026 regulatory shifts (Digital Omnibus deferrals, Article 50 in force Aug 2026)? Verify dates against artificialintelligenceact.eu before quoting them.
  • Which of the 'names to know' the hiring manager actually follows -- LinkedIn stalking the panel would sharpen the reading list.
  • Depth on fine-tuning and RLHF: does the role expect PM-level fluency only, or hands-on experience with training pipelines?
