Learning plan: building and maintaining multi-agent systems with Claude

A map of what to learn, in the order it was researched. Each topic gives the words to search for, what it is, why it matters here, the best practices found, and where to go read or watch.

Assembled 2026-09-03 by 5 research agents working in parallel, then merged and de-duplicated: 107 topics. Everything below is a first draft and has not been reviewed yet.

The six seed topics this started from

  1. 1Context window management / splitting work across sessions
  2. 2Inter-agent communication / passing plans and specs between sessions
  3. 3The overseer / orchestrator conversation pattern
  4. 4The continuous maintenance / custodian engineer
  5. 5Structuring large inputs for the agent
  6. 6Verbal dump, organize with one LLM, hand to the primary LLM

Verdict on the three handoff mechanisms

  • copy paste: Works, but it is the lossy channel the literature calls the telephone game, and it bloats the orchestrator's context with detail the orchestrator should not hold. Keep it only for the orchestrator's short brief to a specialist. For the return trip, have the specialist write the spec to a versioned file (specs/<feature>.md with acceptance criteria, interfaces, non-goals, verification step) and hand the orchestrator a path plus a 5-10 line summary; the orchestrator @-references the file when it needs detail.
  • shared md file: Right instinct (it is a blackboard), wrong shape. One file that every session appends to is last-writer-wins, goes stale, and nobody knows what is current. Upgrade to: per-session/per-task handoff files with a fixed status schema (goal, done, not-working, decisions, next), an append-only INDEX.md, written on SessionStart/PreCompact/end via hooks; or graduate to a real ledger (Claude Code Task tools inside a team, or Beads) where work is claimed and status is queryable.
  • direct messages: The correct tool for time-sensitive short findings between hand-run sessions, and Anthropic built it for exactly Mike's layout (cross-session messaging, v2.1.224+). It is plain text, capped, throttled, and treated as untrusted; it is not a spec transport. Use it for 'X landed, rebase is safe', 'spec written at path Y', decision requests, and notify_when_idle. Inside agent teams, be aware of the still-open silent-drop bugs and verify with the task list, not the message.
  • recommended default: Artifact plus pointer. Specs and plans are files in the repo; each specialist owns distinct files (ideally its own worktree); status lives in a structured ledger (per-session handoff files with an index, or a task list); SendMessage carries only short notifications that point at those files; the orchestrator holds summaries and paths, never full specs; every handoff ends with an acceptance check (spec criteria, /goal, or a TaskCompleted/Stop hook) before the orchestrator relays it onward. Put this protocol in CLAUDE.md so every session starts knowing it.

Verdict on the dump-then-organize workflow

Yes, it is a recognised pattern, under several names: 'meta-prompting' (one LLM writing or refining a prompt for another), 'prompt refinement', and more generally 'prompt chaining' (the output of one call becomes the input of the next). Anthropic itself ships two versions - the Console prompt generator / cookbook 'metaprompt' notebook, and the Console prompt improver - and community Claude Code skills ('Prompt Refiner', 'Brain-Dump Capture') do exactly the dump-then-organize step. Harper Reed's brainstorm -> spec -> plan -> execute workflow is the best-known practitioner version. Honest caveats: (1) with today's Claude Code you don't need a second model or a second app; the same Claude in plan mode, an interview skill, or a /brief skill can organize the dump inside the session with codebase context, which is usually better than a context-free organizer. (2) The organizer will smooth over ambiguities, so read its output and ask it to list 'open questions' rather than guessing. (3) Anthropic's stronger recommendation for larger features is the inverse: give a one-line brief and let Claude interview you with AskUserQuestion, then write SPEC.md and implement in a fresh session. Best of both: paste the raw dump, ask for a structured brief plus an interview on what's missing, approve the spec, then execute. Maturity: emerging as a named workflow, established as a technique.

Context management, session splitting, orchestrator pattern 18

1Context engineering (the discipline)

establishedSeed topic 1
Search forcontext engineeringcontext engineering for AI agentsattention budgetsmallest set of high-signal tokenswrite select compress isolatejust-in-time context retrieval

What it is. The umbrella name for everything Mike is asking about. Coined widely after Karpathy's June 2025 post ('the delicate art and science of filling the context window with just the right information for the next step'), and formalized by Anthropic's Sept 2025 engineering post. It treats the context window as a finite, degrading resource and asks 'what configuration of context is most likely to produce the behavior I want?' rather than 'what prompt should I write?'. LangChain's Lance Martin gives the most portable mental model: Write (persist state outside the window), Select (pull in only what is relevant), Compress (summarize/trim), Isolate (give messy work its own window).

Why it matters for you. Every symptom he has (full windows, overseer bloat, spec loss between sessions) is a context-engineering problem with a name and a literature. Learning the vocabulary unlocks every other topic in this file.

Best practices (4)
  • Treat context as the primary constraint; Anthropic's own best-practices page says 'most best practices are based on one constraint: the context window fills up fast and performance degrades as it fills.'
  • Apply Write/Select/Compress/Isolate to each session: write state to files, select via @file references and skills, compress with /compact, isolate with subagents/worktrees.
  • Prefer just-in-time retrieval (lightweight pointers, let the agent read what it needs) over pre-loading everything into CLAUDE.md.
  • Read the Anthropic post's 'long-horizon' section: compaction, structured note-taking, and sub-agent architectures are the three canonical techniques.
Where to learn (8 sources)
  • Effective context engineering for AI agents Anthropic (Rajasekaran, Dixon, Ryan, Hadfield), 2025-09, blog
  • Andrej Karpathy on X: +1 for 'context engineering' over 'prompt engineering' Andrej Karpathy, 2025-06, thread
  • Context engineering Simon Willison, 2025-06, blog
  • Context Engineering for Agents (write, select, compress, isolate) LangChain / Lance Martin, 2025-07, blog
  • langchain-ai/context_engineering notebooks LangChain, 2025, repo
  • Context Engineering for Coding Agents Birgitta Böckeler / Thoughtworks (martinfowler.com), 2026-02, blog

Names to know: Andrej Karpathy, Anthropic Applied AI team (Prithvi Rajasekaran, Jeremy Hadfield), Lance Martin (LangChain), Birgitta Böckeler (Thoughtworks), Yichao 'Peak' Ji (Manus), Simon Willison

2Context rot, lost-in-the-middle, and the 'dumb zone'

establishedSeed topic 1
Search forcontext rotlost in the middlelong context degradationneedle in a haystackdumb zone 40%attention budgetperformance degrades as context fills

What it is. The empirical reason context management matters. Chroma's July 2025 'Context Rot' report tested 18 frontier models and found all of them degrade as input length grows, even on trivially simple tasks; distractors and low question-answer similarity make it worse. The 2023 'Lost in the Middle' paper showed models attend best to the start and end of context. Practitioners translated this into rules of thumb: Dex Horthy (HumanLayer) calls ~40% context fill the start of the 'dumb zone'; Geoffrey Huntley observes quality clipping around 147-152k of a 200k window; several guides say never exceed 60%.

Why it matters for you. Explains why his overseer session gets worse before it hits the hard limit, and why 'just wait for auto-compact' is the wrong strategy. The fix is to act at 40-60%, not 95%.

Best practices (4)
  • Watch /context (or a custom status line) and treat ~40-50% as a yellow light, ~60% as red.
  • Put critical instructions at the top of CLAUDE.md and SKILL.md files (start-of-context is attended to best; truncation keeps the start).
  • Keep distractors out: scope investigations narrowly, delegate broad reads to subagents, don't dump logs into the main window.
  • Do not assume a 1M-context model removes the problem; rot is about attention, not capacity.
Where to learn (7 sources)
  • Context Rot: How Increasing Input Tokens Impacts LLM Performance Chroma (Kelly Hong, Anton Troynikov, Jeff Huber), 2025-07, paper
  • LLM Context Rot (summary of the Chroma study) Cobus Greyling, 2025, blog
  • Context Rot: Why LLMs Degrade as Context Grows Morph, blog
  • Advanced Context Engineering for Coding Agents (Dex Horthy talk notes, 'dumb zone') ametel01 summarizing Dex Horthy / HumanLayer, 2025, blog
  • Dex Horthy talk video: Advanced Context Engineering for Coding Agents Dex Horthy / AI Engineer, 2025, video
  • Ralph Wiggum as a software engineer (observed 147k-152k quality clip) Geoffrey Huntley, 2025-07, blog

Names to know: Kelly Hong / Anton Troynikov / Jeff Huber (Chroma), Dex Horthy (HumanLayer), Geoffrey Huntley

3Compaction: /compact vs /clear, what survives, auto-compact thresholds

establishedSeed topic 1
Search forClaude Code compact/compact vs /clearauto-compact/autocompactwhat survives compactioncompact with focus instructionssummarize from here/btw side question

What it is. Claude Code's built-in Compress lever. /compact replaces conversation history with a structured summary (optionally focused: '/compact focus on the auth bug'); /clear wipes context entirely but saves the transcript for /resume; /rewind lets you 'Summarize from here' or 'Summarize up to here' to compact only part of the conversation. The docs publish a 'what survives compaction' table: project-root CLAUDE.md, auto memory, and the plan-mode plan are re-injected from disk; the last five read/edited files are re-read; invoked skill bodies are re-injected (5k tokens/skill cap); nested CLAUDE.md and path-scoped rules only reload when their files are touched; hook-added context is summarized away. /autocompact <tokens> sets when the automatic pass fires. /btw asks a side question without adding it to history.

Why it matters for you. He is probably letting auto-compact fire at 95% and losing the wrong things. Knowing what survives tells him what to move into CLAUDE.md/plan files so it can't be summarized away.

Best practices (5)
  • Compact at a natural breakpoint (after a subtask) around 50-60%, with a focus hint, instead of letting auto-compact guess at 95%.
  • Add a CLAUDE.md line like 'When compacting, always preserve the full list of modified files and any test commands' to steer the summary.
  • After two failed corrections on the same issue, /clear and restart with a better prompt (Anthropic's explicit rule).
  • Use /clear between unrelated tasks ('kitchen sink session' is a named anti-pattern), /btw for throwaway questions, and /context to audit what is loaded.
  • Anything you cannot afford to lose to a summary belongs in a file on disk, not in chat.
Where to learn (5 sources)
  • Explore the context window (interactive; includes 'What survives compaction' table) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Best practices for Claude Code ('Manage context aggressively', failure patterns) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Checkpointing (rewind and summarize) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Claude Code Context Window Management: how auto compact actually works AI Workflow Pro, 2026, blog
  • Claude Code Context Window: /compact, /clear explained explainx.ai, 2026, blog

Names to know: Anthropic Claude Code team

4Durable memory: CLAUDE.md, auto memory, .claude/rules, skills (progressive disclosure)

establishedSeed topic 1
Search forCLAUDE.md best practiceswriting a good CLAUDE.mdClaude Code auto memory MEMORY.md.claude/rules path-scoped rulesprogressive disclosureAGENTS.mdinstruction budget 150-200 instructionsskills vs CLAUDE.md

What it is. The Write/Select layer that persists across sessions. CLAUDE.md (user, project, local scopes; loaded every session; imports via @path) holds instructions you write. Auto memory (~/.claude/projects/<project>/memory/MEMORY.md plus topic files; first 200 lines/25KB loaded) holds notes Claude writes about your corrections and preferences. .claude/rules/*.md can be path-scoped so they load only when matching files are read. Skills (SKILL.md) load on demand from a ~100-token description, which is the progressive-disclosure mechanism for anything not needed every session. HumanLayer's guidance: keep CLAUDE.md short (theirs is under 60 lines), because the system prompt already spends ~50 of a model's ~150-200 reliably-followed instructions; OpenAI's harness-engineering team keeps AGENTS.md ~100 lines as a map with pointers to docs/.

Why it matters for you. This is where the overseer's standing decisions and each specialist's conventions should live so they survive /clear and compaction and are shared across his parallel sessions. It is also the main defense against the telephone game.

Best practices (6)
  • Ask of every CLAUDE.md line: 'would removing this cause a mistake?' If not, cut it. Run /doctor to get trim proposals.
  • Keep CLAUDE.md under ~200 lines (docs) or far shorter (HumanLayer: <60); move procedures into skills and area-specific rules into path-scoped .claude/rules.
  • Prefer pointers to copies: file:line references and 'read docs/X.md when doing Y' rather than pasted content.
  • Add to CLAUDE.md when Claude makes the same mistake twice or you re-type a correction from last session; check it into git.
  • Use /memory to audit auto memory; use /context to confirm which memory files actually loaded.
  • For multi-tool teams, keep one AGENTS.md and have CLAUDE.md import it (@AGENTS.md).
Where to learn (7 sources)
  • How Claude remembers your project (CLAUDE.md + auto memory + rules) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Writing a good CLAUDE.md HumanLayer (Kyle), 2025-11, blog
  • Claude Code Auto Memory: complete guide Is Ray, Not Array, 2026-03, blog
  • Claude Code Skills vs Slash Commands: Complete 2026 Guide The Prompt Shelf, 2026, blog
  • Harness engineering: leveraging Codex in an agent-first world (AGENTS.md as ~100-line map) OpenAI, 2026-02, blog
  • OpenAI's Agent-First Codebase Learnings (summary) Alex Lavaee, 2026, blog

Names to know: HumanLayer (Dex Horthy, Kyle), Boris Cherny (team shares one CLAUDE.md, adds a line every time Claude errs), OpenAI Codex team

5Session hygiene: short scoped sessions, naming, resume/branch/fork, checkpoints

establishedSeed topic 1
Search forClaude Code sessions/rename named sessionsclaude --resume --continue--fork-session/branch/rewind checkpointsresume from summaryone task per session

What it is. Anthropic's docs now say 'treat sessions like branches: each workstream gets its own persistent context.' Sessions are saved transcripts you can name (claude -n auth-refactor, /rename), resume (--continue, --resume <name>), branch (/branch copies the transcript; --fork-session from the CLI), and rewind (every prompt is a checkpoint; /rewind restores conversation, code, or both). Resuming a large, cold session offers 'Resume from summary' (runs /compact) versus 'as-is'. The docs' named anti-patterns are the kitchen-sink session, correcting over and over, and infinite exploration.

Why it matters for you. His 'fork' habit already maps to /branch and --fork-session. Naming and scoping sessions per feature, and starting a fresh session once a spec exists, is the cheap fix for windows that never stop filling.

Best practices (4)
  • One session per workstream, named after it; /clear or a new session when the task changes.
  • Once a spec/plan exists, start a fresh session to implement it (Anthropic: 'the new session has clean context focused entirely on implementation').
  • Use /branch to try a risky approach without polluting the main session; /rewind instead of accumulating corrections.
  • Long-lived overseer session: keep it in plan/review mode and push implementation into fresh sessions or subagents so it stays lean.
Where to learn (5 sources)
  • Manage sessions (name, resume, branch, fork) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Checkpointing Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Best practices for Claude Code ('Manage your session', 'Avoid common failure patterns') Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Claude Code --continue and --resume guide The Prompt Shelf, 2026, blog
  • Named Sessions: your git branches have names, why shouldn't your Claude sessions Rajesh Royal (DEV), 2026, blog

Names to know: Anthropic Claude Code team

6Research -> Plan -> Implement, plan mode, spec-first prompting

established
Search forClaude Code plan modeexplore plan code commitresearch plan implement RPIspec-driven developmentspec-firstinterview me then write SPEC.mdKiro requirements design tasksGitHub Spec Kit

What it is. The dominant workflow shape across Anthropic docs (Explore -> Plan -> Implement -> Commit), HumanLayer (Research -> Plan -> Implement with a fresh context per phase and 'intentional compaction' into markdown artifacts), Superpowers (brainstorm -> plan -> TDD implement), and the spec-driven-development movement (Kiro's requirements.md/design.md/tasks.md, GitHub Spec Kit, BMAD). Plan mode (Shift+Tab, or claude --permission-mode plan) makes Claude read-only while it researches; Ctrl+G opens the plan in your editor. Anthropic's 'let Claude interview you' prompt produces a SPEC.md that you then execute in a new session. Boris Cherny starts most sessions in plan mode and iterates until he likes the plan, then lets Claude one-shot it.

Why it matters for you. As a PM this is his natural leverage point: the plan/spec is the artifact that crosses session boundaries without loss, and it is what the overseer should produce and hand to specialists.

Best practices (5)
  • Plan when a mistake would be annoying to reverse or the change touches many files; skip planning for one-sentence diffs.
  • Use the interview prompt: 'Interview me in detail using AskUserQuestion... then write a complete spec to SPEC.md.' Then implement in a fresh session.
  • Make specs self-contained: name files/interfaces, state what is out of scope, end with an end-to-end verification step.
  • Write research and plan artifacts to disk (research.md, plan.md) and review those, not thousands of lines of diff (HumanLayer).
  • The plan Claude writes in plan mode survives compaction; conversation-only decisions do not.
Where to learn (8 sources)
  • Best practices: Explore first, then plan, then code; Let Claude interview you Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • The explore -> plan -> code -> commit workflow (Claude Code 101) Anthropic Claude Academy, 2026, docs
  • The Explore -> Plan -> Code -> Commit workflow in Claude Code (video) YouTube (Claude Code 101 series), video
  • Advanced Context Engineering for Coding Agents (RPI, intentional compaction) summary of Dex Horthy / HumanLayer, 2025, blog
  • Kiro and the future of AI spec-driven software development AWS Kiro, 2025, blog
  • Spec-Driven Development (SDD): The Definitive 2026 Guide BCMS, 2026, blog

7State in the repo, not in chat: progress files, handoff docs, agent harnesses, fresh-context loops

established
Search forlong-running agents harnessinitializer agentclaude-progress.txtfeature_list.jsonhandoff documentsession handoffprogress filerepository as system of record

What it is. The Write lever taken seriously: make the filesystem and git the memory, so any fresh session can pick up. Anthropic's Nov 2025 harness post uses an initializer agent (creates init.sh, claude-progress.txt, feature_list.json, first commit) and a coding agent that starts each session by reading the progress file and git log, does one feature, verifies, commits, and leaves clean handoff notes. OpenAI's harness-engineering post (3 engineers, ~1M+ lines, zero hand-written code) says 'if it isn't in the repo it doesn't exist' and keeps design docs, execution plans, and debt tracking versioned. Geoffrey Huntley's Ralph Wiggum loop is the extreme form: `while :; do cat PROMPT.md | claude ; done`, one task per iteration, brand-new context every pass, state in @fix_plan.md and specs. Community tooling (handoff CLI, agent-handoff, Claude-Handover plugin, softaworks session-handoff skill) packages the handoff-document pattern.

Why it matters for you. This is the direct cure for 'the overseer forgets' and 'the specialist session died at 100%': if every session writes what it did, what is verified, and what is next to a file in the repo, the context window stops being the source of truth.

Best practices (10)
  • Keep a per-feature progress/handoff file (what was attempted, what changed, how it was verified, what is still failing, next steps) and have every session read it first and update it last.
  • One feature per session; commit with descriptive messages as handoff points; leave the tree mergeable.
  • Start-of-session ritual: read progress file, check git log, run the smoke test, then work.
  • Consider a Ralph-style loop for well-specified backlog grinding (fresh context each pass, PROMPT.md + plan file), but keep humans reviewing plans.
  • For the overseer: keep a DECISIONS.md / architecture notes file so decisions are re-readable rather than re-derived.
  • Keep the source of truth in files the agent reads on boot: feature list with pass/fail, progress notes, a runnable init script.
  • Require one feature per session and end-to-end verification before marking it passing.
  • Never let the worker be the judge; a separate evaluator (subagent, /goal evaluator, Stop hook) decides completion.
  • Leave artifacts a cold-start agent can use: git log, progress file, failing test list.
  • Design for interruption: every session should leave the repo in a committable state.
Where to learn (12 sources)
  • Effective harnesses for long-running agents Anthropic (Justin Young), 2025-11, blog
  • anthropics/cwc-long-running-agents (companion repo) Anthropic, 2026, repo
  • Harness engineering: leveraging Codex in an agent-first world OpenAI, 2026-02, blog
  • Ralph Wiggum as a 'software engineer' Geoffrey Huntley, 2025-07, blog
  • Inventing the Ralph Wiggum Loop (podcast with Geoffrey Huntley) Dev Interrupted / LinearB, 2026, talk
  • The Ralph Wiggum Loop: autonomous code generation with a fresh context codecentric, 2026, blog

Names to know: Justin Young (Anthropic), Geoffrey Huntley, OpenAI Codex team, Addy Osmani, Anthropic Applied AI team

8Subagents in Claude Code (context isolation, custom agents, forks, background)

establishedSeed topic 2
Search forClaude Code subagents.claude/agents custom agentsExplore agentAgent tool/subtask fork subagentbackground subagentssubagent context isolationsubagents as context control not roleplay

What it is. A subagent is a worker with its own context window, system prompt, tool allowlist, and optionally its own model, memory, and worktree; only its final summary returns to the caller. Defined as markdown with YAML frontmatter in .claude/agents/ (project) or ~/.claude/agents/ (user); built-ins are Explore, Plan, and general-purpose. Nesting is allowed to 3 layers by default, up to 20 concurrent. A fork (/subtask) inherits the parent's whole conversation instead of starting fresh, good for side tasks that need the history. Dex Horthy's framing: subagents are a context-control mechanism (fork a clean window for messy reads, return facts), not role-play personas.

Why it matters for you. The cheapest way to keep his overseer lean: research, log-reading, and review go into subagents; only summaries land in the main window. Custom agents in .claude/agents let him codify each 'specialist' once.

Best practices (11)
  • Prompt 'use subagents to investigate X' for anything that would flood context (Anthropic's 'infinite exploration' anti-pattern fix).
  • Give each subagent a tight description (descriptions cost ~15k tokens total budget at startup), restrict tools (read-only for reviewers), and put detail in the body.
  • Use forks (/subtask) when the side task needs the conversation history; use fresh subagents when it should not.
  • Parallel subagents returning verbose results can still bloat the caller; ask for terse structured summaries.
  • Remember what a subagent does NOT get: conversation history, the main session's auto memory. Put shared conventions in CLAUDE.md so both see them.
  • Give research subagents read-only tools (Read, Grep, Glob); adding Bash/Edit removes the isolation guarantee that justified the subagent.
  • Use model: haiku or sonnet for simple, high-volume subagents; reserve opus for judgment-heavy reviewers.
  • Add an adversarial review step: a fresh subagent reviews the diff against PLAN.md and reports gaps that affect correctness only (tell it to ignore style, or it will over-engineer).
  • Use memory: project so a reviewer subagent accumulates recurring issues across sessions.
  • Use isolation: worktree for parallelizable migrations and batch edits.
  • Include 'use proactively' in descriptions to encourage automatic delegation; check the /usage attribution to see which subagents cost the most.
Where to learn (8 sources)
  • Create custom subagents Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Subagents in the SDK Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Claude Code subagents: the 2026 production playbook Totalum, 2026, blog
  • Claude Code Subagents: A Practical 2026 Guide Nimbalyst, 2026, blog
  • VoltAgent/awesome-claude-code-subagents (100+ examples) VoltAgent, 2025, repo
  • Claude Code Sub Agents Tutorial ZazenCodes, blog

Names to know: Anthropic Claude Code team, Dex Horthy, VoltAgent (subagent library)

9Parallel sessions with git worktrees (Boris's five-tab pattern, writer/reviewer)

establishedSeed topic 2
Search forgit worktrees Claude Codeclaude --worktree -wparallel Claude Code sessionsmultiple Claude instanceswriter reviewer patternone Claude per tabEnterWorktree.worktreeinclude

What it is. The manual, human-coordinated way to run many sessions on one repo without edits colliding. `claude --worktree <name>` creates .claude/worktrees/<name> on branch worktree-<name>; the desktop app gives every session a worktree automatically; subagents can get `isolation: worktree`. Boris Cherny runs five local sessions in numbered tabs (feature, tests, docs, reviews...) plus 5-10 web sessions, using notifications to know which needs input. Anthropic's Writer/Reviewer pattern: session A implements, session B (fresh context, no bias toward code it wrote) reviews, A addresses the feedback.

Why it matters for you. This is exactly his current setup, now with first-class tooling. Worktrees remove the file-conflict risk between specialist sessions, and the Writer/Reviewer split is a principled version of 'run it past the overseer.'

Best practices (4)
  • One worktree per specialist session; add .claude/worktrees/ to .gitignore; use .worktreeinclude to copy .env files in.
  • Number/name tabs by purpose; turn on notifications so idle sessions don't sit waiting.
  • Use a separate fresh-context session (or subagent) as reviewer rather than asking the writer to self-review.
  • Partition work by file ownership; integrate after verification, not during.
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • Run parallel sessions with worktrees Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Best practices: Run multiple Claude sessions (Writer/Reviewer) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • How the Creator of Claude Code Actually Uses Claude Code Push to Prod (on Boris Cherny), 2026, blog
  • Boris Cherny's Claude Code tips (132+ tips) community compilation, 2026, blog
  • Mastering Git Worktrees with Claude Code for Parallel Development Dogukan Uraz Tuna, 2025, blog
  • Claude Code Worktrees: Parallel Sessions Without Conflicts claudefa.st, 2026, blog

Names to know: Boris Cherny, Anthropic Claude Code team

10Agent teams (lead + teammates, shared task list, mailbox)

emergingSeed topic 3
Search forClaude Code agent teamsCLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMSteam lead teammatesshared task listTeammateIdle hookswarm orchestration Claude Codesubagents vs agent teamsteam lead / teammates

What it is. Anthropic's experimental, built-in version of Mike's orchestrator setup: one session is the lead, it spawns teammates (full Claude Code sessions with their own windows), populates a shared task list with dependencies, and teammates message each other and the lead. Differences from subagents: teammates are peers who self-coordinate and talk to each other; subagents just report back. Docs list the strong use cases (research and review, new modules with clear ownership, competing-hypothesis debugging, cross-layer changes) and limits (no session resume for teammates, no nested teams, no worktree isolation for teammates so file ownership must be partitioned, linear token scaling, lead is fixed).

Why it matters for you. It is the closest official implementation of 'overseer plus specialists.' Even if he keeps running sessions by hand, the docs' sizing and pitfalls advice (3-5 teammates, 5-6 tasks each, avoid same-file edits, lead sometimes starts implementing itself) transfers directly.

Best practices (11)
  • Check whether subagents or cross-session messaging suffice before forming a team (docs say so explicitly).
  • Start with research/review tasks; graduate to parallel implementation once file ownership is clear.
  • 3-5 teammates, self-contained tasks with a clear deliverable, 5-6 tasks per teammate so the lead can rebalance.
  • Include full task context in the spawn prompt; teammates do not inherit the lead's conversation.
  • Use TaskCompleted/TeammateIdle hooks as quality gates; tell the lead to 'wait for your teammates' if it starts doing the work itself.
  • Embed task-specific context in the spawn prompt; teammates have no conversation history (30 Tips #22).
  • Partition file ownership so no two teammates edit the same file; teams do not use worktrees.
  • Use TaskCompleted hooks to reject completion until tests pass; use TeammateIdle to push a teammate back to work.
  • Start with read-only tasks (review, research, competing hypotheses) before parallel implementation.
  • Monitor and steer; the lead may start implementing itself or declare done early.
  • Know the open bugs: silent drops of teammate-to-lead SendMessage for background teammates (#43706), sends to a misspelled name that 'succeed' into an orphan inbox (#25135), duplicate spawns after idle (#29271), team config lost after compaction.
Where to learn (12 sources)
  • Orchestrate teams of Claude Code sessions Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • 30 Tips for Claude Code Agent Teams Push to Prod, 2026, blog
  • Claude Code Swarm Orchestration Skill (gist) Kieran Klaassen, 2026, repo
  • Agent Teams with Claude Code and Claude Agent SDK Isaac Kargar, 2026, blog
  • zircote/claude-team-orchestration plugin zircote, 2026, repo
  • Hooks reference Claude Code Docs, 2026-09, docs

Names to know: Anthropic Claude Code team, Kieran Klaassen, Cobus Greyling, John Kim (Push to Prod), Jaymin West, WorldofAI

11Cross-session messaging and agent view (coordinating the sessions you run yourself)

emergingSeed topic 3
Search forClaude Code cross-session messagingSendMessage ListAgents/list-agents@session mentionnotify_when_idleclaude agents agent viewbackground sessionsRemote Control

What it is. Two 2026 features that turn Mike's hand-run session fleet into something coordinated without a full agent team. Cross-session messaging (v2.1.224+) lets one session's Claude discover (ListAgents) and message (SendMessage) your other sessions on the machine, on the web, or on other machines: 'let @api-worker know the schema migration finished', or ask to be notified when another session goes idle. Messages are plain text, never the sender's history, and the receiving Claude is told it came from another session (it cannot approve permissions or change config). Agent view (`claude agents`, research preview) is one screen to dispatch background sessions, see which need input, and attach.

Why it matters for you. This is the missing glue for his overseer: the overseer can push decisions to specialists and be pinged when they finish, and he can watch all of them from one screen, without the overseer's window carrying everyone's transcripts.

Best practices (10)
  • Name sessions (/rename) so they are addressable with @name.
  • Have the overseer send short findings/decisions to affected specialists instead of pasting; ask for notify-when-idle instead of polling.
  • Because a message is text only, pair it with the shared files (spec, progress, DECISIONS.md) for anything substantive.
  • Use claude agents to dispatch long independent tasks to the background and check back.
  • Name sessions with /rename or --name (e.g. orchestrator, api-worker) so @-mentions and messages route unambiguously.
  • Send a pointer, not a payload: 'Spec written to specs/payments-webhooks.md, sections 3-4 need your review' beats pasting the spec into the message.
  • Set crossSessionInbound to accept on specialist sessions you leave unattended; held messages expire after 5 minutes by default.
  • Use notify_when_idle to get a single notice when a long-running specialist finishes instead of polling.
  • Treat inbound messages as untrusted data (the harness already does); never let a message drive permission or config changes.
  • Batch updates; rapid bursts to one session are refused at the sender.
Where to learn (7 sources)
  • Message your other Claude Code sessions (cross-session messaging) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Run agents in parallel (comparison of subagents, agent view, agent teams, workflows) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Manage multiple agents with agent view Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Agent view in Claude Code (announcement) Anthropic, 2026, blog
  • Claude Code Agents In 2026: Agent View, Subagents, Teams, and what parallel sessions cost CloudZero, 2026, blog
  • Claude Code Cross-Session Messaging Guide (2026) explainx.ai, 2026-08, blog

Names to know: Anthropic Claude Code team, Cobus Greyling (agent view write-ups)

12The orchestrator-worker pattern (supervisor, lead agent, planner/executor)

establishedSeed topic 3
Search fororchestrator-workers patternorchestrator-workersupervisor patternlead agent subagentshierarchical agentsmanager agentplanner executormulti-agent research system

What it is. Yes, Mike's overseer setup is a recognized pattern. Anthropic's 'Building Effective Agents' (Dec 2024) names it orchestrator-workers: a central LLM dynamically decomposes a task, delegates to workers, and synthesizes results; recommended when subtasks cannot be predicted up front (coding is their example). Anthropic's Research feature (June 2025 post) is a production orchestrator-worker system: an Opus lead plans and spawns Sonnet subagents in parallel; it beat single-agent Opus by 90% on their eval but used ~15x the tokens, and the authors flag that coding is a poor fit when work is not parallelizable and needs shared context. Anthropic's Jan 2026 follow-up says multi-agent earns its cost for context protection, parallelization, and specialization; expect 3-10x tokens; decompose by context boundaries, not by problem type. Addy Osmani (Mar 2026) describes the shift from 'pair with one AI' to 'manage an agent team' with lead, specialists, and a standing reviewer.

Why it matters for you. Validates his instinct and gives him the sizing rules and the known failure modes (orchestrator bloat, duplicated work, premature 'done', telephone game) so he can design around them rather than rediscover them.

Best practices (6)
  • The lead's job is delegation quality: each task description needs objective, output format, tools/sources to use, and clear boundaries (Anthropic Research lessons).
  • Scale effort to complexity with explicit rules (simple task: one worker, few calls; complex: many) so the lead doesn't over-spawn.
  • Keep the lead out of implementation: it plans, assigns, reviews summaries, and writes decisions to disk; workers read files, not the lead's transcript.
  • Decompose by context boundary: a feature and its tests belong to the same worker; frontend/backend/tests can be separate workers.
  • Add a fresh-context reviewer between worker completion and lead acceptance (Osmani's 'dedicated reviewer', Anthropic's adversarial review).
  • WIP limit of 3-5 concurrent workers; kill/reassign a worker stuck 3+ iterations on the same error.
Where to learn (7 sources)
  • Building Effective AI Agents Anthropic (Erik Schluntz, Barry Zhang), 2024-12, blog
  • How we built our multi-agent research system Anthropic (Hadfield, Zhang, Lien, Scholz, Fox, Ford), 2025-06, blog
  • When to use multi-agent systems (and when not to) Anthropic, 2026-01, blog
  • The Code Agent Orchestra: what makes multi-agent coding work Addy Osmani, 2026-03, blog
  • Orchestrator-Worker Pattern: An Engineering Reference (2026) BuildingEffectiveAgents.com, 2026, blog
  • How and when to build multi-agent systems LangChain, 2025, blog

Names to know: Anthropic (Erik Schluntz, Barry Zhang, Jeremy Hadfield), Addy Osmani, LangChain (Harrison Chase, Lance Martin)

13The counterargument: single-threaded agents, shared context, and the telephone game

establishedSeed topic 3
Search forDon't build multi-agentsCognition context engineeringsingle writer principleshare full agent tracestelephone game multi-agentcontext loss at handoffsactions carry implicit decisionsPerspectiveGap

What it is. Cognition (Devin) published 'Don't Build Multi-Agents' (June 2025, Walden Yan) the same week as Anthropic's research post. Two principles: share context (full traces, not summaries) and 'actions carry implicit decisions; conflicting decisions produce bad results.' Their Flappy Bird example: two parallel workers build visually incompatible assets because neither saw the other's choices. Their default is a single-threaded linear agent with a dedicated compression model for long tasks; their 2026 refinement is 'writes stay single-threaded, extra agents contribute intelligence, not actions.' Anthropic's Jan 2026 post concedes the same failure modes: context loss at handoffs ('telephone game'), coordination overhead, and workers declaring early victory. The PerspectiveGap benchmark measured orchestration prompts omitting needed shared background 12-45% of the time.

Why it matters for you. His 'telephone game spec loss' worry is a documented, measured failure mode. The synthesis of the debate is his design rule: parallelize reads and reviews freely, serialize or strictly partition writes, and move shared context into files both sides read.

Best practices (5)
  • Single writer per file/area at a time; parallel agents for research, review, and hypothesis testing.
  • Never let a worker act on a paraphrase of the spec: point it at the spec file itself and require it to restate scope before starting.
  • Include 'shared background' explicitly in every delegation (the thing most often omitted): conventions, decisions already made, what is out of scope.
  • Have the lead check worker output against the plan, not just accept 'done' (early-victory problem).
  • Start with the simplest architecture that works; add agents only when evidence (context overflow, real parallelism) justifies it.
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • Don't Build Multi-Agents Cognition (Walden Yan), 2025-06, blog
  • The Agent Architecture Wars: why two AI giants disagree on multi-agent systems Maureese Williams, 2025, blog
  • 3 Multi-Agent Design Patterns: the 'Single Writer' principle from Cognition masa_wunder (note.com), 2026, blog
  • When to use multi-agent systems (and when not to) (failure modes section) Anthropic, 2026-01, blog
  • PerspectiveGap: A Benchmark for Multi-Agent Orchestration Prompting arXiv, 2026-06, paper
  • Agent Orchestration Is the Hard Part: what breaks when agents need to talk to each other dgtldept, 2026, blog

Names to know: Walden Yan (Cognition), Anthropic

14Fan-out/fan-in at scale: dynamic workflows, /batch, ultracode

emergingSeed topic 2
Search forClaude Code dynamic workflowsultracode/batchfan-out fan-inparallel subagents hundredsmap-reduce agents/deep-research workfloworchestration as code

What it is. For work that outgrows turn-by-turn delegation. A dynamic workflow (research preview, May 2026) is a JavaScript script Claude writes that calls agent()/pipeline()/parallel() to run tens to hundreds of subagents; the script, not Claude's context, holds intermediate results, and it can have agents adversarially cross-check each other. Trigger with 'ultracode:' or 'use a workflow', or /effort ultracode to let Claude decide. Limits: 16 concurrent, 1,000 agents per run. /batch splits one large change across 5-30 worktree-isolated subagents that each open a PR. Jarred Sumner used workflows to port Bun from Zig to Rust.

Why it matters for you. Not for daily feature work, but it answers 'what is the fan-out/swarm thing called in Claude Code' and is the right tool for cross-cutting sweeps (audits, migrations, mass reviews) that would otherwise eat a specialist session's context.

Best practices (7)
  • Use workflows for many-item, same-step jobs (audit every route, migrate every component, review every changed file) and for cross-checked research; use subagents/teams for a handful of tasks.
  • Run on a small slice first to gauge token spend; set /config workflowSizeGuideline.
  • Save a good run as a command (.claude/workflows/) so the orchestration is repeatable.
  • Use a workflow when the task outgrows a handful of subagents or you want findings adversarially verified before you see them; save good runs as /commands.
  • Start agent teams with research/review tasks; keep teams 3-5, give each teammate its own files, and enforce quality with TeammateIdle/TaskCompleted hooks.
  • Run a workflow on a small slice first to gauge spend; watch the 'Large workflow' warning.
  • Note the orchestrator/overseer pattern and inter-agent comms are covered in depth by other research clusters; treat this entry as the feature map.
Where to learn (8 sources)
  • Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Introducing dynamic workflows in Claude Code Anthropic, 2026-05, blog
  • Dynamic Workflows for PMs: Orchestrate AI Agents in Claude Code Product Compass (Pawel Huryn), 2026, blog
  • Claude Code Dynamic Workflows: When to Fan Out Stride, 2026, blog
  • Best practices: Fan out across files (/batch, claude -p loops) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Orchestrate teams of Claude Code sessions (agent teams) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs

Names to know: Anthropic Claude Code team, Jarred Sumner (Bun), Pawel Huryn (Product Compass, PM-oriented)

15Task decomposition and file ownership for parallel agents

establishedSeed topic 2
Search fortask decomposition AI agentsdecompose by context boundariesfile ownership parallel agentsavoid file conflicts agent teamsWIP limits agentstask sizing subagentsvertical slice vs layer split

What it is. The unglamorous skill that decides whether parallel sessions help or hurt. Guidance converges across sources: split by context boundary (a feature plus its tests together; separate modules or layers apart), give each worker exclusive file ownership, size tasks as self-contained deliverables (a function, a test file, a review), keep 3-5 workers in flight, and put dependency order in a shared task list rather than in anyone's head. Anthropic's Research post found vague decomposition causes workers to duplicate each other; Cognition's Flappy Bird shows why shared visual/API decisions cannot be split.

Why it matters for you. His 'specialist per feature area' split is a good default; the refinement is to check every delegation for hidden shared decisions (schemas, APIs, UI conventions) and pull those up to the overseer or into a spec before workers start.

Best practices (4)
  • Before spawning, list the decisions each task will make; anything two tasks both decide becomes an overseer decision written to a file first.
  • Assign file/directory ownership explicitly in the task prompt; use worktrees when ownership can't be guaranteed.
  • Write tasks with objective, deliverable, verification command, and out-of-scope list.
  • Prefer 5-6 medium tasks per worker over one huge task; too-small tasks make coordination cost exceed the benefit.
Where to learn (5 sources)
  • Orchestrate teams of Claude Code sessions (Best practices: size tasks, avoid file conflicts) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • When to use multi-agent systems (decompose by context boundaries) Anthropic, 2026-01, blog
  • The Code Agent Orchestra (file ownership, WIP limits, kill criteria) Addy Osmani, 2026-03, blog
  • How we built our multi-agent research system (delegation lessons) Anthropic, 2025-06, blog
  • Multi-Agent Orchestration: a practical architecture without the buzzwords Augment Code, 2026, blog

Names to know: Addy Osmani, Anthropic, Walden Yan (Cognition)

16Verification loops and fresh-context adversarial review

establishedSeed topic 3
Search forgive Claude a way to verify its workadversarial review subagentwriter reviewer pattern/code-review fresh contextStop hook quality gate/goal conditionverification is the bottleneckreview plan not diff

What it is. Anthropic's top best practice and the orchestrator's real job. Give the agent a check it can run (tests, build, screenshot diff) and it closes its own loop; escalate from 'run the tests' in a prompt to /goal conditions, Stop hooks (deterministic gate), and a verification subagent that sees only the diff and criteria, not the reasoning that produced it. Boris Cherny: a feedback loop 2-3x's output quality. Osmani: the bottleneck has shifted from generation to verification. HumanLayer: review research and plans (small) rather than thousands of lines of diff; attach transcripts to PRs.

Why it matters for you. 'Running every feature past the overseer' is a verification loop done by hand in the most expensive window. Moving it to a fresh-context reviewer subagent or session, with the spec/plan as the rubric, keeps the overseer lean and catches more.

Best practices (4)
  • Every delegation ends with a runnable check and a demand for evidence (test output, command + result, screenshot).
  • Review with a fresh context: 'Use a subagent to review the diff against PLAN.md; report gaps that affect correctness, not style.'
  • Tell reviewers to flag only correctness/requirement gaps to avoid over-engineering from 'find something' bias.
  • Use Stop hooks or /goal for unattended runs; use TaskCompleted hooks as team quality gates.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Best practices: Give Claude a way to verify its work; Add an adversarial review step Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • 13 Practical Claude Code Tips from Its Creator, Boris Cherny MadAppGang (on Boris Cherny), 2026, blog
  • The Code Agent Orchestra ('the bottleneck has shifted') Addy Osmani, 2026-03, blog
  • How Claude Code is used in practice (people make ~70% of planning decisions, ~20% of execution) Anthropic research, 2026-06, paper

Names to know: Boris Cherny, Addy Osmani, Dex Horthy

17How comparable tools do it: Cursor, Codex CLI, Gemini CLI, OpenCode, Aider

establishedSeed topic 2
Search forCursor background agents parallel worktreesCodex CLI subagents AGENTS.mdGemini CLI subagents GEMINI.mdOpenCode primary agent subagentAider repo map architect modeAGENTS.md cross-toolagentmaxxing multi-CLI orchestration

What it is. The same primitives under different names. Cursor: Composer plus cloud/background agents (up to eight parallel jobs on separate worktrees), .cursor/rules/*.mdc glob-scoped rules, plan mode. Codex CLI: AGENTS.md instructions, subagent workflows, Codex Cloud async PRs, and OpenAI's harness-engineering write-up. Gemini CLI: subagents (markdown + frontmatter, parallel), GEMINI.md/AGENTS.md context files (reportedly being folded into Antigravity CLI in mid-2026). OpenCode: Build and Plan primary agents plus General/Explore/Scout subagents invoked by @mention. Aider: a PageRank-based repo map for cheap context, and architect mode (a planner model plus an editor model, i.e. planner/executor in one tool). AGENTS.md is the emerging cross-tool instruction file; Claude Code reads CLAUDE.md but can import AGENTS.md.

Why it matters for you. Lets him recognize the pattern when a YouTube lesson is Cursor- or Codex-flavored, and shows the ideas (rules files, plan mode, subagents, worktrees, planner/executor) are tool-independent.

Best practices (3)
  • Search for the concept name (subagents, worktrees, plan mode, rules file) plus the tool name; the workflow transfers.
  • If ever mixing tools, keep one AGENTS.md and import it from CLAUDE.md.
  • Aider's architect/editor split is a clean mental model for overseer (architect) vs specialist (editor).
