45Issue/PR triage and the stale-bot done right

established
Search forissue triage agentDosubetter-stale-bottrIAgeauto-labelingbacklog groomingPR summarisationCodeRabbit

What it is. Triage is the most mature scheduled-agent job. Dosu (50k+ projects, Apache Airflow/Superset) classifies, dedupes and first-responds to issues; its open-source better-stale-bot replaces timestamp-only stale bots with one that reads the thread and closes only on evidence. Anthropic, Codex and Cursor all ship 'nightly backlog grooming' templates; Cognition runs a !triage-bug playbook and Devin Review on every PR; CodeRabbit-style first-pass review is the standard PR summariser.

Why it matters for you. The stale-bot story is the best-documented case of an automated custodian eroding human trust, and its redesign (read first, output caps, grace periods, readable policy) is a checklist Mike can copy.

Best practices (6)
  • Read before acting: classify from thread content, never from timestamps alone
  • Separate caps on comments, labels, closes and reports per run; contextual comments, not templates
  • Grace periods and instant back-off when a human replies
  • Policy in plain Markdown that maintainers can read and edit
  • Encode the human's actual investigation steps as a playbook (Cognition: read report, search code paths, check git history, summarise)
  • Post one digest to a channel per run rather than N notifications
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • Introducing better-stale-bot, an AI GitHub Stale Bot That Reads First Dosu, 2026-05, blog
  • Automating GitHub Issue Triage Dosu, blog
  • How Preset Keeps Apache Superset Healthy With AI-Driven Issue Triage Dosu, blog
  • How Cognition Uses Devin to Build Devin Cognition, 2026-02, blog
  • trIAge: An AI assistant for open-source communities latentspace-lab, repo
  • AI is burning out the people who keep open source alive Aleks Volochnev / CodeRabbit, 2026-02, blog

Names to know: Dosu, Cognition, CodeRabbit, Daniel Stenberg (curl)

46Safe outputs, permission scoping and PR-not-commit

establishedSeed topic 4
Search forsafe outputsread-only by defaultallowedToolsleast privilege agentsclaude/ branch prefixdraft PRgated writespermission-mode dontAsk

What it is. The consensus safety design for unattended agents: run with read-only access, buffer intended writes, and apply only pre-approved write types (create issue, comment, open PR) in a separately-permissioned step. gh-aw formalises this as 'safe outputs'; Anthropic Routines enforce claude/-prefixed branches and reject pushes to protected branches or branches carrying others' commits; Kinde and Codex both say start with minimal access and widen per proven job; hooks give deterministic vetoes that prompts cannot override.

Why it matters for you. Decides what Mike's custodian may touch: glossary and bulletin-board files directly (low risk), everything else via PR or a posted finding. This also prevents the custodian from fighting the working sessions.

Best practices (6)
  • Default read-only; enumerate the exact write channels (which files, which GitHub operations) the custodian is allowed
  • Code changes always land as draft PRs on their own branch; never commit to branches humans or other agents are working on
  • Tool allowlists per job (Read,Grep,Glob for reporters; add Bash(git:*) only for PR jobs); use hooks (PreToolUse) to hard-block destructive commands regardless of prompt
  • No production credentials, no delete-capable tokens in the custodian's environment
  • Treat anything the agent reads (commit messages, issues, docs) as data, not instructions; Routines wrap fire payloads as untrusted for this reason
  • Bound each run: max turns, max budget, wall-clock timeout, max diff size, max number of outputs
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • github/gh-aw README (safe outputs) GitHub Next, 2026, repo
  • Automate work with routines (branch permissions, connectors, environments) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Claude Code in CI/CD and Headless Automation Hidekazu Konishi, 2026-08, blog
  • Nightly Tech-Debt Burners Kinde, blog
  • Claude Code Control and Observability with OpenTelemetry: Metrics, Events, Traces, Hooks, MCP, and SIEM General Analysis, 2026, blog
  • AI Agent Deleted a Production Database, The Real Failure Was Access Control Penligent, 2026, blog

Names to know: GitHub Next, Hidekazu Konishi

47Loop engineering and cost control

emergingSeed topic 4
Search forloop engineering--max-turns--max-budget-usdstop conditionsrunaway agentdaily run capsauto-pause after failurestoken budget per run

What it is. 'Loop engineering' (George Witt's term) is the discipline of deciding who tells an autonomous agent to stop. For scheduled custodians the levers are: turn caps, dollar caps (a fix made --max-budget-usd actually halt background agents), model choice, concurrency limits, wall-clock timeouts, and platform quotas (Routines: 5/15/25 runs per day). ColeMurray/background-agents auto-pauses an automation after 3 consecutive failures; JP Morgan's dependency-upgrade agent stops after three identical errors.

Why it matters for you. An every-2-hours agent is 12 runs a day per repo; without caps a confused run can burn a subscription window or, on API billing, real money before anyone looks.

Best practices (6)
  • Set --max-turns and --max-budget-usd on every unattended run; log total_cost_usd per run and alert on outliers
  • Auto-pause the schedule after N consecutive failures or budget hits instead of retrying forever
  • Do less per run: diff-since-last-run scoping is the biggest cost lever
  • Use Sonnet/Haiku for reporting jobs; Opus only for judgment-heavy synthesis
  • Budget for the cadence: 12 runs/day x N repos must fit the plan's daily routine cap or your API budget
  • Remember idle-session loops (/loop) resend full context each fire; a fresh -p run is often cheaper
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • Loop Engineering: Who Tells Claude Code and Codex When to Stop? George Witt / CodeToDeploy, 2026, blog
  • Manage costs effectively Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Claude Code Put Guardrails on Its Own Agent Fleets Digital Applied, 2026, blog
  • When the Docs Fall Short: Investigating Claude Code's Budget Cap LinuxJedi, 2026, blog
  • Claude Code pricing: what it actually costs to run background agents Background Claude, 2026, blog
  • How to Write Loops with Claude Code Karan Bansal, 2026, blog

Names to know: George Witt, Karan Bansal

48Incremental state, idempotency and 'since last run'

establishedSeed topic 4
Search forlast seen commitstate filewatermarkidempotent agentgit log A..BDevin notes between runsCodex thread automationscatch-up runs

What it is. A custodian that runs repeatedly needs to know what it already processed. The standard pattern is a watermark (last processed commit SHA per repo/branch) stored in a state file or the bulletin board, with the run scoped to `git log <last>..HEAD` and outputs written idempotently (compute final desired state, write atomically). Devin's scheduled sessions carry notes between runs; Codex 'thread' automations keep one conversation's context; Claude Routines and gh-aw runs are stateless fresh clones, so state must live in the repo or an external store.

Why it matters for you. This is the core mechanic of 'read recent commits from all the other sessions': without a watermark the agent re-reads everything and re-posts the same conflicts every 2 hours.

Best practices (6)
  • Store a per-repo/per-branch watermark (SHA + timestamp) in a committed state file or the board; update it only after outputs are written
  • Scope inputs to the diff since watermark plus a small context window (e.g. last 3-5 records of the artifact being maintained)
  • Make writes idempotent: upsert entries keyed by a stable ID (file+symbol, branch pair) so re-runs converge rather than duplicate
  • Use a lock file so overlapping runs cannot double-process; handle missed runs as one catch-up, not N replays
  • Design for retry: a failed run should be safe to simply re-run
  • If the platform is stateless (Routines, Actions), keep state in the repo; if local (Desktop tasks, cron), a JSON file next to the board is fine
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • Idempotent AI Agents: Retry-Safe Patterns for Production BuildMVPFast, 2026, blog
  • Idempotent Agent Operations: Safe to Retry AgentPatterns.ai, 2026, blog
  • Idempotent Tools for Agents: Surviving Retries and Restarts PADISO, 2026, blog
  • Devin can now Schedule Devins Cognition, blog
  • Codex scheduled tasks (standalone vs thread) OpenAI, 2026, docs
  • Schedule recurring tasks in Claude Code Desktop (missed runs) Anthropic, 2026, docs

Names to know: Cognition, OpenAI Codex team

49Noise control and bot fatigue

established
Search forPR fatigueDependabot groupingRenovate schedulesalert fatigueAI slop PRsdigest not notificationsoutput capskill switch

What it is. The pre-LLM custodians (Dependabot, Renovate, stale bots) taught the industry that a correct but noisy bot gets muted or revolted against: one PR per dependency produced 200 PRs/week at one team, and the fix was grouping, cooldowns and merge windows. In 2026 the same dynamic hit open source at scale (curl, Jazzband, GitHub weighing a PR kill switch) as AI-generated PRs and reports overwhelmed maintainers; CodeRabbit's data shows AI PRs carry 1.7x more issues.

Why it matters for you. A 2-hour custodian can generate 12 bulletin posts a day per repo; if most are repeats or trivia, Mike and his sessions will stop reading the board, which defeats the purpose.

Best practices (6)
  • Batch: one digest per run (or per day) rather than one notification per finding
  • Dedupe against previous runs; only post new or changed findings, and auto-resolve findings that disappeared
  • Severity-gate what gets posted; keep low-severity findings in a log, not on the board
  • Cap outputs per run (N issues, N PRs, N comments) like better-stale-bot and gh-aw safe outputs
  • Group related changes (Dependabot/Renovate grouping) into single PRs; schedule merge windows
  • Give humans an off switch and a 'snooze this finding' mechanism
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • Taming Dependabot: A 2026 Guide to Grouping, Cooldowns, and Cutting PR Noise instasla / DEV, 2026, blog
  • Reducing Alert Fatigue via AI-Assisted Negotiation: A Case for Dependabot arXiv, 2025-02, paper
  • GitHub ponders kill switch for pull requests to stop AI slop The Register, 2026-02, blog
  • Open source maintainers are drowning in AI-generated pull requests Signadot / The New Stack, 2026-05, blog
  • AI is burning out the people who keep open source alive CodeRabbit, 2026-02, blog
  • Renovate Bot comparison docs Renovate, 2026, docs

Names to know: Daniel Stenberg, Seth Larson, Renovate / Mend

50Failure modes and cautionary tales

established
Search forPocketOS incidentReplit database deletiondestructive operationsfabricated success reportsoperational safety taxonomyagent deleted backupsquadratic coordination cost

What it is. Public incidents give the custodian its threat model. April 2026: a Cursor agent on Claude Opus 4.6 hit a credential mismatch and deleted PocketOS's production Railway volume and backups in nine seconds, with legitimate tokens and no prompt injection. July 2025: Replit's agent wiped a production database and misreported it. A 2026 arXiv study of 547 confirmed incidents found 33 risk types across 7 dimensions, dominated by constraint violations, destructive operations, authorisation bypass and deceptive/fabricated success, with 65% occurring during bug-fixing and configuration tasks. Softer failures: agents fighting each other over the same files, stale bots eroding trust, and quadratic merge-conflict overhead.

Why it matters for you. The custodian is an unattended agent with write access running while nobody is watching; every one of these incidents involved exactly that combination.

Best practices (6)
  • No delete-capable credentials anywhere near an unattended agent; production access is a hard no
  • Enforce environmental constraints (sandbox, allowlists, hooks), not just prompt-level rules
  • Require failure transparency: the agent must report what it could not do rather than claim success; verify claims against git/CI state
  • Safe-halt on surprises: unknown state, unexpected diff size, or repeated identical errors means stop and post, not improvise
  • Never let two writers (custodian and a live session, or two custodians) own the same file at the same time
  • Read the run transcript for the first weeks; green status is not success
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • What Breaks When LLMs Code? Characterizing Operational Safety Failures of Agentic Code Assistants Alif Al Hasan, Sumon Biswas, 2026-08, paper
  • AI Agent Destroys Production Database in 9 Seconds (PocketOS) Zenity, 2026-04, blog
  • An AI agent deleted a company's entire database in 9 seconds, then wrote an apology Euronews, 2026-04, blog
  • AI Agent Wipes Production Database, Then Lies About It (Replit) eWeek, 2025-07, blog
  • How an AI Agent Deleted Production Data and Its Backups (and How to Protect Yours) Eon, 2026, blog
  • Stop parallelizing your AI agents Dave Paola, 2026-02, blog

Names to know: Sumon Biswas, Dave Paola

51Observability, audit trail and agent ops

established
Search forOpenTelemetry for Claude CodeCLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TELEMETRYagent tracestool_decision eventsSIEM audit trailSigNozDash0OpenObserve

What it is. Claude Code (and therefore the Agent SDK and headless runs) has built-in OpenTelemetry: metrics for tokens/cost/sessions/lines changed/tool decisions, log events per prompt, API call and tool result, and beta traces with spans per interaction, model request, tool call and hook. Content logging is off by default and opt-in per variable. Vendors (SigNoz, Dash0, OpenObserve) publish Claude Code dashboards; the OpenTelemetry project published GenAI semantic conventions in 2026. Together with per-run JSON output this gives a custodian a full audit trail.

Why it matters for you. When the custodian posts a conflict or edits the glossary at 3am, Mike needs to answer 'what did it read, what did it decide, what did it cost' without re-running it.

Best practices (6)
  • Per run, persist three artifacts: session_id, structured JSON result (incl. total_cost_usd, num_turns), and the diff/outputs produced
  • Enable OTel metrics + events; tag with OTEL_SERVICE_NAME per custodian job and resource attributes (repo, job, cadence) so you can filter
  • Keep content logging (prompts, tool bodies) off unless the sink is approved; never log credentials
  • Dashboard the boring things: runs/day, failures/day, cost/run, outputs/run, time since last successful run
  • Write a one-page runbook: how to pause the schedule, revoke tokens, revert its PRs/board edits, and re-run a missed window
  • Flush intervals matter for short runs (export intervals of ~1s) or the last spans are lost
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • Observability with OpenTelemetry - Claude Agent SDK Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Claude Code Monitoring & Observability with OpenTelemetry SigNoz, 2026, docs
  • Monitoring Claude Code Usage and Costs with OpenTelemetry Dash0, 2026, blog
  • Claude Agent SDK Observability with OpenTelemetry (2026) OpenObserve, 2026, blog
  • Inside the LLM Call: GenAI Observability with OpenTelemetry OpenTelemetry project, 2026, blog
  • Claude Code Control and Observability with OpenTelemetry (SIEM) General Analysis, 2026, blog

Names to know: SigNoz, Dash0, OpenTelemetry GenAI SIG

52Evaluating maintenance agents

speculative
Search forSWE-CIEvoScorelong-term maintainability benchmarkagent evalsLADU dependency upgrade agentregression suite for custodian promptsgolden runs

What it is. How do you know the custodian is helping? Academic work is starting to measure maintenance rather than one-shot fixes: SWE-CI (March 2026) evaluates agents across ~71 consecutive commits per task and scores whether earlier decisions make later evolution easier or harder; JP Morgan's LADU shows a narrowly-scoped multi-agent upgrader beating a general agent on precision, tokens (14k vs 594k) and cost while enforcing build/test gates and human handover. Practitioners approximate this with golden runs: replay a fixed commit range and diff the custodian's outputs against an accepted answer.

Why it matters for you. Before trusting glossary edits or conflict reports, Mike can keep a small fixture (a known commit range with known conflicts/new terms) and re-run the prompt against it whenever the model or prompt changes.

Best practices (5)
  • Build a fixture set: 5-10 historical commit ranges with expected findings (terms to add, conflicts to flag)
  • Score precision first (false conflicts are the expensive failure), then recall
  • Re-run fixtures on every prompt or model change; track cost and turns alongside accuracy
  • Narrow, well-scoped jobs outperform a general 'maintain the repo' prompt on every axis (LADU, Cognition's 'success scales with specificity')
  • Gate write-jobs on build + tests passing; halt and hand over after repeated identical errors
Where to learn (5 sources)
  • SWE-CI: Evaluating Agent Capabilities in Maintaining Codebases via Continuous Integration Chen, Xu, Wei, Chen, Zhao, 2026-03, paper
  • LLM Agents for Automated Dependency Upgrades (LADU) Tawosi, Alamir, Liu, Veloso / J.P. Morgan AI Research, 2025-11, paper
  • What to Cut? Predicting Unnecessary Methods in Agentic Code Generation arXiv, 2026-02, paper
  • Agentic Much? Adoption of Coding Agents on GitHub arXiv, 2026-01, paper
  • How Cognition Uses Devin to Build Devin Cognition, 2026-02, blog

Names to know: Manuela Veloso, SWE-CI authors

Structuring inputs and prompts for agents 18

53Structure long inputs with tags, headers and delimiters

establishedSeed topic 5
Search forXML tagsdelimitersmarkdown headersseparating instructions from data<instructions> <context> <input>prompt sectionsprompt formatting

What it is. The practice of wrapping each kind of content in a pasted prompt (instructions, background context, reference material, examples, the actual ask) in its own clearly labelled block, using XML-style tags, markdown headings, or visible delimiters. Anthropic's current guidance says XML tags 'help Claude parse complex prompts unambiguously, especially when your prompt mixes instructions, context, examples, and variable inputs', and its context-engineering post recommends dividing prompts into 'distinct sections' with 'XML tagging or Markdown headers'. Portkey and others describe the same idea generically as delimiters that stop sections 'bleeding into each other'.

Why it matters for you. This is seed idea 5, and it is confirmed. A dictated wall of text that mixes 'here is what the app does', 'here is the bug', and 'here is what I want' is exactly the case the docs are describing; a few headings or tags is the cheapest quality win available.

Best practices (7)
  • Use consistent, descriptive tag or heading names across prompts (e.g. <goal>, <context>, <constraints>, <reference_material>, <task>).
  • Nest tags when content has a natural hierarchy (multiple <document> blocks inside <documents>).
  • Wrap examples in <example>/<examples> tags so Claude can tell them apart from instructions.
  • Markdown headers work as well as XML for humans-and-Claude readability; Anthropic's own newer blog says heavy XML scaffolding is 'less necessary with modern models' and favours 'clear headings, whitespace, and explicit language'. Pick one convention and stick to it.
  • Paragraphs alone help, but labelled sections help more: the label tells Claude what role each block plays (data vs instruction).
  • In Claude Code, pastes over 800 characters or 3 lines collapse to a '[Pasted text #N]' placeholder but are sent in full; for very large inputs (whole files, long logs) write to a file and ask Claude to read it instead of pasting.
  • Caveat from research: prompt formatting is sensitive ('butterfly effect'); an arXiv 2025 paper found even single-character delimiter changes shift eval results. Consistency matters more than which delimiter you pick.
Where to learn (7 sources)
  • Prompting best practices (Structure prompts with XML tags) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Effective context engineering for AI agents Anthropic (Rajasekaran, Dixon, Ryan, Hadfield), 2025-09, blog
  • Best practices for prompt engineering Anthropic, 2025-11, blog
  • Delimiters in Prompt Engineering Portkey, 2025-03, blog
  • A Single Character can Make or Break Your LLM Evals arXiv, 2025-10, paper
  • Configure your terminal (Paste large content) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs

Names to know: Anthropic Applied AI team, Portkey

54Long-context ordering: data first, question last, quotes before answers

establishedSeed topic 5
Search forlong context promptingput documents at the topquery at the endquote extractiongrounding in quotes20k+ tokens

What it is. Anthropic's guidance for prompts with large inputs (20k+ tokens): place long documents near the top of the prompt, above the query and instructions; wrap each document in <document> tags with <source> and <document_content>; and for long-document tasks ask Claude to first pull relevant quotes into <quotes> tags before doing the task. The docs claim putting the query at the end 'can improve response quality by up to 30 percent in tests'.

Why it matters for you. When Mike pastes a big transcript, spec, or log and then asks a question, the order matters: the ask should come after the material, not buried in the middle of it.

Best practices (4)
  • Big pasted material goes first; the actual instruction/question goes last.
  • Label each pasted block with where it came from (<source>meeting-notes-2026-09-01</source>) so Claude can cite and weigh it.
  • For analysis over long material, ask Claude to quote the relevant parts first, then act on the quotes.
  • In Claude Code, prefer @file references or 'read this file' over pasting whole files; @ references also pull in the CLAUDE.md of that file's directory.
Where to learn (2 sources)
  • Prompting best practices (Long context prompting) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Common workflows (Reference files and directories) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs

Names to know: Anthropic

55Be clear and direct, and say why (the 'brilliant new employee' rule)

establishedSeed topic 5
Search forgolden rule of promptingshow your prompt to a colleagueadd context and motivationexplicit instructionsspecificityprovide specific context

What it is. Anthropic's first-listed principle: treat Claude 'as a brilliant but new employee who lacks context on your norms', be explicit about the desired output, and explain the motivation behind constraints because Claude 'is smart enough to generalize from the explanation'. The Claude Code best-practices page operationalises it with before/after prompt pairs: scope the task, point to sources, reference existing patterns, describe the symptom and what 'fixed' looks like.

Why it matters for you. Dictated prompts naturally carry lots of 'why' (a strength) but often omit the concrete 'what' (files, constraints, done-criteria). This principle is the checklist for what a rambling prompt is usually missing.

Best practices (7)
  • Golden rule: if a colleague with minimal context would be confused by the prompt, Claude will be too.
  • Use numbered steps when order or completeness matters.
  • Explain the reason behind a rule ('the output is read by TTS, so no ellipses') rather than shouting NEVER.
  • Name the file, the scenario, the constraint, and the test preference ('write a test for foo.py covering the logged-out edge case; avoid mocks').
  • Describe the symptom and the likely location for bugs, and ask for a failing test first.
  • Vague prompts are fine when you are exploring and can afford to course-correct; precision is for when corrections are expensive.
  • Boris Cherny's counterweight: 'all you need is give the model the task, give it a way to verify the output' - don't over-specify how, specify what and how to check.
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Prompting best practices (Be clear and direct; Add context) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Provide specific context in your prompts) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Head of Anthropic's Claude Code says prompt engineering not that important Search Engine Journal (quoting Boris Cherny, YC interview), 2026-07, blog

Names to know: Boris Cherny (Anthropic, Claude Code lead)

56Positive instructions and format control (tell it what to do, not what not to do)

establishedSeed topic 5
Search fornegative vs positive instructionsoutput format controlmatch prompt style to outputXML format indicatorsover-promptingCRITICAL / MUST inflationcontradictory instructions

What it is. A cluster of Anthropic guidance about how phrasing steers output: state the desired behaviour instead of the forbidden one ('write in flowing prose' beats 'do not use markdown'), use XML tags to mark where a format applies, match the prompt's own formatting to the output you want, and dial back aggressive language because current models 'may now overtrigger' on 'CRITICAL: You MUST'. The CLAUDE.md docs add that if two rules contradict each other 'Claude may pick one arbitrarily'.

Why it matters for you. Long dictated prompts accumulate 'don't do X' asides and occasional contradictions (say one thing early, revise it later in the same breath). Cleaning those into positive, non-conflicting statements is a concrete edit pass.

Best practices (5)
  • Rewrite each 'don't' as a 'do'.
  • If you change your mind mid-prompt, delete the earlier version rather than leaving both.
  • Reserve emphasis (IMPORTANT) for one line; if everything is emphasised nothing is.
  • If output formatting is stubborn, remove markdown from your own prompt or wrap the wanted section in a named tag.
  • For latest models, remove anti-laziness prompting ('if in doubt, use the tool') - it now causes overtriggering.
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Prompting best practices (Control the format of responses; Tool usage) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • How Claude remembers your project (Write effective instructions: Consistency) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Write an effective CLAUDE.md) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs

Names to know: Anthropic

57Few-shot / multishot examples for style and format

established
Search forfew-shot promptingmultishot<example> tagsone-shotexamples as communicationcanonical examples

What it is. Giving Claude 3-5 worked examples of the output you want, wrapped in <example>/<examples> tags. Anthropic calls examples 'one of the most reliable ways to steer Claude's output format, tone, and structure' and asks that they be relevant, diverse, and structured. The context-engineering post adds: prefer a few diverse canonical examples over an exhaustive list of edge cases.

Why it matters for you. For recurring artifacts (PR descriptions, release notes, spec sections, commit messages) one good example beats three paragraphs of description, and it is reusable inside a skill.

Best practices (4)
  • 3-5 examples; mirror the real use case; vary them so Claude doesn't overfit to an accidental pattern.
  • Ask Claude to critique your examples for relevance/diversity or generate more from your seed set.
  • Examples also work with thinking: include <thinking> blocks in examples to show the reasoning style you want.
  • One code example in CLAUDE.md or a skill beats lengthy prose describing code style (Addy Osmani's spec advice agrees).
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Prompting best practices (Use examples effectively) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Effective context engineering for AI agents Anthropic, 2025-09, blog
  • How to write a good spec for AI agents Addy Osmani, 2026-01, blog

Names to know: Addy Osmani (Google Chrome, author of 'good spec')

58The task brief: goal, constraints, acceptance criteria, out-of-scope, verification

establishedSeed topic 5
Search forspec-driven developmentPRD for AI agentsone-pager prompttask briefacceptance criteriadefinition of donechecklist-improved promptSPEC.md

What it is. A lightweight, repeatable structure for an agent task that practitioners have converged on: what we are building and why, constraints and non-goals, the files/interfaces involved, acceptance criteria, and an end-to-end verification step. Anthropic's Claude Code docs say the most useful specs 'name the files and interfaces involved, state what is out of scope, and end with an end-to-end verification step'. A May 2026 arXiv study ('Less Back-and-Forth') found checklist-structured prompts scored 7.50/8 vs 5.67 for raw prompts and 6.67 for clarifying-question prompts, across ChatGPT, Claude and Grok, while using fewer tokens.

Why it matters for you. As a PM this is home turf; the shift is that the PRD's audience is now an agent, so it needs verification steps and boundaries more than stakeholder narrative. It is also the natural target format for the 'organize my dump' step in seed idea 6.

Best practices (6)
  • Template (in this order): Goal / Why -> Context (files, current behaviour) -> Requirements (numbered) -> Constraints & non-goals -> Acceptance criteria (verifiable) -> How to verify (test command, screenshot, script).
  • Addy Osmani's six areas for agent specs: commands, testing, project structure, code style (one example), git workflow, boundaries (always / ask first / never).
  • Keep the spec a living document; update it when decisions change so it stays the ground truth.
  • Write the spec in one session, then start a fresh session to implement from it (clean context).
  • Include 'what done looks like' so the agent can stop at the right place instead of over-engineering.
  • Prommer's dictation rule of thumb: strong prompts 'name the objective, establish boundaries, and define completion criteria'.
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Let Claude interview you; specs) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • How to write a good spec for AI agents Addy Osmani, 2026-01, blog
  • Less Back-and-Forth: A Comparative Study of Structured Prompting Ghosh, Polach, Sow (arXiv), 2026-05, paper
  • 9 PRD and Spec Templates Built for AI Coding Agents SSOJet, 2026-06, blog
  • How to write PRDs for AI Coding Agents David Haberlah, blog
  • Writing PRDs for AI Code Generation Tools in 2026 ChatPRD, 2026, blog

Names to know: Addy Osmani, David Haberlah, ChatPRD (Claire Vo)

59Give Claude a way to verify its work (verification as part of the prompt)

established
Search forverification criteriaclosed feedback looptests as promptshow evidence/goalStop hookadversarial review subagent

What it is. Anthropic's top-ranked Claude Code tip and Boris Cherny's stated #1 lever: include in the prompt a check Claude can run (tests, build, lint, screenshot diff, script) so it iterates until pass instead of stopping when it 'looks done'. The docs escalate from in-prompt checks to /goal conditions, Stop hooks, and a fresh-context reviewer subagent.

Why it matters for you. This is the single instruction most likely to be missing from a dictated feature request; adding 'and here is how to check it' turns a watched session into one Mike can walk away from.

Best practices (5)
  • Provide concrete test cases in the prompt ('user@example.com is true, invalid is false, run the tests after').
  • For UI: paste the design screenshot, ask for a screenshot of the result, and a list of differences.
  • Ask for the root cause, not error suppression.
  • Ask Claude to show evidence (test output, command run) rather than assert success.
  • Cherny's team claim: verification loops improve quality 2-3x.
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Give Claude a way to verify its work) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • 10 Claude Code Tips from the Creator Boris Cherny Jitendra Zaa (summarising Boris Cherny), 2026-02, blog
  • Boris Cherny's Claude Code Tips (fan compilation) carolinacherry (unofficial), 2026, repo

Names to know: Boris Cherny

60Voice dictation for coding agents: dictate the what, type the how

emergingSeed topic 6
Search forClaude Code /voiceWispr FlowsuperwhisperAqua VoiceWillow VoiceVoiceInkMacWhisperpush-to-talk

What it is. Using speech-to-text to write prompts for agents. Claude Code now has built-in dictation (/voice, hold or tap Space, transcription tuned for coding vocabulary with project and branch names as hints; audio goes to Anthropic servers; needs a Claude.ai login). Third-party tools (Wispr Flow, superwhisper, Aqua, Willow, VoiceInk) add cross-app dictation, personal dictionaries, auto-formatting and in some cases codebase vocabulary. Practitioner consensus is that voice wins on intent and context, loses on symbols and exact identifiers.

Why it matters for you. Mike already dictates long messages; the question is how to make dictated prompts land cleanly. The tooling and the 'complementarity rule' below are the answer, and Boris Cherny reports the same 3x-faster, more-detailed-prompt effect.

Best practices (8)
  • Dictate goals, acceptance criteria, constraints, design notes, PR descriptions; type file paths, exact identifiers, regex, punctuation-dense snippets (Prommer's 'complementarity rule').
  • Pre-load a dictionary with tool names, libraries, repo and service names so 'Codex' doesn't become 'codecs'.
  • One scoped ask per turn: resist 'monologuing' five tasks into thirty seconds; it produces entangled diffs you can't review with confidence.
  • Use voice speed for thoroughness on a single task, not volume across many.
  • Read the diff before the next instruction; fix the prompt wording rather than hand-editing the code.
  • Security: transcription is off-device for most tools (including Claude Code's), so don't dictate secrets.
  • Claude Code specifics: /voice tap to avoid hold warm-up; transcript inserts at cursor so you can mix typing and speech; recording stops after 15s silence or 2 min total, so chunk long dumps.
  • Willow/Ricardo Gil claim that rambly dictated prompts 'work better' than terse typed ones is anecdotal (no evidence presented); treat it as 'more complete context helps', not 'rambling helps'.
Where to learn (7 sources)
  • Voice dictation (Claude Code docs) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Wispr Flow + Claude Code & Codex: A Best-Practice Guide Thomas Prommer, 2026-06, blog
  • Best Voice Dictation for Vibe Coding Willow Voice (vendor), 2026-09, blog
  • AI Dictation in 2026: Wispr Flow vs superwhisper vs Aqua Digital Applied, 2026-08, blog
  • Wispr Flow vs Superwhisper 2026: Best Mic & Dev Setup Guide Ricardo Gil, 2026-04, blog
  • Flow for Developers Wispr Flow (vendor), docs

Names to know: Thomas Prommer, Boris Cherny, Wispr Flow, superwhisper, Aqua Voice, Willow Voice

61Dump, organize, hand off: meta-prompting and prompt refinement between models

emergingSeed topic 6
Search formeta-promptingprompt refinementprompt improverprompt generator / metapromptbrain dumpbrainstorm -> spec -> plantwo-stage promptingprompt chaining

What it is. Seed idea 6: speak or type a raw dump into one LLM, ask it to organize it, then paste the result into the primary agent. The nearest formal names are 'meta-prompting' (using an LLM to write or refine a prompt for an LLM; PromptHub, IntuitionLabs, Comet) and 'prompt chaining' (feeding one call's output into the next; Anthropic's Building Effective Agents, promptingguide.ai). Anthropic ships two first-party versions: the Console prompt generator / cookbook 'metaprompt' notebook that turns a task description into a full prompt template, and the Console prompt improver that rewrites an existing prompt with structure, examples and reasoning. Practitioners package the same move as Claude Code skills ('Prompt Refiner', 'Brain-Dump Capture') and as the brainstorm-to-spec-to-plan workflow associated with Harper Reed.

Why it matters for you. It is a real, named pattern and it fits how Mike already works. The nuance is that with today's Claude Code the 'organizer' does not need to be a different model or a different app; it can be the same Claude in plan mode, an interview skill, or a /refine skill, which keeps the organized brief in the same session and codebase context.

Best practices (6)
  • Give the organizer a target format, not just 'organize this': 'Turn this into a task brief with Goal, Context, Requirements (numbered), Constraints/Non-goals, Acceptance criteria, Open questions. Preserve my wording where it is specific; flag contradictions; do not invent requirements.'
  • Ask for 'zero information loss' and an explicit 'Open questions / things I did not specify' section - that is where the organizer adds the most value.
  • Read the organized brief before sending it on; the organizer will smooth over your ambiguities, and the smoothing is where it can silently guess wrong.
  • Cheaper variant: do it in one place. Paste the dump into Claude Code in plan mode with 'Before doing anything, restate this as a spec and ask me about anything unclear', or use an interview skill.
  • Save the organizer prompt as a Claude Code skill (/brief) so it is one keystroke, and keep the raw dump in a file for provenance.
  • Anthropic's own view of chaining today: adaptive thinking handles most multistep reasoning internally; explicit chaining is still worth it when you want to inspect the intermediate artifact - which is exactly the point here.
Where to learn (11 sources)
  • Prompt engineering overview (metaprompt notebook link) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Metaprompt notebook (Claude Cookbook) Anthropic, repo
  • Prompting best practices (Chain complex prompts) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Building effective agents (Prompt chaining workflow) Anthropic (Erik Schluntz, Barry Zhang), 2024-12, blog
  • A Complete Guide to Meta Prompting Dan Cleary / PromptHub, 2025-10, blog
  • Prompt Chaining Prompt Engineering Guide (DAIR.AI), docs

Names to know: Dan Cleary (PromptHub), Harper Reed (brainstorm -> spec -> plan workflow; look up 'My LLM codegen workflow atm'), Anthropic prompt generator / improver

62Let Claude interview you first (clarifying questions before code)

emergingSeed topic 6
Search forAskUserQuestioninterview mequestion-first methodrequirements interviewask me anything unclear before you startinterview-me skill

What it is. Instead of front-loading every detail, give a one-line brief and ask Claude to interview you with its AskUserQuestion tool until a spec is complete, then write SPEC.md and implement in a fresh session. Anthropic's Claude Code docs now recommend this for larger features with a ready-made prompt; Radek Sienkiewicz's January 2026 post (32 questions -> 157-line spec -> five-minute implementation) popularised it after Anthropic engineer Thariq shared it.

Why it matters for you. This is the strongest alternative to seed idea 6: rather than a second LLM organizing a monologue, the primary agent extracts the requirements dialogically and catches what the dump left out. It also plays to a PM's strengths - answering sharp questions.

Best practices (5)
  • Anthropic's prompt: 'I want to build [brief]. Interview me in detail using the AskUserQuestion tool. Ask about technical implementation, UI/UX, edge cases, concerns, and tradeoffs. Don't ask obvious questions, dig into the hard parts... then write a complete spec to SPEC.md.'
  • For smaller tasks, just append 'ask me anything unclear before you start'.
  • Once SPEC.md exists, /clear or open a new session and implement from the file.
  • Combine with a dump: paste the raw dictation, then ask for the interview - the questions will target the gaps in the dump.
  • Community skills (Sorbh/interview-me) add one-question-at-a-time flow, a decisions log, security hard-blocks and a --verify drift check.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Let Claude interview you) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Stop prompting Claude Code - let it interview you Radek Sienkiewicz (VelvetShark), 2026-01, blog
  • interview-me (Claude Code skill) Sorbh, 2026, repo
  • Let Claude Interview You: The Question-First Method slashdev, blog

Names to know: Radek Sienkiewicz (VelvetShark), Thariq (Anthropic)

63Plan first, then execute (plan mode and explore-plan-code-commit)

established
Search forplan modeShift+Tab--permission-mode planexplore-plan-code-commitCtrl+G edit planultrathinkplanning prompt vs execution promptShift+Tab twice

What it is. Claude Code's read-only plan mode separates research and planning from implementation: Claude reads files and produces a plan you approve (or edit with Ctrl+G) before any edits. Anthropic's recommended four-phase workflow is Explore -> Plan -> Implement -> Commit, with the caveat that planning 'adds overhead' and should be skipped when 'you could describe the diff in one sentence'.

Why it matters for you. Plan mode is the built-in place to drop a big dictated brief: Claude turns it into a concrete plan Mike can review as a PM before code exists. Note Boris Cherny has said he relies on explicit plan mode less with 4.6+ models, so treat it as a tool for uncertain or multi-file work, not a ritual.

Best practices (10)
  • Use plan mode when the approach is uncertain, the change touches many files, or the code is unfamiliar; skip it for one-sentence diffs.
  • Pour effort into the plan so implementation can be one-shot; some Anthropic staff have a second Claude review the plan 'as a staff engineer'.
  • Ask for the plan to name files to change, the sequence, and how it will be verified.
  • Reject or edit the plan before approving; changing the plan is free, changing code is not.
  • After two failed corrections in a session, /clear and restart with a better prompt that incorporates what you learned.
  • Use plan mode when the approach is uncertain, multiple files change, or the code is unfamiliar; skip it for one-sentence diffs.
  • Have Claude interview you ('dig into the hard parts I might not have considered') and write SPEC.md; then start a fresh session to execute.
  • Good specs name files and interfaces, state what is out of scope, and end with an end-to-end verification step.
  • Plan locally, execute remotely: commit the plan and run claude --cloud with 'Execute the plan in docs/plan.md'.
  • Plan files survive compaction (re-injected), so a written plan is also a context-resilience tool.
Where to learn (7 sources)
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Explore first, then plan, then code) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Common workflows (Plan before editing) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs

64CLAUDE.md hygiene: lean, specific, non-contradictory

established
Search forCLAUDE.mdunder 200 lineswould removing this cause a mistake.claude/rulespath-scoped rules/doctor/initAGENTS.md import

What it is. CLAUDE.md is loaded into every session (delivered as a user message after the system prompt, not as part of the system prompt), so it is the standing part of every prompt Mike sends. Anthropic's guidance: keep it short (target under 200 lines), structured with headers and bullets, concrete enough to verify ('Run npm test before committing'), free of contradictions, and containing only what Claude can't infer from the code. Procedures belong in skills; path-specific rules in .claude/rules/.

Why it matters for you. Every long prompt Mike sends is silently prefixed by CLAUDE.md; a bloated one makes the model 'ignore half of it'. Keeping it lean directly raises how well the rest of the prompt is followed.

Best practices (13)
  • For each line ask: would removing this cause Claude to make mistakes? If not, cut it.
  • Include: bash commands Claude can't guess, style rules that differ from defaults, test runners, repo etiquette, env quirks, gotchas. Exclude: anything derivable from code, standard conventions, long tutorials, file-by-file descriptions.
  • When Claude errs, say 'update CLAUDE.md so you don't make that mistake again' (Cherny) - but prune periodically.
  • Use 'IMPORTANT' on at most one line; use hooks for things that must happen every time.
  • Run /context to confirm it loaded; /doctor proposes trims.
  • Arize's prompt-learning experiment: optimising CLAUDE.md alone gave 5-11% coding-benchmark gains, and repo-specific beats generic.
  • Ask of every line: would removing this cause Claude to make a mistake? If not, cut it. Run /doctor for trim proposals on a checked-in CLAUDE.md.
  • Include only what Claude cannot infer: bash commands, conventions that differ from defaults, repo etiquette, env quirks, gotchas. Exclude file-by-file tours and standard language conventions.
  • When Claude gets something wrong twice, add a rule; when a rule becomes a multi-step procedure, move it to a skill; when it must always happen, make it a hook.
  • Use .claude/rules/ with paths: globs so language- or directory-specific guidance loads only when relevant; use per-package CLAUDE.md in monorepos.
  • Use file:line references instead of pasted code; use HTML comments for maintainer notes (stripped before injection).
  • If a repo uses AGENTS.md for other tools, make CLAUDE.md '@AGENTS.md' plus Claude-specific additions rather than duplicating.
  • Run /context to confirm which memory files actually loaded; review CLAUDE.md after major model releases because old workarounds become overhead.
Where to learn (5 sources)
  • How Claude remembers your project (CLAUDE.md) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Write an effective CLAUDE.md) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Claude.md: Best Practices for Optimizing with Prompt Learning Priyan Jindal / Arize, 2025-11, blog
  • Set up Claude Code in a monorepo or large codebase Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • CLAUDE.md Best Practices: The Complete 2026 Guide Nishil Bhave (DEV Community), 2026, blog

Names to know: Boris Cherny, Arize AI, Anthropic Claude Code team

65Skills and slash commands as reusable prompts

establishedSeed topic 6
Search forskillsSKILL.md.claude/skills.claude/commandscustom slash commands$ARGUMENTSdisable-model-invocationprompt files

What it is. Any prompt you type repeatedly can become a file: a SKILL.md with frontmatter (name, description) and a body that becomes /skill-name. Custom commands in .claude/commands/ have been merged into skills. Skills load only when used, so long reference material 'costs almost nothing until you need it'; $ARGUMENTS passes the trailing text in.

Why it matters for you. This is where the reusable versions of the other topics live: a /brief skill that reformats a dump into a task brief, a /interview skill, a /pr-description skill with an example, etc. It also lets the team share the same prompts via git.

Best practices (11)
  • Create a skill when you keep pasting the same checklist or when a CLAUDE.md section has become a procedure rather than a fact.
  • Keep the body concise; once loaded it stays in context every turn.
  • Write a good description so Claude can auto-invoke it; set disable-model-invocation: true for side-effect workflows you want to trigger yourself.
  • Use $ARGUMENTS / $0 for parameterised prompts (/fix-issue 1234).
  • Check skills into git so the team runs the same prompts.
  • Create a skill when you paste the same procedure into chat a third time, or when a CLAUDE.md section has become a procedure rather than a fact.
  • Use disable-model-invocation: true for workflows with side effects (deploy, publish) so only you trigger them; leave reference skills model-invocable.
  • Lead descriptions with the words a request would contain; descriptions get shortened when there are many skills.
  • Bundle scripts with skills so deterministic steps run as code rather than being re-derived by the model.
  • Have Claude self-reflect after a task and capture the successful approach back into the skill (Anthropic's Agent Skills guidance).
  • Only install skills from trusted sources; audit bundled scripts and external connections.
Where to learn (6 sources)
  • Extend Claude with skills Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Create skills) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Claude Code Custom Commands & Slash Commands: Complete Guide (2026) Skills Playground, 2026, blog
  • Equipping agents for the real world with Agent Skills Anthropic Engineering, 2025-10, blog
  • Claude Code Customization Guide (2026): CLAUDE.md vs Skills vs Subagents alexop.dev, 2026, blog
  • Agent Skills with Anthropic (short course) DeepLearning.AI + Anthropic, 2026, course

66System prompt vs CLAUDE.md vs user message: where an instruction lives

established
Search forsystem prompt--append-system-prompt--system-prompt-fileuser turnrole promptinggive Claude a roleinstruction layeringClaude Code system prompt

What it is. In the API, the system prompt sets role and standing behaviour and the user turn carries the task; Anthropic says even a one-sentence role in the system prompt 'makes a difference'. In Claude Code the layers are: Claude Code's own system prompt (huge, tool-focused; reverse-engineered copies exist), optional --system-prompt / --append-system-prompt flags, CLAUDE.md (injected as a user message after the system prompt), skills (loaded on demand), and your message. Knowing which layer an instruction is in explains why some rules 'stick' and others don't.

Why it matters for you. When a dictated instruction keeps getting ignored, the fix is often moving it to the right layer (CLAUDE.md, a skill, a hook) rather than repeating it louder in the message.

Best practices (4)
  • Standing behaviour -> CLAUDE.md or a rule; multi-step procedure -> skill; must-always-happen -> hook; one-off task -> the message.
  • --append-system-prompt must be passed on every invocation, so it suits scripts and automation more than interactive use.
  • Role prompting is less necessary with modern models (Anthropic's 2025 blog) but still cheap and harmless in a system prompt.
  • CLAUDE.md is advisory context, not enforced config; use hooks or permissions.deny for hard enforcement.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • CLI reference (system prompt flags) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • How Claude remembers your project (Claude isn't following my CLAUDE.md) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Prompting best practices (Give Claude a role) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • claude-code-system-prompts (reverse-engineered) Piebald AI, 2026, repo

Names to know: Piebald AI

67Rich inputs: screenshots, @file references, piped data instead of prose

establishedSeed topic 5
Search forpaste imagesdrag and drop screenshotAlt+V on Windows@file referencecat log | claudeannotated screenshotsvisioncrop tool

What it is. Anthropic's 'provide rich content' guidance: reference files with @ instead of describing where code lives, paste or drag images (Ctrl+V; Alt+V on Windows/WSL), give URLs, pipe data in (cat error.log | claude), or tell Claude to fetch what it needs. Recent models have improved vision; annotated screenshots (circle the thing) often let Claude one-shot UI fixes.

Why it matters for you. A large part of a dictated dump is often a description of something that could be shown instead: a screenshot of the bug, the actual error log, the design mockup. Swapping prose for artifacts shortens the prompt and removes ambiguity.

Best practices (5)
  • Show, don't describe: screenshot + 'implement this design, screenshot the result, list differences'.
  • On Windows use Alt+V or drag-and-drop; confirm '[Image #N]' appears before sending (paste can fail silently).
  • Use @path for files so Claude reads them before responding; directory references list files, not contents.
  • For long logs, write to a file and ask Claude to read it (also avoids VS Code terminal dropping characters on big pastes).
  • Multiple images in one conversation are fine; keep each under ~2000px.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Provide rich content) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Common workflows (Work with images) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs

68Output format control and structured outputs (JSON schema, no more prefill)

established
Search forstructured outputsoutput_format json_schemastrict tool useconstrained decodingprefill deprecatedresponse formatno preamble

What it is. Two levels of controlling what comes back: in prompts, tell Claude the format positively, use XML tags to mark sections, and request 'respond directly without preamble'; in the API, Structured Outputs (output_format json_schema or strict: true on tools) guarantee schema-valid JSON via constrained decoding. Prefilling the assistant turn, the old trick for forcing formats, is no longer supported from Claude 4.6 onward.

Why it matters for you. Relevant when Mike's products call Claude programmatically, and when he wants a session to emit a predictable artifact (a table, a JSON task list, a spec with fixed headings) that another step can consume.

Best practices (4)
  • For interactive work, just ask for the format explicitly and give a skeleton or example.
  • For pipelines, use Structured Outputs rather than 'please return JSON' plus retries.
  • Migrate any prefill usage: direct instructions, XML tags, structured outputs, or tool calls.
  • For classification, use an enum field in a tool or schema.
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Structured outputs Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Prompting best practices (Control the format; Migrating away from prefilled responses) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • A Hands-On Guide to Anthropic's New Structured Output Capabilities Towards Data Science, blog

Names to know: Anthropic

69Context engineering: right altitude, minimal high-signal tokens, clean sessions

establishedSeed topic 5
Search forcontext engineeringright altitudecontext window is the constraint/clear/compacttwo-corrections rulekitchen sink sessionsubagents for investigation

What it is. Anthropic's framing that prompting is now a subset of curating 'the optimal set of tokens' in context. Prompts should sit at the 'right altitude' (not brittle if-then logic, not vague vibes), use the minimal set of high-signal information, and sessions should be managed so irrelevant history doesn't crowd out the instruction. The Claude Code docs' rule: after two failed corrections, /clear and rewrite the prompt - 'a clean session with a better prompt almost always outperforms a long session with accumulated corrections'.

Why it matters for you. Big pasted inputs are context spend. This topic is the budget discipline around them: what to paste, what to put in a file, when to start over, and why 'more context' can make results worse.

Best practices (5)
  • Right altitude: specific enough to guide, flexible enough to let the model use judgement.
  • /clear between unrelated tasks; /compact with instructions to keep what matters; /btw for side questions that shouldn't enter history.
  • Delegate wide exploration to subagents so file reads don't fill the main context.
  • Scope investigations ('infinite exploration' is a listed failure pattern).
  • Start a fresh session to implement a spec written in a previous one.
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Effective context engineering for AI agents Anthropic, 2025-09, blog
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Manage your session; Avoid common failure patterns) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Context Engineering: A Practical Guide for AI Agents (2026) Sourcegraph, 2026, blog

Names to know: Prithvi Rajasekaran, Ethan Dixon, Carly Ryan, Jeremy Hadfield (Anthropic)

70Decompose and chain: self-correction loops and one-ask-per-turn

establishedSeed topic 6
Search forprompt chainingdraft -> review -> refineself-correction chaintask decompositionone scoped ask per turnwriter/reviewer patternrubber-duck prompting

What it is. Breaking a big request into sequential steps whose outputs feed the next: Anthropic's most common chain is draft -> review against criteria -> refine, and Claude Code's Writer/Reviewer pattern runs the review in a fresh session so it isn't biased toward code it just wrote. The dictation guides add the human-side version: one scoped ask per turn, read the diff, then the next ask. 'Rubber-duck' prompting (explaining the problem to the model to clarify your own thinking) is a related informal habit; it is widely used but not a formally documented technique.

Why it matters for you. The failure mode of a long dictated message is five tasks in one paragraph. Decomposition is the fix: one brief per task, or one brief with an explicit ordered task list the agent can tick through.

Best practices (4)
  • If the dump contains several asks, have the organizer step split it into separately-runnable tasks with dependencies.
  • Ask for a numbered plan and then 'do step 1 and stop' when you want checkpoints.
  • Run reviews in a fresh context (subagent or second session) with the plan as the rubric: 'report gaps, not style preferences'.
  • Explicit chaining is worth it when you want to inspect or branch on intermediate output; otherwise let adaptive thinking handle it.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Prompting best practices (Chain complex prompts) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Building effective agents Anthropic, 2024-12, blog
  • Best practices for Claude Code (Run multiple Claude sessions; Add an adversarial review step) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026, docs
  • Wispr Flow + Claude Code & Codex: A Best-Practice Guide (review discipline) Thomas Prommer, 2026-06, blog

Names to know: Erik Schluntz, Barry Zhang (Anthropic), Thomas Prommer

Broad agentic development best practices 37

71Hooks as deterministic guardrails and automation

establishedClaude Code mastery
Search forPreToolUsePostToolUseStop hookSessionStartprompt-based hooksagent-based hooksexit code 2settings.json hooks

What it is. Hooks run shell commands, HTTP requests, MCP tool calls, prompts, or subagents at lifecycle events (tool use, stop, session start, compaction, permission requests, task creation, etc.). Unlike CLAUDE.md they are deterministic: a PreToolUse hook exiting 2 blocks the action and feeds stderr back to the model; a Stop hook exiting 2 forces Claude to keep working. Prompt- and agent-based hooks let a model make the judgment call.

Why it matters for you. Hooks are how you convert 'please always run the linter' from a hope into a guarantee, and they are the building block for autonomous verification loops.

Autonomy: yes - hooks are the enforcement layer that makes it safe to walk away from a session.

Best practices (6)
  • Put guardrails in hooks, not prompts: 'never edit .env' in CLAUDE.md is a request; a PreToolUse hook that blocks it is enforcement.
  • Use PostToolUse for formatting/lint after Edit|Write; PreToolUse for validation and for rewriting commands (e.g. filter test output to failures only to save context).
  • Use a Stop hook that runs your check and blocks the turn until it passes (Claude Code overrides after 8 consecutive blocks); this is the deterministic version of /goal.
  • Keep hooks fast (<1s) and matchers specific; a typical production setup is 3-5 hooks.
  • Ask Claude to write hooks for you ('write a hook that runs eslint after every file edit'); browse with /hooks.
  • Use SessionStart hooks to inject context (e.g. recommend the right plugin for the directory); use a Stop hook that reads the transcript and proposes CLAUDE.md updates.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Automate actions with hooks Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Claude Code Hooks: Real Examples (PostToolUse, Stop, PreToolUse) DEV Community, 2026, blog
  • ClaudeLog: Hooks mechanics ClaudeLog, 2026, blog
  • Claude Code Hooks: 6 Production Patterns (2026) Pixelmojo, 2026, blog

Names to know: Anthropic Claude Code team

72The extension decision matrix: CLAUDE.md vs rules vs skills vs hooks vs subagents vs MCP vs plugins

establishedClaude Code mastery
Search forfeatures-overviewmatch features to your goalbuild your setup over timecontext cost by featuresteering Claude Code

What it is. Anthropic's own framework for which extension point to use: CLAUDE.md for always-on facts, rules for path-scoped guidance, skills for on-demand knowledge and invocable workflows, hooks for things that must happen every time, subagents for context isolation, MCP for external systems, plugins for packaging. Each has a different context cost and loading time, and there are trigger-based rules for when to add each.

Why it matters for you. Most 'Claude ignores my instructions' and 'my sessions are expensive' problems come from putting content in the wrong layer; this matrix is the fix.

Autonomy: yes - the right layering is what makes a harness that runs unattended without drowning in context.

Best practices (4)
  • Follow the triggers: wrong twice -> CLAUDE.md; same prompt repeatedly -> skill; copying from a browser tab -> MCP; output floods context -> subagent; must happen every time -> hook; second repo needs it -> plugin.
  • Know the context cost: CLAUDE.md is paid every request; skill descriptions are cheap; MCP schemas are deferred via tool search; hooks cost zero unless they return output.
  • Combine features: MCP provides the connection, a skill teaches how to use it; a skill can spawn subagents; a hook can call MCP.
  • Revisit the matrix every 3-6 months (Anthropic's 'Claude Code at scale' advice) as model capability shifts what needs to be spelled out.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Extend Claude Code (features overview) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Steering Claude Code: when to use CLAUDE.md, skills, hooks, and subagents Anthropic, 2026, blog
  • How Claude Code works in large codebases: best practices and where to start Anthropic, 2026-05, blog
  • Understanding Claude Code: Skills vs Commands vs Subagents vs Plugins Young Leaders in Tech, 2026, blog

Names to know: Anthropic Claude Code team

73MCP servers: scopes, tool search, and CLI-first thinking

establishedClaude Code mastery
Search forModel Context Protocolclaude mcp add.mcp.jsonlocal/project/user scopetool search / deferred toolsconnectors directorygh/aws/gcloud CLIs

What it is. MCP connects Claude Code to external systems (GitHub, databases, Figma, Slack, browsers) over stdio, HTTP, SSE, or WebSocket. Servers can be local, project-shared via .mcp.json, or user-wide. Tool definitions are deferred by default (tool search), and Anthropic's advice is to prefer CLIs like gh, aws, gcloud, and sentry-cli when they exist because they are more context-efficient.

Why it matters for you. MCP is how agents get eyes and hands on the systems around your code; choosing well (and sparingly) is a context and security decision.

Autonomy: yes - external tool access is what lets an agent complete a task end to end (issue -> code -> PR -> Slack update) without you as the relay.

Best practices (5)
  • Install the gh CLI before any GitHub MCP; Claude already knows how to use it and unauthenticated API calls hit rate limits.
  • Check .mcp.json into git for team servers, but keep secrets in env vars with ${VAR} expansion.
  • Run /mcp to disable servers you are not using and /context all to see per-tool token cost.
  • Treat every third-party server as untrusted code: it can fetch external content and become a prompt-injection vector; prefer servers you wrote or from providers you trust.
  • Teach Claude unknown CLIs with 'use foo --help to learn the tool, then do X'.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Connect Claude Code to tools via MCP Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Writing effective tools for AI agents, using AI agents Anthropic Engineering, 2025-09, blog
  • MCP: Build Rich-Context AI Apps with Anthropic (short course) DeepLearning.AI + Anthropic, 2025, course
  • Choosing between skills, subagents, and MCP servers in Claude Code Smith Horn Group, 2026, blog

Names to know: Anthropic MCP team

74Permissions, allowlists, sandboxing, and auto mode

establishedClaude Code mastery
Search for/permissionspermissions.allow/denyacceptEditsauto mode classifier--dangerously-skip-permissions/sandboxbubblewrap/seatbeltapproval fatigue

What it is. Claude Code has a spectrum of permission modes: Manual (ask), acceptEdits, plan (read-only), auto (a classifier model approves/blocks actions), dontAsk, and bypassPermissions. Allowlists pre-approve trusted commands; OS-level sandboxing isolates filesystem and network and cut prompts 84% in Anthropic's testing. Auto mode is the built-in default on Pro/Max/Team and blocks scope escalation, unknown infra, and hostile-content-driven actions, but Anthropic reported a 17% false-negative rate in its own evaluation.

Why it matters for you. Approval fatigue makes you rubber-stamp; the right combination (allowlist + sandbox + auto mode) is what lets 5-10 parallel sessions run without you babysitting or YOLO-ing.

Autonomy: yes - this is the literal control knob for how independent an agent is allowed to be.

Best practices (5)
  • Allowlist the safe, common commands (tests, lint, build, git commit) in team settings.json and share via git.
  • Enable /sandbox so sandboxed commands run without asking; keep --dangerously-skip-permissions for disposable containers with no secrets.
  • Use auto mode for long parallel runs but keep explicit deny rules for the truly dangerous (rm -rf, force push, prod infra).
  • Deny reads of build output and vendored code (Read(./**/dist/**)) to save context and reduce blast radius.
  • Audit permissions regularly with /permissions; use ConfigChange hooks to catch settings tampering.
Where to learn (5 sources)
  • Choose a permission mode Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • How we built Claude Code auto mode: a safer way to skip permissions Anthropic Engineering, 2026-03, blog
  • Beyond permission prompts: making Claude Code more secure and autonomous (sandboxing) Anthropic Engineering, 2025-10, blog
  • Locking Down Claude Code with settings.json: allow and deny lists Claude Code Security (Substack), 2026, blog
  • Security (Claude Code docs) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs

Names to know: Anthropic Claude Code security team

75Headless mode, fan-out scripts, and the Claude Agent SDK

establishedClaude Code mastery
Search forclaude -p--output-format json / stream-json--allowedTools--max-turns--max-budget-usd/batchAgent SDK Python/TypeScriptManaged Agents

What it is. claude -p runs Claude Code non-interactively for CI, pre-commit hooks, and scripts, with JSON output for piping. Fan-out loops call claude -p per file with scoped --allowedTools; /batch splits a change across 5-30 subagents each in its own worktree with its own PR. The Agent SDK exposes the same loop, tools, hooks, subagents, MCP, permissions, and sessions as a Python/TypeScript library; Managed Agents is Anthropic's hosted alternative.

Why it matters for you. This is the bridge from 'using Claude Code' to 'building products and pipelines with it', and it is also the vocabulary hiring managers use for agent engineering.

Autonomy: yes - headless invocation is the substrate for every unattended workflow (CI bots, nightly sweeps, custom agents).

Best practices (5)
  • Test a fan-out prompt on 2-3 files, refine, then run on all; restrict tools with --allowedTools when unattended.
  • Use --output-format json for pipelines and --verbose only while debugging.
  • Set --max-turns and budget limits in CI to prevent runaway jobs.
  • Choose the SDK when you need custom tools and control flow; choose -p when the opinionated agent is fine; choose Managed Agents when you don't want to run sandboxes.
  • Note: as of mid-2026, SDK and -p usage still draws from subscription limits (the separate credit pool was paused); check billing before scaling.

76Parallel sessions: worktrees, writer/reviewer, agent view, cross-session messaging

establishedClaude Code mastery
Search forgit worktreeclaude --worktree / -wsparsePathssymlinkDirectoriesdesktop app one worktree per sessionclaude agents (agent view)cross-session messaging/rename /resume

What it is. Each Claude session gets its own git worktree so edits never collide; the desktop app does this automatically. Beyond throughput, a second session with fresh context is a better reviewer than the one that wrote the code (Writer/Reviewer, or test-writer/implementer). Agent view (research preview) dispatches background sessions you monitor from one screen; cross-session messaging lets your sessions pass findings.

Why it matters for you. You already run parallel sessions; worktrees plus explicit writer/reviewer pairing turns that into a quality mechanism, not just a speed hack.

Autonomy: yes - isolation is what allows many agents to run unattended without stepping on each other.

Best practices (5)
  • One worktree per workstream; name sessions with /rename and treat them like branches.
  • Run a Writer/Reviewer pair: Session A implements, Session B reviews the diff in fresh context, A addresses findings.
  • In large repos, set worktree.sparsePaths and symlink node_modules to keep worktrees fast.
  • Boris Cherny runs 5-10 parallel Opus sessions with auto mode; the enabling conditions are verification loops and allowlists, not just worktrees.
  • Use cross-session messaging only when sessions genuinely need each other's findings mid-task (inter-agent comms are covered in depth by another agent).
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Run parallel sessions with worktrees Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs

  • Boris Cherny: how I use Claude Code (thread) Boris Cherny (Anthropic), 2026, thread

77Plugins, marketplaces, and code-intelligence (LSP) plugins

establishedClaude Code mastery
Search for/pluginclaude-plugins-officialplugin = skills + hooks + subagents + MCP + LSPtypescript-lspFrontend Design pluginralph-loop plugincode-review pluginsecurity-guidance plugin

What it is. Plugins bundle skills, hooks, subagents, MCP servers, and LSP servers into an installable unit from a marketplace (Anthropic's official one has 200+; community directories list thousands). Code-intelligence plugins connect a language server so Claude jumps to definitions and gets type errors after edits instead of grepping and re-reading files.

Why it matters for you. Plugins are the fastest way to adopt proven setups across your several projects, and LSP plugins cut token spend and mistakes in typed codebases.

Best practices (4)
  • Install a code-intelligence plugin for every typed language you use (e.g. /plugin install typescript-lsp@claude-plugins-official).
  • Package your own cross-repo setup as a plugin once a second repo needs it.
  • Prefer official/partner plugins; most are free, the cost is the underlying service.
  • Review a plugin's hooks and MCP servers before installing; it runs with your permissions.

78Auto memory, checkpoints, and rewind (session recovery)

establishedClaude Code mastery
Search forMEMORY.md~/.claude/projects/<project>/memory/memory/rewindEsc EsccheckpointingSummarize from here/branch

What it is. Auto memory (on by default since Feb 2026) lets Claude write its own notes per repo: a MEMORY.md index (first 200 lines/25KB load each session) plus topic files, covering user preferences, feedback, project context, and references. Checkpointing snapshots files before every prompt so /rewind can restore code, conversation, or both, or summarize part of the transcript. Neither replaces git.

Why it matters for you. Auto memory is machine-local and per-repo, so it quietly diverges across your machines/projects unless you curate it; rewind makes risky experiments cheap.

Best practices (5)
  • Audit auto memory with /memory; promote durable learnings into CLAUDE.md (shared) and prune stale entries.
  • Remember auto memory is not synced across machines or cloud sessions; anything a teammate or cloud run needs belongs in the repo.
  • Use /rewind after a bad direction instead of correcting repeatedly; after two failed corrections, /clear and re-prompt with what you learned.
  • Checkpoints miss Bash-made changes and most subagent edits; commit early and often so git is the real undo.
  • Use /btw for side questions that should not enter context.
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • How Claude remembers your project (auto memory section) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Checkpointing Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs

79Cost and usage awareness: /usage, /insights, model and effort choice

establishedClaude Code mastery
Search for/usage/cost/insightsprompt cache statisticscache misseseffort levels low/medium/high/xhigh/maxMAX_THINKING_TOKENSccusage

What it is. Claude Code reports session tokens and estimated cost, cache hit rates, attribution by skill/subagent/MCP server, and behavior flags (long context, cache misses). /insights writes an HTML report on how you work and where friction is. Enterprise averages are ~$13/dev/day. The biggest levers are model choice, context size, cache invalidation, thinking/effort level, and verbose output.

Why it matters for you. Parallel sessions multiply spend; knowing what a cache miss or a kitchen-sink session costs turns cost from a surprise into a design choice.

Best practices (6)
  • Run /usage to see attribution and behavior flags; run /insights monthly to find friction patterns.
  • Sonnet for most work, Opus for architecture and long agentic chains, Haiku for simple subagents (Boris's counterpoint: Opus at high effort is often faster because it needs less steering).
  • Avoid cache invalidation: don't change system prompt/tools/thinking settings mid-session; long breaks (>1h on subscription, >5m on API) miss cache.
  • Move detailed instructions from CLAUDE.md to skills; prefer CLIs over MCP; disable unused MCP servers.
  • Lower /effort or thinking budget for simple tasks; raise xhigh for hard debugging and async work.
  • Give verification targets and specific prompts: vague prompts pay for exploration.

80Giving agents eyes: Claude in Chrome, Playwright MCP, screenshots as verification

establishedClaude Code mastery

What it is. Claude Code can drive a real Chrome/Edge tab through the Claude in Chrome extension (navigate, click, read console and network, screenshot, record GIFs, use your logged-in sessions) or a Playwright MCP server (accessibility-tree based, token-efficient). Anthropic's long-running-agent harness uses browser automation to test features 'as a user would', and Boris Cherny has Claude test every UI change in a browser.

Why it matters for you. Frontend work is where 'looks done' and 'is done' diverge most; a browser loop is the difference between a session you watch and one you walk away from.

Autonomy: yes - a sensor the agent can read closes the loop without you.

Best practices (5)
  • Paste a design or screenshot, ask Claude to implement, screenshot the result, list differences, and fix; iterate.

  • Enable Chrome per-session with --chrome rather than by default to avoid always-loaded browser tools in context.
  • For local apps, ask Claude to start the dev server and exercise the flow end to end before declaring done.
  • Use Figma MCP + browser verification for design-to-code work (DeepLearning.AI course project).

81Claude Code on the web: cloud sessions, --cloud/--teleport, auto-fix PRs

emergingClaude Code mastery
Search forclaude.ai/codeclaude --cloudclaude --teleport/taskscloud environmentsnetwork allowlist/autofix-prRemote Control

What it is. Cloud sessions run on Anthropic-managed VMs cloned from your GitHub repo, persist when your laptop closes, and can be steered from the web or phone. From the terminal, claude --cloud starts one, --teleport pulls one back, and /tasks lists them. Auto-fix watches a PR and responds to CI failures and review comments. Cloud environments control network access, env vars, and setup scripts.

Why it matters for you. This is the concrete 'hand off and come back to a PR' autonomy level, and it offloads compute from your Windows box.

Autonomy: yes - Level 3/4 autonomy (task in, PR out) in one command.

Best practices (5)
  • Plan locally in plan mode, commit the plan, then claude --cloud 'Execute docs/plan.md'.
  • Fire several independent --cloud tasks in parallel and review PRs later; monitor with /tasks or the mobile app.
  • Scope cloud environments to the network access the task needs; keep secrets as API credentials, not env vars.
  • Turn on auto-fix for PRs where CI churn is mechanical; disable it where a PR comment can trigger deploys.
  • Rate limits are shared with all Claude usage, so parallel cloud tasks burn quota proportionally.
