82Verification loops: give Claude a way to verify its work

establishedEngineering practice
Search forfeedback loopdefinition of done is executabletrust-then-verify gapshow evidence not assertionsverification gap/verifytests/build/lint/screenshot as signal

What it is. The single most repeated best practice from Anthropic and Boris Cherny: Claude stops when work 'looks done', so give it a check it can run (tests, build exit code, linter, diff-against-fixture script, browser screenshot) and it iterates until the check passes. Cherny claims this 2-3x's output quality. Anthropic's docs describe an escalation ladder: in-prompt check -> /goal condition -> Stop hook gate -> verification subagent.

Why it matters for you. This is the one habit that converts 'watch every step' into 'walk away and review evidence'.

Autonomy: yes - the core enabler; every higher rung of autonomy assumes this exists.

Best practices (5)
  • Write prompts with verification criteria: example test cases, expected output, 'run the tests after implementing', 'address the root cause, don't suppress the error'.
  • Have Claude show evidence (test output, command + result, screenshot) instead of asserting success.
  • Escalate gating as trust grows: prompt -> /goal -> Stop hook -> adversarial reviewer subagent.
  • Match the sensor to the domain: bash command, test suite, browser for frontend, simulator for mobile, computer use for desktop.
  • If you cannot verify it, do not ship it.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Best practices: Give Claude a way to verify its work Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Give Claude a way to verify its work (Boris Cherny post) Boris Cherny, 2026, thread
  • Verification Beats Prompting: Boris Cherny's top Claude Code tip Vibe Coder blog, 2026, blog
  • Claude Code Autonomous Loops: Ship Features While You Sleep claudefa.st, 2026, blog

Names to know: Boris Cherny

83Test-driven development with agents

establishedEngineering practice
Search forred-green-refactorwrite a failing test firstTDD Guardtest-writer / implementer splitavoid mocksreproduce the bug then fix

What it is. Claude naturally writes implementation then tests; TDD requires the inverse and must be prompted explicitly ('write a FAILING test, do not implement yet'). Anthropic's best-practices flow for bugs is 'write a failing test that reproduces it, then fix'. TDD Guard is a hook-based plugin that blocks implementation without a failing test and blocks over-implementation, across JS/TS, Python, PHP, Go, Rust, Ruby.

Why it matters for you. Tests are the most portable verification loop and the one thing that keeps regenerated code from silently regressing.

Autonomy: yes - a real test suite is what lets an agent iterate to done unattended.

Best practices (5)
  • Prompt the sequence explicitly: failing test -> minimal implementation -> refactor; confirm the test fails first.
  • Have one session write tests from the spec and another make them pass (Anthropic's suggestion) to avoid tests that assert the wrong thing.
  • Ask for tests without mocks where feasible and prefer running single tests over the whole suite for speed.
  • Install TDD Guard or an equivalent hook if you want enforcement rather than encouragement.
  • Watch for AI tests that assert current behavior rather than intended behavior; review test names and assertions, not just green checks.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • TDD Guard: Automated TDD enforcement for Claude Code nizos, 2026, repo
  • Test-Driven Development with Claude Code (course module) Steve Kinney, 2026, course
  • A Claude Code TDD Skill: Forcing Red-Green-Refactor Discipline alexop.dev, 2026, blog
  • claude-code-ultimate-guide: TDD with Claude Florian Bruniaux, 2026, repo

Names to know: Kent Beck, Steve Kinney

84Reviewing AI-generated code: small PRs, adversarial review, and review tooling

establishedEngineering practice
Search for/code-reviewUltrareviewCode Review (GitHub App)stacked PRsunder 400 lineshallucinated APIstests that assert the wrong thingreview the diff against the plan

What it is. AI code needs the same scrutiny as human code plus a check that it does what the prompt intended. Industry data shows AI PRs get accepted far less often and hide plausible-but-wrong logic, invented APIs, and wrong-assertion tests. The practical fixes: keep PRs small (a few hundred lines), state acceptance criteria up front, run automated linters/scanners first, and use a fresh-context reviewer (Claude Code's /code-review, a review subagent, cloud Ultrareview, or a GitHub review action).

Why it matters for you. Review is now the bottleneck; if you cannot review it, you cannot ship it, and small diffs are what make review of agent output humane.

Autonomy: yes - automated review gates are what allow more of the pipeline to run without you.

Best practices (5)
  • Keep PRs under ~400 lines; split large agent work into stacked PRs.
  • Write the acceptance criteria before the code so review checks intent, not just style.
  • Let deterministic tools (lint, typecheck, security scan) catch 70-80% before a human or model reviewer looks.
  • Have a fresh-context reviewer check the diff against PLAN.md and report only correctness gaps; ignore its style nitpicks.
  • Read tests as carefully as code: AI tests often lock in bugs.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • How to Review AI-Generated Code in 2026 CodeAnt AI, 2026, blog

85Git discipline as the agent safety net

establishedEngineering practice
Search forcommit early and oftenbranch per sessionclaude/ prefixed branchesdescriptive commit messagescheckpoints are not gitgit history as agent memoryconventional commits

What it is. Every serious practitioner (Willison, Osmani, Anthropic's harness posts) lists version control as a precondition for agentic work: it is how you undo agent mistakes, how a fresh session reconstructs what happened (Anthropic's harness reads git log + progress file), and how parallel work merges. Cloud sessions push to claude/ branches; auto-fix and review bots operate on PRs.

Why it matters for you. With multiple parallel sessions, git is the only shared truth; sloppy commits make both review and recovery painful.

Autonomy: yes - git log plus progress notes is how long-running agents resume across context resets.

Best practices (4)
  • Commit at every green checkpoint; ask Claude to commit with a message that explains why.
  • One branch per session/worktree; never let two agents edit the same files on the same branch.
  • Let Claude use git history to answer 'why' questions ('look through the git history and summarize how this API came to be').
  • Make 'commit and open a PR' a skill so the end of every task is the same ritual.

86Evals for agents and prompts (error analysis first)

establishedEngineering practice
Search forproduct evals vs benchmarkserror analysisbinary pass/failLLM-as-judge validationcode-based / model-based / human graderspass@k vs pass^k20-50 tasks from real failureseval saturation

What it is. Evals measure whether your agent works on your tasks with your data. Anthropic's 'Demystifying evals' distinguishes single-turn from multi-turn agent evals, three grader types, pass@k vs pass^k, and recommends starting with 20-50 tasks drawn from real failures. Hamel Husain's approach: read 100+ traces, cluster failure modes, use binary judgments, validate LLM judges against human labels, reject generic metrics, and wire evals into CI and production monitoring.

Why it matters for you. Evals are how a PM defines quality for an AI feature and how you know a prompt or harness change helped; the course is literally aimed at engineers and PMs.

Autonomy: yes - you cannot safely raise autonomy without a way to measure regressions.

Best practices (6)
  • Do error analysis before building graders: sample traces, write notes, build a failure taxonomy, count.
  • Prefer binary pass/fail over 1-5 scales; appoint one domain expert as the quality 'benevolent dictator'.
  • Convert manual test procedures and user-reported bugs into eval tasks; keep tasks unambiguous and solvable.
  • Grade outcomes, not step sequences; review transcripts to check graders are fair.
  • Run a small curated set in CI and sample production asynchronously; feed new failures back into CI.
  • Apply the same loop to your own Claude Code setup: when a CLAUDE.md or skill change is made, have a few repeatable tasks to check it helped.
Where to learn (5 sources)
  • Demystifying evals for AI agents Anthropic Engineering, 2026-01, blog
  • AI Evals: Everything You Need to Know (FAQ) Hamel Husain, 2025-05 (updated 2026-09), blog
  • Your AI Product Needs Evals Hamel Husain, 2024, blog
  • AI Evals For Engineers & PMs (Maven course) Hamel Husain and Shreya Shankar, 2026, course
  • A pragmatic guide to LLM evals for devs The Pragmatic Engineer (Gergely Orosz), 2026, blog

Names to know: Hamel Husain, Shreya Shankar, Gergely Orosz

87Observability and tracing for agents

establishedEngineering practice
Search forLangfuseLangSmithBraintrustArize PhoenixOpenTelemetry / OpenInferencetraces and spansOTEL export from Claude Codeskill_activated event

What it is. Agent observability is trace-first: every LLM call, tool call, and subagent becomes a span you can inspect, cost, and turn into an eval case. Langfuse (MIT, self-hostable) and Arize Phoenix (OTel-native) are the open-source picks; LangSmith is strongest for LangChain/LangGraph; Braintrust leads on eval-driven CI gates. Claude Code itself exports OpenTelemetry metrics and logs (tokens, cost, tool activity, skill activations).

Why it matters for you. If you build an AI feature, traces are your debugging surface; for your own Claude Code usage, OTel export is how you see which skills, MCP servers, and sessions burn money.

Autonomy: yes - unattended agents need traces because nobody was watching.

Best practices (4)
  • Instrument once with OpenTelemetry/OpenInference so you can switch vendors.
  • Turn traces into evals: bookmark bad traces, label them, add them to the CI set.
  • Enable Claude Code's OTel logs with tool details to find unused skills and expensive servers.
  • Pick the tool by what you optimize: experiment loop (Braintrust), trace depth (Langfuse/LangSmith), data warehouse landing (Arize).
Where to learn (4 sources)

88Agent security: prompt injection, MCP supply chain, least privilege

emergingEngineering practice
Search forindirect prompt injectionexcessive agency (OWASP LLM Top 10)MCP CVEstool poisoningskill supply chainsandbox / VM / devcontainerisolated context for web fetchhuman-in-the-loop for consequential actions

What it is. Prompt injection remains the top cause of agentic security failures and is unsolved for fully autonomous agents; 2026 saw CVEs in MCP SDKs and servers, a poisoned npm MCP package, and agent-environment poisoning attacks. The threat model is now supply-chain shaped: any agent, skill, or server in your dependency graph can steer your agent. Claude Code's mitigations are permissions, sandboxing, isolated contexts for fetched content, trust dialogs for repos and MCP servers, and network command approval.

Why it matters for you. More autonomy equals more blast radius; the security posture is what determines how far up the autonomy ladder you can responsibly go.

Autonomy: yes (as a constraint) - defines which actions must never be autonomous.

Best practices (5)
  • Treat all observed content (web, files, issues, PR comments) as data, not instructions; avoid piping untrusted content straight into Claude.
  • Run untrusted or high-autonomy work in a VM, devcontainer, or cloud sandbox with restricted egress.
  • Vet MCP servers, skills, and plugins like dependencies: pin versions, read the code, prefer first-party/reviewed connectors.
  • Keep humans at consequential decisions (payments, deploys, deletes) even when the rest is automated; deny-list those tools.
  • Run /security-review before merging agent branches; scan for secrets in commits.
Where to learn (5 sources)
  • MCP Security Crisis: Systemic Design Flaws in AI Agent Infrastructure Cloud Security Alliance, 2026-05, paper
  • Prompt injection still drives most agentic AI security failures in production (OWASP) Help Net Security / OWASP, 2026-06, blog
  • Securing AI agents: when AI tools move from reading to acting Microsoft Security, 2026-06, blog
  • Security (Claude Code docs) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Skills Are Not Islands: dependency and risk in agent skill supply chains arXiv, 2026-07, paper

Names to know: OWASP LLM Top 10, Cloud Security Alliance, Simon Willison (prompt injection coverage)

89Harness engineering (Agent = Model + Harness)

emergingEngineering practice
Search forharnessguides (feedforward) vs sensors (feedback)computational vs inferential checksmaintainability / architecture-fitness / behaviour harnessfitness functionsdrift detectiondocs as the agent's mapgarbage-collect the codebase

What it is. The 2026 discipline of building the environment around a model so it behaves reliably: tools, verification loops, context/memory, guardrails, observability. Böckeler (Thoughtworks) frames it as control systems: guides that prevent bad behavior and sensors that detect it, each either deterministic (linters, tests, types) or inferential (model review). OpenAI's account of an agent-built codebase emphasizes AGENTS.md as a map, docs as the agent's primary knowledge, linters as guardrails, and periodic 'garbage collection' of drift. Mitchell Hashimoto's 'Agent = Model + Harness' is the slogan; Anthropic's 'Claude Code at scale' says the harness is built from CLAUDE.md, hooks, skills, plugins, and MCP.

Why it matters for you. This is the unifying frame for everything else in this list and the phrase hiring managers now use; the mindset is 'when the agent makes a mistake, engineer the harness so it never makes it again'.

Autonomy: yes - harness quality is the ceiling on autonomy.

Best practices (5)
  • Classify each control: is it a guide or a sensor, deterministic or inferential? Prefer deterministic where possible.
  • Invest first in the maintainability harness (lint, format, types, tests), then architecture fitness checks, then behaviour checks.
  • Keep docs current because agents read docs, not Slack; schedule drift detection and cleanup passes.
  • Aim the harness at directing human attention to where it matters, not at eliminating humans.
  • Keep a 'lessons -> harness' log: every mistake becomes a rule, hook, test, or skill.
Where to learn (5 sources)
  • Harness engineering for coding agent users Birgitta Böckeler (Thoughtworks) on martinfowler.com, 2026-04, blog
  • Harness engineering: leveraging Codex in an agent-first world OpenAI, 2026, blog
  • Harness Engineering: Making AI Coding Agents Work in 2026 Faros AI, 2026, blog
  • awesome-harness-engineering ai-boost, 2026, repo
  • Long-running agents Addy Osmani, 2026-04, blog

Names to know: Birgitta Böckeler, Mitchell Hashimoto, Martin Fowler, Addy Osmani

90Documentation as code for agents: ADRs, AGENTS.md, and docs the agent can navigate

establishedProcess
Search forArchitecture Decision RecordsNygard ADR formatAGENTS.mddocs/ as agent memoryprogressive disclosure in docsdecision indexdocs drift routine

What it is. When agents generate code, ADRs stop being paperwork and become the durable 'why' the agent reads before making architectural choices; AGENTS.md/CLAUDE.md capture 'what must always/never happen'. Practitioners keep one ADR per decision with an index, reference the ADR directory from the agent instructions, and have agents both consult and draft ADRs. OpenAI's harness write-up treats the docs directory as the agent's map of the codebase.

Why it matters for you. As the human with product context, you are the natural author of ADRs; they are the highest-leverage documentation for agents and a portable artifact when explaining your work to hiring managers.

Autonomy: yes - decision records let agents make consistent choices without asking you.

Best practices (5)
  • Adopt a lightweight ADR template (Status, Context, Decision, Consequences), one file per decision, plus an index the agent scans first.
  • Point CLAUDE.md at docs/adr and require checking it before framework/db/protocol choices.
  • Have Claude draft an ADR at the end of any session that made an architectural choice; you edit and approve.
  • Schedule docs-drift checks (Anthropic lists it as a routine use case) so docs stay trustworthy for agents.
  • Prefer docs that explain intent and constraints over restating what the code says.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • AGENTS.md vs Architecture Decision Records: how AI coding agents are changing architecture documentation in 2026 Faisal Feroz (AI Advances), 2026, blog
  • Architecture Decision Records for AI Coding Agents CodeMySpec, 2026, blog
  • agent-skills: documentation-and-adrs skill Addy Osmani, 2026, repo
  • ADRs in Practice: Aligning Teams and AI Agents Through Better Documentation Nexapp, 2026, blog

Names to know: Michael Nygard (ADR format), Addy Osmani

91Autonomy ladders: from autocomplete to the dark factory

emergingAutonomy
Search forlevels of autonomyoperator/collaborator/consultant/approver/observertask agentautonomous teammateagentic avalanchedark factoryAI is an amplifier not a fix (DORA)when NOT to

What it is. Several five-level frameworks describe the same progression: L1 completion, L2 chat generation, L3 hand off a task and get a PR, L4 agent picks work from a backlog / runs unsupervised, L5 fully autonomous 'dark factory' pipeline with no human review. A user-centered academic variant defines levels by the human's role (operator -> observer). Swarmia's and DORA's caveat: higher is not better; L3+ only works if the engineering pipeline (tests, CI, review) is already healthy, because AI amplifies whatever is there.

Why it matters for you. A shared vocabulary for where you are, where each project could go, and how to explain your practice to hiring managers.

Autonomy: yes - it is the map of autonomy itself.

Best practices (4)
  • Assess each project's level honestly and identify the single missing precondition for the next rung (usually tests or a verification sensor).
  • Match the level to the task: mechanical, well-specified work goes higher; ambiguous product work stays lower.
  • Do not chase L5; aim for 'walk away for hours and review evidence' (L3-L4) with hard stops on irreversible actions.
  • Use the ladder as a talking point: 'I run L3 with adversarial review and am experimenting with L4 loops'.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • The 5 Levels of AI Coding Autonomy: From Spicy Autocomplete to the Dark Factory MindStudio, 2026-07, blog
  • Five levels of AI coding agent autonomy, and why higher isn't always better Miikka Holkeri (Swarmia), 2026-03, blog
  • The 5 levels of AI agent autonomy: learning from self-driving cars Tessl, 2025, blog
  • Levels of Autonomy for AI Agents arXiv / Knight First Amendment Institute, 2025-06, paper

Names to know: Miikka Holkeri, DORA research program

92/goal, Stop hooks, and completion gating (a separate judge decides 'done')

emergingAutonomy
Search for/goalgoal evaluator (Haiku)not yet met / met / impossibleStop hook exit 28-block override/loop vs /goal vs Stop hookverification subagentauto mode + goal

What it is. Claude Code's /goal sets a completion condition; after every turn a small model judges whether the transcript demonstrates it and Claude keeps working until met, impossible, or stalled. It is a session-scoped prompt-based Stop hook; a script Stop hook is the deterministic equivalent and a verification subagent is the 'second opinion' version. Combined with auto mode (no per-tool prompts), this removes per-turn prompts too.

Why it matters for you. This is the built-in, low-setup way to make a session self-driving with a judge that is not the worker.

Autonomy: yes - removes the human from the 'is it done yet?' loop.

Best practices (4)
  • Write conditions with one measurable end state, a stated check ('npm test exits 0'), constraints, and a bound ('or stop after 20 turns').
  • Run /goal in auto mode for unattended turns; it works in -p for CI-style runs.
  • Prefer a script Stop hook when the check is deterministic; use /goal or prompt hooks when judgment is needed.
  • Watch the evaluator's reasons (Ctrl+O) to catch conditions the transcript cannot demonstrate.
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Keep Claude working toward a goal (/goal) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Run prompts on a schedule (/loop; compare scheduling options) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Automate actions with hooks (Stop hook) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs

Names to know: Anthropic Claude Code team

93Event-driven and background PR agents (routines, GitHub Action, and the competitive landscape)

emergingAutonomy
Search forroutines (schedule/API/GitHub triggers)/scheduleclaude-code-action@claude in PRsCode Review GitHub AppDevinOpenAI Codex cloudGoogle Jules

What it is. Background agents follow one lifecycle: ticket or event -> cloud sandbox -> autonomous edit -> PR -> human review. Claude Code offers routines (cron, HTTP trigger, GitHub events) and a GitHub Action (@claude mentions, automation prompts, scheduled reports). Competitors: Devin (most autonomous, per-VM), Codex cloud, Jules (free tier), Cursor cloud agents, Copilot's coding agent. Independent tests show a large gap between vendor merge-rate claims and real-world success, so scoping matters more than the vendor.

Why it matters for you. Knowing the landscape and the shared lifecycle lets you speak credibly about background agents and pick the right tool per project. (Scheduled custodian agents are covered in depth elsewhere; this entry is the map.)

Autonomy: yes - this is Level 3-4 autonomy in production form.

Best practices (5)
  • Give background agents well-scoped tickets with acceptance criteria; that is where every vendor's success rate is highest.
  • Start with review and triage routines (read-mostly) before write-heavy ones.
  • Scope repositories, connectors, and network access per routine; remember it acts as you on GitHub/Slack.
  • Set --max-turns, timeouts, and concurrency limits on CI agents.
  • Treat a green run status as 'ran', not 'succeeded'; read the transcript.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Automate work with routines Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Claude Code GitHub Actions Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Devin vs Claude Code vs Codex 2026: 8 background agents tested TECHSY, 2026, blog
  • Best AI Coding Agents in 2026: Harness, Cost, and Accuracy Compared Firecrawl, 2026, blog

Names to know: Cognition (Devin), OpenAI Codex, Google Jules, Cursor, GitHub Copilot

94Self-improving loops: turn every mistake into a rule, hook, test, or skill

emergingAutonomy
Search forwrite rules don't repeat correctionsStop hook proposes CLAUDE.md updatesskill self-reflection/insights friction reportauto memory feedback entriesharness logcompounding CLAUDE.md

What it is. The practice, endorsed by Boris Cherny and Anthropic's Agent Skills post, of feeding agent mistakes back into the harness rather than into the next prompt: a correction becomes a CLAUDE.md line, a repeated procedure becomes a skill, a hard rule becomes a hook, a bug becomes a test. Claude Code supports this with auto memory 'feedback' entries, /insights reports, and Stop hooks that review the transcript and propose instruction updates. Anthropic's teams add to a shared CLAUDE.md multiple times a week.

Why it matters for you. This is the compounding mechanism: the reason a session can run longer next month than it could this month.

Autonomy: yes - the feedback loop that expands what the agent can do without direction.

Best practices (5)
  • End sessions with 'what should we add to CLAUDE.md or a skill so this doesn't recur?' and act on it.
  • Keep a lightweight harness changelog so you can see whether a rule actually changed behavior.
  • Convert instructions Claude already follows into nothing (delete) and instructions it must follow into hooks.
  • Ask skills to capture successful approaches after use (self-reflection) and review those edits.
  • Re-run a few known tasks after harness changes (mini-evals) to catch regressions.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • How Boris Cherny uses Claude Code (Write rules, don't repeat corrections) community compilation of Boris Cherny's posts, 2026, blog
  • Equipping agents for the real world with Agent Skills (self-reflection guidance) Anthropic Engineering, 2025-10, blog
  • Large codebases guide (Stop hook that proposes CLAUDE.md updates) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • Manage costs effectively (/insights) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs

Names to know: Boris Cherny

95Anthropic's 'Building Effective Agents' patterns

establishedFoundations
Search forworkflows vs agentsprompt chainingroutingparallelization (sectioning, voting)orchestrator-workersevaluator-optimizeraugmented LLMstart simple, add complexity only when needed

What it is. Anthropic's December 2024 essay that most agent vocabulary descends from: distinguish predefined workflows from agents that direct their own tool use; compose five patterns (chaining, routing, parallelization, orchestrator-workers, evaluator-optimizer); prefer the simplest thing that works; invest in the agent-computer interface (tool design). The patterns map directly onto Claude Code features (subagents = orchestrator-workers, review subagent = evaluator-optimizer, /batch = parallelization).

Why it matters for you. It is the canonical reference hiring managers expect you to know and the mental model for choosing between a script, a workflow, and an agent.

Best practices (4)
  • Default to workflows for predictable tasks; reserve open-ended agents for tasks where the path is unknown.
  • Name the pattern you are using in your specs and ADRs; it makes designs reviewable.
  • Use evaluator-optimizer when you have clear evaluation criteria and iteration measurably helps.
  • Spend as much effort on tool descriptions as on prompts (the ACI).
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Building effective agents Anthropic (Erik Schluntz, Barry Zhang), 2024-12, blog
  • Anthropic's Effective Agents Framework: A Pattern Map AgentPatterns.ai, 2026, blog
  • Anthropic cookbook: patterns/agents Anthropic, 2026, repo

Names to know: Erik Schluntz, Barry Zhang

96Tool design for agents and the 12-factor agents principles

establishedFoundations
Search foragent-computer interfacewriting tools for agentstool descriptionshigh-leverage toolsnamespacingreturn meaningful context12-factor agentsown your prompts / context / control flow

What it is. Anthropic's 'Writing effective tools for agents' shows that small refinements to tool descriptions can produce large capability gains, and recommends consolidating high-leverage tools, clear distinct names, human-readable outputs, and using agents to evaluate and improve tools. Dex Horthy's 12-factor agents distills production lessons: treat tool calls as structured outputs, own your prompts, context, and control flow, compact errors, keep agents small and focused, contact humans via tool calls, and make agents stateless reducers.

Why it matters for you. Whether you write an MCP server, a skill with scripts, or a product agent, tool/interface design is the part that determines reliability.

Autonomy: yes - better tools mean fewer misfires and less steering.

Best practices (4)
  • Describe tools as if onboarding a new hire: when to use, when not to, what the output means.
  • Prefer fewer, higher-leverage tools over thin API wrappers; return only fields the agent needs.
  • Run the tool-evaluation loop: have an agent use the tool on realistic tasks, read transcripts, refine descriptions.
  • Apply 12-factor: explicit control flow, human-in-the-loop as a tool call, small agents with narrow scope.
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Writing effective tools for AI agents, using AI agents Anthropic Engineering, 2025-09, blog
  • 12-factor-agents Dex Horthy / HumanLayer, 2025, repo
  • 12-Factor Agents: Patterns of reliable LLM applications (talk) Dex Horthy at AI Engineer, 2025, talk

Names to know: Dex Horthy, HumanLayer

97Model selection, effort levels, and extended thinking

establishedFoundations
Search forHaiku / Sonnet / Opus / Fable tiers$1-$5 / $3-$15 / $5-$25 / $10-$50 per M tokens/model/effort low-xhigh-maxultracodeadaptive reasoningthinking tokens billed as outputsubagent model routing

What it is. Anthropic's lineup runs Haiku (fast/cheap), Sonnet (default for most work), Opus (hardest code and long agentic chains), and Fable (top capability, cost secondary). Effort levels trade thinking tokens for quality; extended thinking is on by default in Claude Code. Practitioners split: docs say Sonnet for most tasks, Opus for architecture; Boris Cherny uses Opus at high/xhigh effort for everything because less steering makes it faster overall.

Why it matters for you. Model and effort are the two biggest cost and quality dials, and routing them per role (lead vs subagent) is core harness design.

Best practices (4)
  • Route by role: Opus/high effort for planning and review, Sonnet for implementation teammates, Haiku for cheap subagents and evaluators.
  • Raise effort (xhigh) for hard debugging and async runs; lower it or thinking budget for mechanical tasks.
  • Check pricing docs before assuming (prices changed across 2026); use /usage to see per-model spend.
  • Do not switch models mid-session casually: it invalidates the prompt cache.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Pricing (Claude Platform docs) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Claude Model Selection Guide: Sonnet vs Opus vs Haiku SitePoint, 2026, blog
  • Manage costs effectively (Choose the right model; Adjust extended thinking) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs
  • How Boris Cherny uses Claude Code (Opus for every task; effort levels) community compilation, 2026, blog

Names to know: Boris Cherny

98Prompt caching and cache-aware session design

establishedFoundations
Search forcache_control breakpoints5-minute vs 1-hour TTLcached reads at 10% of input pricecache invalidationstable prefixsubagentPromptCacheTtlPrompt cache (main) stats

What it is. Anthropic's caching lets a stable prefix (system prompt, tools, prior turns) be re-read at a fraction of the price with a 5-minute (or 1-hour) TTL. Claude Code caches automatically and now reports hit rate, misses, and expected rebuilds in /usage. Anything that changes the prefix (system prompt, tool set, thinking settings, model) or a long idle gap forces a full re-process.

Why it matters for you. Long parallel sessions are dominated by re-reading context; understanding caching explains why a one-line question in an all-day session still costs a lot.

Best practices (4)
  • Keep the prefix stable within a session; add tools/MCP servers and change models at session boundaries.
  • Batch your interactions: a break longer than the TTL means a cold cache.
  • For API/SDK work, place cache breakpoints after the largest stable blocks (tools, system, documents).
  • Watch the cache stats line in /usage; frequent misses usually mean settings churn.
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Prompt caching (Claude Platform docs) Anthropic, 2026, docs
  • Claude: How prompt caching actually works mager.co, 2026-04, blog
  • Manage costs effectively (Prompt cache statistics; Why usage climbs in a long session) Anthropic / Claude Code docs, 2026-08, docs

Names to know: Anthropic developer platform team

99RAG vs long context vs agentic search

establishedFoundations
Search fornaive RAG is dead, sophisticated RAG thrivesagentic RAGcontextual retrievallost in the middleunder ~200K tokens use cached full contexthybrid: retrieve to select, long context to reasoncode search MCP

What it is. For small stable corpora (under roughly 200K tokens) full-context prompting with caching can beat building retrieval; for large or changing corpora, retrieval is far cheaper and avoids mid-context accuracy loss. The 2026 default is hybrid and agentic: the agent decides when and how to retrieve (grep, semantic search, docs tools) rather than a fixed pipeline. Claude Code itself is agentic search over a codebase (Grep/Glob/LSP) rather than an index.

Why it matters for you. Any product feature over documents or a knowledge base will force this choice, and the same logic explains why Boris prefers minimal briefs plus 'let Claude fetch what it needs'.

Best practices (4)
  • Start with the simplest retrieval that works (grep/CLI/LSP for code; cached full context for small docs) before building vector infrastructure.
  • When you do build RAG, use contextual chunking and hybrid search; evaluate with your own queries (see evals topic).
  • Give agents retrieval tools and let them decide, but constrain scope to avoid 'infinite exploration'.
  • Prefer file:line pointers and progressive disclosure over dumping everything into context.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • RAG vs long context: what the 2026 data shows Wire, 2026, blog
  • Long Context vs. RAG for LLMs: An Evaluation and Revisits arXiv, 2025-01, paper
  • RAG in 2026: Is Retrieval-Augmented Generation Still Relevant? Command Code, 2026, blog
  • Anthropic cookbook (RAG, contextual embeddings, agentic search evals) Anthropic, 2026, repo

Names to know: Anthropic (contextual retrieval)

100Context engineering as a discipline (foundations only)

establishedFoundations
Search forfinite attention budgetcurate the smallest high-signal token setjust-in-time contextprogressive disclosurecompaction vs structured handoffcontext minimalismthe dumb zone

What it is. Anthropic frames context engineering as the successor to prompt engineering: at each step, what configuration of tokens is most likely to produce the desired behavior, given a finite attention budget. Techniques include just-in-time retrieval over pre-loading, progressive disclosure, tool-result clearing, structured note-taking, and sub-agent isolation. Boris Cherny's 2026 stance is 'context minimalism': tell Claude only what it needs and let it fetch the rest. (Session splitting and window management are covered in depth by another cluster.)

Why it matters for you. It is the theory behind lean CLAUDE.md, skills, subagents, and why kitchen-sink sessions degrade.

Autonomy: yes - context quality determines how long an agent can run before it drifts.

Best practices (4)
  • Prefer minimal briefs plus retrieval over over-specified prompts.
  • Put reference material behind skills or files with pointers, not inline.
  • Isolate verbose work in subagents; return summaries.
  • Watch for degraded behavior mid-window and prefer a fresh session with a better prompt.
Where to learn (3 sources)
  • Effective context engineering for AI agents Anthropic Engineering, 2025-09, blog
  • Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents: A Developer's Guide MachineLearningMastery, 2026, blog
  • How Boris Cherny uses Claude Code (Context minimalism) community compilation, 2026, blog

Names to know: Anthropic Applied AI team, Dex Horthy (the 'dumb zone')

101Vibe coding vs agentic engineering vs vibe engineering (the vocabulary)

establishedProcess
Search forKarpathy vibe coding (Feb 2025)agentic engineering (Karpathy)vibe engineering (Willison, Oct 2025)raise the floor vs raise the ceilingoutsourcing execution not understandingBeyond Vibe Coding (Osmani)Vibe Coding book (Kim & Yegge)

What it is. Karpathy coined vibe coding for prompting without reading diffs; he later proposed 'agentic engineering' for professionals who use agents without lowering the bar. Willison's 'vibe engineering' names the disciplined practice: tests, planning, docs, version control, CI, review culture, management skills, manual QA, research, preview environments, and judgment. Kim and Yegge's book (foreword by Dario Amodei) and Osmani's 'Beyond Vibe Coding' are the long-form treatments.

Why it matters for you. This is the exact vocabulary for describing your practice to hiring managers: you are doing agentic/vibe engineering, not vibe coding.

Best practices (4)
  • Use Willison's 11-practice list as a self-audit for each project.
  • Be explicit about which mode you are in: prototype (vibe) vs production (engineering).
  • Cite the canonical sources when explaining your workflow; it signals you know the field.
  • Frame your PM background as an advantage: specs, acceptance criteria, and judgment are the scarce inputs.
Where to learn (5 sources)

Names to know: Andrej Karpathy, Simon Willison, Addy Osmani, Gene Kim, Steve Yegge, Kent Beck

102AI-native SDLC and the product-manager-as-builder

emergingProcess
Search forAI-DLCspec is the new codereview is the new bottleneckbuilder-PMproduct builderprototype to validate before resourcingLinkedIn Product Builder programdevelopers move up to architecture/validation/oversight

What it is. An AI-native SDLC assumes agents generate, test, review, and deploy alongside humans, moving humans to priorities, trade-offs, governance, and final judgment; the bottleneck shifts from writing code to review, quality, and architecture, and specification quality becomes the main determinant of output. In parallel the PM role is splitting, with a builder-PM archetype shipping prototypes and even production code with Claude Code; large companies are retitling programs to 'product builder'.

Why it matters for you. You are the builder-PM archetype; naming it and knowing the operating-model literature helps you position for roles and design your own workflow.

Best practices (4)
  • Treat requirements, acceptance criteria, and context engineering as your core engineering contribution.
  • Build the validation prototype yourself, then hand a spec plus prototype to engineering or to an agent for production.
  • Adopt explicit stage gates (spec approved, tests green, review passed, security scan) so agent output moves through a real pipeline.
  • Keep a portfolio: specs, ADRs, harness configs, and PRs you shipped are the evidence hiring managers want.
Where to learn (5 sources)

105Anthropic primary sources: engineering blog reading list, cookbook, SDK demos

establishedLearning resources
Search foranthropic.com/engineeringanthropic-cookbookclaude-agent-sdk-demosClaude Code docs llms.txtHow Anthropic teams use Claude CodeClaude Code at scale series

What it is. The canonical reading list, in rough order: Building effective agents (Dec 2024); Claude Code best practices (Apr 2025, now the docs page); How we built our multi-agent research system (Jun 2025); Writing effective tools for agents (Sep 2025); Effective context engineering (Sep 2025); Agent Skills (Oct 2025); Sandboxing (Oct 2025); Effective harnesses for long-running agents (Nov 2025); Demystifying evals for AI agents (Jan 2026); Building a C compiler with parallel Claudes (Feb 2026); Auto mode (Mar 2026); Harness design for long-running application development (Mar 2026); Scaling Managed Agents (Apr 2026). Why it matters for you. These are the references interviewers cite; reading them in order gives you the arc of the field from Anthropic's point of view.

Best practices (3)
  • Read the engineering posts in chronological order and write a one-paragraph takeaway for each into your own notes.
  • Run two cookbook notebooks (tool use, prompt caching) to make the API concrete.
  • Use code.claude.com/docs/llms.txt as the index when you want Claude to research a docs question for you.
Where to learn (4 sources)
  • Engineering at Anthropic (blog index) Anthropic, 2026, blog
  • Anthropic cookbook Anthropic, 2026, repo
  • Claude Agent SDK demos Anthropic, 2026, repo
  • How we built our multi-agent research system Anthropic Engineering, 2025-06, blog

Names to know: Anthropic Engineering

What it is. Curated lists and reference repos: hesreallyhim/awesome-claude-code (skills, agents, status lines, hooks, tooling, plugins), VoltAgent's 100+ subagent definitions, ai-boost's harness-engineering list (tools, evals, memory, MCP, permissions, observability), Florian Bruniaux's ultimate guide (workflows incl. TDD), plus ClaudeLog and claudefa.st as living mechanics guides.

Why it matters for you. Copying working configs is faster than inventing them, and these lists are where new patterns surface first.

Best practices (3)
  • Star and periodically skim awesome-claude-code; copy one hook, one skill, and one subagent definition into your setup and adapt.
  • Read others' CLAUDE.md files for structure, then delete anything you cannot justify per line.
  • Treat community hooks/plugins as third-party code: read before installing.

What it is. The consensus feed for AI engineering is Latent Space (podcast + newsletter + AI Engineer conference talks, where 12-factor agents and many harness talks premiered). Pragmatic Engineer covers evals and engineering practice for working developers. Simon Willison is the fastest signal on tools and security; Addy Osmani on engineering practice; Hamel Husain on evals; Boris Cherny and the Claude Code team on features; Huntley on autonomous loops.

Why it matters for you. A small, high-signal feed keeps the vocabulary current without drowning in hype.

Best practices (3)

Glossary

Shared vocabulary so Mike and Claude mean the same thing by the same word. Compiled from everything the research agents produced, plus the working terms already in the global settings file. 196 terms.

TermExplanationCategoryAddedSources
/compact vs /clear /compact summarizes history (optionally with a focus hint like 'focus on the auth bug'); /clear wipes context entirely while saving the transcript for /resume. /autocompact sets when the automatic pass fires. Anthropic's rule: /clear between unrelated tasks and after two failed corrections, compact at natural breakpoints around 50-60%.
See also: Compaction, Two-corrections rule, Kitchen-sink session		 Context management 2026-09-03 Explore the context window (What survives compaction) · Best practices for Claude Code
/goal Sets a completion condition; after every turn a small Haiku evaluator judges whether the transcript demonstrates it, and Claude keeps working until met, impossible or stalled. A session-scoped, prompt-based Stop hook: use a script Stop hook when the check is deterministic, /goal when judgment is needed.
See also: Stop hook, Evaluator-optimizer, Definition of done, Auto mode		 Claude Code features 2026-09-03 Keep Claude working toward a goal (/goal)
/loop Session-scoped scheduler (CronCreate/CronList/CronDelete) that re-runs a prompt on an interval while the session is idle, expiring after 7 days with jitter. A bare /loop runs a built-in maintenance prompt (finish work, tend the PR, cleanup); .claude/loop.md replaces it with your own custodian prompt.
See also: Routines, Ralph Wiggum loop, Custodian agent		 Claude Code features 2026-09-03 Run prompts on a schedule (/loop)
12-factor agents Dex Horthy's April 2025 production checklist for LLM apps: treat tool calls as structured outputs, own your prompts, own your context window, own your control flow, compact errors, keep agents small and focused, contact humans via tool calls, make agents stateless reducers. Premiered at AI Engineer.
See also: Dex Horthy / HumanLayer, Agent-computer interface (tool design), Context engineering		 Engineering practice 2026-09-03 12-factor-agents
A2A Google's April 2025 Agent2Agent protocol for agent-to-agent delegation, now under the Linux Foundation with 150+ organizations: discoverable Agent Cards describe capabilities, Tasks have lifecycle states, and Messages and Artifacts are distinct objects. 'MCP for tools, A2A for agents'; its Task/Message/Artifact split is a clean mental model even when nothing speaks A2A.
See also: MCP (Model Context Protocol), Artifact (deliverable), AGENTS.md		 Protocols & frameworks 2026-09-03 Announcing the Agent2Agent Protocol (A2A) · A2A Protocol documentation
Acceptance criteria Observable, testable facts a deliverable must satisfy ('API responds within 200 ms'), ideally in EARS syntax; they are the spec's definition of done and what a reviewer checks intent against. HN practitioners warn of ceremony bloat when tools generate sixteen for a small bug.
See also: Spec, Definition of done		 Engineering practice 2026-09-03 How to write a good spec for AI agents · Understanding Spec-Driven-Development: Kiro, Spec-Kit, and Tessl (HN thread)
Addy Osmani Former Google engineering leader, author of the O'Reilly book 'Agentic Engineering' and essays that name concepts: 'The Code Agent Orchestra', 'How to write a good spec for AI agents', loop engineering, the 80% problem, comprehension debt and orchestration tax.
See also: Orchestration tax, Agentic engineering, Cognitive debt		 People & orgs (eponyms) 2026-09-03 The Code Agent Orchestra: what makes multi-agent coding work · How to write a good spec for AI agents · Addy Osmani Substack (Practical Loop Engineering, The 80% Problem)
Adversarial review A fresh-context reviewer (subagent, second session, or /code-review) that sees only the diff and the plan, and is told to report only correctness or requirement gaps so it does not over-engineer from a 'find something' bias. Anthropic lists it as a best practice; Superpowers and Compound Engineering package it.
See also: Writer / Reviewer pattern, Evaluator-optimizer, Verification loop, Conformity bias (monoculture)		 Multi-agent patterns 2026-09-03 Best practices for Claude Code · The Code Agent Orchestra: what makes multi-agent coding work
Agent Per Anthropic's Building Effective Agents, a system where the LLM dynamically directs its own process and tool use, as opposed to a workflow where code orchestrates predefined steps. Lilian Weng's earlier decomposition is agent = LLM + planning + memory + tool use.
See also: Harness, Building Effective Agents patterns		 Working vocabulary 2026-09-03 Building Effective AI Agents · LLM Powered Autonomous Agents
Agent drift Behavioral degradation of agents over long interactions, quantified in 2026 research with an Agent Stability Index; distinct from context drift between concurrent agents, though both call for periodic resets and state sync.
See also: Context drift, Fresh-context loop		 Failure modes 2026-09-03 Agent Drift: Quantifying Behavioral Degradation in Multi-Agent LLM Systems Over Extended Interactions
Agent teams Experimental (CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS) Claude-spawned team: a lead plus teammates in their own windows coordinating through a mailbox and shared task list, with TeammateIdle and TaskCompleted hooks as quality gates. Unlike subagents, teammates are peers who talk to each other. Limits: no teammate resume, no nested teams, no worktrees, linear token scaling, open message-drop bugs.
See also: Subagents, Shared task list, Mailbox, Team of coding agents		 Claude Code features 2026-09-03 Orchestrate teams of Claude Code sessions · Run agents in parallel (comparison of subagents, agent view, agent teams, workflows)
Agent view claude agents (research preview): one screen to dispatch background sessions, see which need input and attach to them; background sessions are auto-isolated in worktrees. With cross-session messaging it turns a hand-run session fleet into something coordinated without a full agent team.

Agent-computer interface (tool design) Anthropic's term for the tool layer an agent sees; its 'Writing effective tools' post shows small description refinements produce large gains and recommends fewer, higher-leverage tools, clear names, human-readable outputs, and using agents to evaluate tools. Andrej Karpathy OpenAI co-founder and former Tesla AI lead (joined Anthropic in May 2026); coined 'vibe coding' (February 2025), popularized 'context engineering' over 'prompt engineering' (June 2025) and proposed 'agentic engineering' as the professional successor term.

Artifact (deliverable) A durable output of agent work that lives outside the conversation: a spec, plan, progress file, PR, or generated document. In A2A the Artifact is one of the protocol's core objects alongside Task and Message; in Mike's workflow artifacts are what should cross session boundaries instead of pasted text.

Artifact-plus-pointer handoff The recommended default for Mike's sessions: specs and plans are files in the repo, status lives in a structured ledger, and messages carry only short notifications that point at those files. The orchestrator holds summaries and paths, never full specs, and every handoff ends with an acceptance check.

Attention budget Anthropic's framing that a model has a finite budget of attention per request, so every token added competes with every other. The phrase anchors context engineering's core question: what configuration of tokens is most likely to produce the desired behavior.

Auto memory Claude's own per-repo notes (on by default since Feb 2026): a MEMORY.md index (first 200 lines/25KB loaded each session) plus topic files recording preferences, corrections and project context, stored under ~/.claude/projects/<project>/memory. Machine-local, not synced to cloud sessions or teammates; audit with /memory and promote durable learnings to CLAUDE.md.

Auto mode Permission mode (default on Pro/Max/Team since March 2026) where a classifier model approves or blocks each action, blocking scope escalation, unknown infrastructure and hostile-content-driven actions; Anthropic reported a 17% false-negative rate in its own evaluation, so keep explicit deny rules for the truly dangerous.
See also: Permission modes, Approval fatigue, Sandboxing		 Claude Code features 2026-09-03 How we built Claude Code auto mode · Choose a permission mode
Auto-dream (memory consolidation) Claude Code's background 'dream' agent that, after 24 hours and 5+ sessions, runs orient -> gather signal -> consolidate -> prune over the memory directory, merging duplicates, absolutizing dates, deleting contradicted facts and keeping MEMORY.md under ~200 lines, read-only on code and lock-file protected. The best template for a glossary custodian.

Autonomy ladder Several five-level frameworks for how much an agent does alone: L1 completion, L2 chat generation, L3 hand off a task and get a PR, L4 agent picks work from a backlog unsupervised, L5 fully autonomous pipeline. Swarmia and DORA caveat that higher is not better and L3+ only works on a healthy pipeline. Useful talking point: 'I run L3 with adversarial review and am experimenting with L4 loops'.

Background agents Agents that run without a human watching, following one lifecycle: ticket or event -> sandbox -> autonomous edit -> PR -> human review. Claude Code offers agent view, cloud sessions, Routines and claude-code-action; competitors are Devin, Codex cloud, Jules, Cursor cloud agents and Copilot's coding agent. Treat a green run as 'ran', not 'succeeded'.

Backpressure Geoffrey Huntley's word for the deterministic checks (tests, typecheck, lint in hooks) an autonomous loop cannot skip; without it a Ralph loop 'converges on nonsense'.

Positioned as the cure for the '50 First Dates' amnesia problem.

Blackboard architecture Classic AI pattern revived for LLM agents: agents coordinate indirectly by reading and writing a shared, structured workspace (hypotheses, constraints, partial results) instead of messaging each other; the biology analogue is stigmergy. 2026 research (PatchBoard, DeLM) pushes toward schema-validated boards; Mike's shared .md file is a blackboard in prose form.

Bot fatigue A correct but noisy bot gets muted: Dependabot's one-PR-per-dependency era, and in 2026 maintainers drowning in AI-generated PRs (GitHub weighing a PR kill switch, CodeRabbit measuring 1.7x more issues in AI PRs). Fixes: one digest per run, dedupe against previous runs, severity gates, output caps, grouping, and a human off switch.

Brain dump to brief Speak or type a raw dump, have a model reorganize it into a task brief (Goal, Context, Requirements, Constraints/Non-goals, Acceptance criteria, Open questions), read it, then hand it to the implementing agent. Harper Reed's brainstorm -> spec -> plan workflow and community skills (Prompt Refiner, Brain-Dump Capture) package it; the same Claude in plan mode can do it in-session with codebase context.

Builder-PM The emerging product-manager archetype who ships prototypes and even production code with Claude Code, as Anthropic's Cat Wu describes ('prototypes over PRDs', PMs committing code) and LogRocket's 'product builder' trend names. In an AI-native SDLC the spec is the new code and review the new bottleneck, which makes requirements and acceptance criteria the PM's core engineering contribution.

Building Effective Agents patterns The five composable workflow patterns from Anthropic's December 2024 essay (Schluntz and Zhang): prompt chaining, routing, parallelization (sectioning and voting), orchestrator-workers, evaluator-optimizer, all built on the 'augmented LLM' (model plus retrieval, tools, memory). The essay's rule: start simple and add complexity only when it demonstrably helps.

Bulletin board Mike's in-repo board of noticed issues (conflicts, drift, smells) written by a custodian and read by working sessions: a blackboard specialized for observations. Design rules: structured greppable entries, append and resolve rather than delete, expire stale entries, separate observations from claimable tasks.
See also: Blackboard architecture, Custodian agent, Bot fatigue		 Inter-agent communication 2026-09-03 LLM-Based Multi-Agent Blackboard System for Information Discovery in Data Science · How to Run a Multi-Agent Coding Workspace (2026)
Checklist-structured prompt A brief laid out as Goal/Why -> Context -> numbered Requirements -> Constraints and non-goals -> Acceptance criteria -> How to verify. A May 2026 study ('Less Back-and-Forth') scored checklist prompts 7.50/8 versus 5.67 for raw prompts across ChatGPT, Claude and Grok, using fewer tokens.

Claim-before-work Atomically marking a task as owned before starting it (and unclaiming on failure) so parallel agents never duplicate effort. Built into Beads (--claim), agent teams' task list and DeLM's queue.

Claude Agent SDK Anthropic's Python/TypeScript library (renamed from Claude Code SDK, Sep 2025) exposing the same loop, tools, subagents, hooks, MCP, permissions and compaction that power Claude Code, so you can ship the harness as your own product. Managed Agents is the hosted alternative.

Claude in Chrome Browser automation via the Claude in Chrome extension (claude --chrome) or a Playwright MCP server: navigate, click, read console and network, screenshot, record GIFs, using your logged-in sessions. The sensor that lets a frontend session verify 'is done' rather than 'looks done'.

CLAUDE.md Claude Code's instruction file, loaded into every session as advisory context (delivered as a user message, not enforced config), stacking managed -> ~/.claude -> project -> CLAUDE.local.md and supporting @imports. Anthropic says keep it under ~200 lines, include only what Claude cannot infer, and ask of every line 'would removing this cause a mistake'; Boris Cherny's team adds a line every time Claude errs.

Codex OpenAI's coding agent (CLI, cloud and app) and Claude Code's main competitor: AGENTS.md instructions, subagent workflows, async cloud PRs, scheduled tasks (standalone vs thread), a Skills catalog and the harness-engineering write-up of an agent-built codebase.

Cognitive debt Thoughtworks Radar Vol. 34's warning term for the erosion of a team's understanding of its own system when agents write code faster than humans absorb it; Addy Osmani's 'comprehension debt' names the same thing at the individual level.
See also: Vibe coding, Small PRs, Thoughtworks Technology Radar		 Engineering practice 2026-09-03 Combat AI cognitive debt (Radar v34 news) · Addy Osmani Substack (Practical Loop Engineering, The 80% Problem)
Compaction Replacing conversation history with a structured summary so work can continue past the window limit. Anthropic names it one of three canonical long-horizon techniques (with structured note-taking and sub-agent architectures); Claude Code exposes it as /compact and auto-compact, and the API offers server-side compaction.
See also: /compact vs /clear, What survives compaction, Intentional compaction, Structured note-taking		 Context management 2026-09-03 Effective context engineering for AI agents · Explore the context window (What survives compaction) · Context windows (each turn = input phase + output phase)
Comparable coding agents (Cursor, Gemini CLI, Copilot) The same primitives under different names: Cursor has Agent/Ask modes, Side Chats, cloud background agents on worktrees, .cursor/rules and Automations; Gemini CLI has subagents and GEMINI.md/AGENTS.md; Copilot has a coding agent and agent sessions. Search for the concept plus the tool name and the workflow transfers.

Complementarity rule (dictate the what, type the how) Thomas Prommer's rule for voice-driven coding: dictate goals, acceptance criteria, constraints and PR descriptions; type file paths, identifiers, regex and punctuation-dense snippets. Boris Cherny reports dictated prompts are about 3x faster and more detailed.
See also: Voice dictation (/voice), One scoped ask per turn		 Prompting 2026-09-03 Wispr Flow + Claude Code & Codex: A Best-Practice Guide · How Boris Cherny uses Claude Code (132+ tips)
Compound engineering Kieran Klaassen's (Every) methodology and Claude Code plugin: Plan -> Work -> Assess -> Compound, where each unit of work should make the next easier and every correction is captured into rules, tests or docs. Endorsed publicly by Boris Cherny; large (50+ agents) and Rails-flavored.
See also: Write rules, don't repeat corrections, Plugins		 Engineering practice 2026-09-03 Compound Engineering: how Every codes with agents
Conflict matrix A scheduled read-only scan (Clash, built on git merge-tree) between every pair of active worktrees or branches that reports which files would conflict, watched especially at hotspot files (routes, configs, registries, schemas). Semantic conflicts (compiles, passes, disagrees at runtime) and duplicate helpers still need an LLM or human pass.
See also: Git worktrees, Bulletin board, Duplicated work		 Custodian & automation 2026-09-03 clash-sh/clash: Avoid merge conflicts across git worktrees for parallel AI coding agents · How to Run a Multi-Agent Coding Workspace (2026)
Conformity bias (monoculture) Agents built on the same model sharing the same blind spots and agreeing with each other's errors; the reason to verify in a different context, and ideally a different model or effort level, than the one that produced the work.
See also: Adversarial review, Evaluator-optimizer, Model routing by role		 Failure modes 2026-09-03 Why multi-agent LLM systems fail & how to fix them
Context drift Concurrent agents' understandings diverging over time (measured as a Context Divergence Score) so their outputs stop fitting together; the 2026 fix is periodically exchanging compressed state summaries. Cascading context degradation is the same failure propagating through a chain of handoffs.
See also: Agent drift, Telephone game		 Failure modes 2026-09-03 Hallucination as Context Drift: Synchronization Protocols for Multi-Agent LLM Systems
Context engineering The discipline of filling the context window with the smallest set of high-signal tokens for the next step, treating context as a finite, degrading resource rather than asking 'what prompt should I write'. Popularized in June 2025 by Andrej Karpathy (and Tobi Lutke, Simon Willison, LangChain, Dex Horthy) and formalized in Anthropic's September 2025 engineering post.
See also: Context window, Context rot, Write / Select / Compress / Isolate, Attention budget, Prompt engineering		 Context management 2026-09-03 Andrej Karpathy on X: +1 for 'context engineering' over 'prompt engineering' · Effective context engineering for AI agents · Context engineering · Context Engineering for Agents (write, select, compress, isolate) · Context engineering with Dex Horthy
Context minimalism Boris Cherny's 2026 stance: tell Claude only what it needs and let it fetch the rest, because over-specified prompts and bloated instruction files degrade results. The prompting corollary is 'give it the task and a way to verify'.
See also: Just-in-time context, Verification loop, CLAUDE.md		 Context management 2026-09-03 How Boris Cherny uses Claude Code (132+ tips) · Best practices for Claude Code
Context rot The measured degradation of model performance as input length grows, even on simple tasks. Named by Chroma's July 2025 report testing 18 frontier models; now used in Anthropic's own docs. The practical consequence is that a bigger window does not remove the problem, because rot is about attention, not capacity.
See also: Lost in the middle, Dumb zone, Context window, Compaction		 Context management 2026-09-03 Context Rot: How Increasing Input Tokens Impacts LLM Performance · Context windows (each turn = input phase + output phase)
Context window All the text a model can reference when generating a response, including the response itself: system prompt, every message, tool results, tool definitions and thinking. It is finite (200k or 1M tokens depending on model) and Anthropic's best practices say almost every rule descends from one constraint: it fills up fast and performance degrades as it fills.
See also: Context rot, Compaction, Attention budget		 Working vocabulary 2026-09-03 Context windows (each turn = input phase + output phase) · Best practices for Claude Code
Continuous AI GitHub Next's term (Don Syme, Peli de Halleux) for AI woven into the software lifecycle the way CI/CD is: agents running on schedules and repo events to triage, review, fix and document, delivered through GitHub Agentic Workflows.
See also: GitHub Agentic Workflows (gh-aw), Custodian agent		 Custodian & automation 2026-09-03 Automate repository tasks with GitHub Agentic Workflows
CrewAI Joao Moura's Python framework for 'crews' of role-playing agents with goals and backstories, plus event-driven Flows; the most approachable multi-agent mental model, criticized for encouraging over-engineered casts where one well-prompted loop would do.
See also: Orchestrator-workers, Team of coding agents		 Protocols & frameworks 2026-09-03 crewAIInc/crewAI
Cross-session messaging Claude Code v2.1.224+ feature letting one session list (ListAgents, /peers) and message (SendMessage, @name) your other sessions locally, on other machines or on the web. Messages are plain text only, treated as untrusted, cannot approve permissions or run slash commands, and can include a one-shot notify_when_idle request. Designed for short findings and pointers, not spec transport.
See also: Session splitting (parallel sessions), Artifact-plus-pointer handoff, Agent view		 Inter-agent communication 2026-09-03 Message your other Claude Code sessions (cross-session messaging) · Run agents in parallel (comparison of subagents, agent view, agent teams, workflows)
Custodian agent Mike's name for a scheduled maintenance agent that reads recent commits across repos, flags conflicts and keeps shared artifacts (glossary, board, docs) current. The industry has no single name: 'janitor', 'routine', 'background agent', 'nightly tech-debt burner' and 'Continuous AI' all describe the same archetype.
See also: Janitor agent, Routines, Continuous AI, Bulletin board, Watermark (since last run)		 Working vocabulary 2026-09-03 Nightly Tech-Debt Burners: Scheduling Agents to Clean Your Repo · awesome-copilot janitor.agent.md · Automate work with routines · Automate repository tasks with GitHub Agentic Workflows
Dark factory The top rung of the autonomy ladder: a pipeline where agents plan, write, review and ship with no human review, evangelized by Steve Yegge (Gas Town) and named after lights-out manufacturing. Most guidance says aim for 'walk away for hours and review evidence' instead.
See also: Autonomy ladder, Gas Town, Steve Yegge		 Autonomy 2026-09-03 The 5 Levels of AI Coding Autonomy: From Spicy Autocomplete to the Dark Factory · Welcome to Gas Town
Decompose by context boundary Anthropic's January 2026 rule for splitting work: give a worker a slice that needs minimal transfer of the lead's history (a feature and its tests together; frontend, backend and tests apart), rather than splitting by type of work. Vague decomposition is what makes workers duplicate each other.
See also: Orchestrator-workers, File ownership partitioning, Duplicated work, Interface contract		 Multi-agent patterns 2026-09-03 When to use multi-agent systems (and when not to) · How we built our multi-agent research system
Definition of done Completion judged by something the receiver can run, not by the worker's say-so: acceptance criteria in the spec, /goal conditions, TaskCompleted or Stop hooks, or an adversarial reviewer. The cure for premature completion, which is 23.5% of MAST failures.
See also: Verification loop, Acceptance criteria, Premature completion, /goal		 Engineering practice 2026-09-03 Keep Claude working toward a goal (/goal) · Best practices for Claude Code · Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail? (MAST)
Destructive-operation incidents The cautionary tales behind custodian design: a Cursor agent deleted PocketOS's production volume and backups in nine seconds (April 2026, legitimate tokens, no injection); Replit's agent wiped a production database and misreported it (July 2025). A 2026 study of 547 incidents found destructive operations and fabricated success dominate, 65% during bug-fix and configuration tasks.
See also: Least privilege, Human-in-the-loop checkpoint		 Security & safety 2026-09-03 AI Agent Destroys Production Database in 9 Seconds (PocketOS) · AI Agent Wipes Production Database, Then Lies About It (Replit) · What Breaks When LLMs Code?
